# Writing Lesson Plans — Full Year (Terms 1–4)

_Generated from the Writing With Us app data files (term1–4-data.js). Each week runs Monday–Thursday, following an I Do / We Do / You Do structure._

## Table of Contents
- [Term 1](#term-1)
- [Term 2](#term-2)

## Term 1

### Week 1

#### Monday: Tier 2 vocabulary: descriptive adjectives

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): words and phrases are chosen deliberately for effect, including Tier 2 vocabulary that is precise and cross-curricular. Students select words that shape meaning and impact for the reader.

**I Do — Watch Me Choose Better Words**

I'm going to show you how to swap a plain word for a much more interesting one. Watch how I upgrade the word "big" in this sentence.

> Example: The big dog ran across the field.

Instead of "big" I could say: enormous, muscular, towering, hulking. I pick "muscular" because it tells us something extra — not just size, but strength. Watch: "The muscular dog ran across the field." Much more vivid!

*Tip: Ask yourself: Does this word paint a picture? If not, upgrade it.*

**We Do — Upgrade Together**

Let's upgrade the plain adjectives in these sentences together. Shout out your ideas!
- The nice sunset filled the sky with colour.
- She wore a pretty dress to the concert.
- It was a bad storm that hit the town.

*Discussion prompt: What word could replace the underlined one? Why is your choice better?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence by replacing the plain adjective with a stronger, more descriptive one. Try to use a different word each time!
- The old bridge creaked in the wind.
- We climbed a high mountain on our trip.
- The puppy had a soft coat.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your own sentence using two vivid adjectives.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Word choice for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "The meaning of words can be extended or changed by their context… vocabulary is chosen deliberately for effect, so students consider the connotations and precision of words." Students at Years 5–6 are expected to select Tier 2 words (precise, cross-curricular vocabulary such as vivid adjectives) that sharpen meaning and create impact for the reader.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Selecting precise words: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases, including subject-specific vocabulary, that are fit for purpose and audience." This lesson builds the habit of upgrading vague adjectives to precise, purposeful ones — a core Phase 2 composing practice.


#### Tuesday: Nouns & proper nouns – identification & use

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure & Punctuation (Knowledge): sentences are made up of words that belong to different classes; proper nouns name specific people, places, and things and always begin with a capital letter.

**I Do — Spot the Noun**

A noun is a naming word — it names a person, place, thing, or idea. A proper noun is the specific name of something and always starts with a capital letter. Watch me find and sort them.

> Example: The girl visited Wellington with her family.

"girl" = common noun (a person). "Wellington" = proper noun (specific place — capital W). "family" = common noun (a group). Proper nouns need a capital because they are the actual name.

*Tip: Ask: Is this the specific name of someone or somewhere? → proper noun → capital letter!*

**We Do — Sort & Capitalise**

Let's sort these words into common nouns and proper nouns together, and fix any missing capitals.
- my friend aisha lives in christchurch near the avon river.
- the teacher read a book called the lion, the witch and the wardrobe.
- on tuesday we visited the auckland museum with mrs chen.

*Discussion prompt: Which words need a capital letter? Which are just common nouns?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence correctly, adding capital letters for all proper nouns.
- last summer, my cousin jake visited queenstown with uncle dan.
- our school, parkside primary, is near the hutt river.
- i am reading a book by roald dahl called matilda.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two sentences — one with 2 common nouns, one with 2 proper nouns.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Word classes: nouns: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Words belong to different classes (e.g. nouns, verbs, adjectives, adverbs, pronouns, prepositions, conjunctions) and understanding word classes helps writers make deliberate choices." Students learn to identify common and proper nouns as a foundation for accurate and purposeful writing.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Capitalisation of proper nouns: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Punctuation signals meaning to readers, including… capital letters at the start of sentences and for proper nouns." Students at Years 5–6 are expected to apply capitalisation conventions accurately and consistently in their own writing.


#### Wednesday: Simple sentence structure: subject + verb + object

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): a simple sentence contains a subject and a verb; students construct grammatically complete sentences and use a variety of sentence structures for effect.

**I Do — Build a Simple Sentence**

Every sentence needs at least a subject (who/what) and a verb (action/state). We can also add an object (what receives the action). Watch me build one piece at a time.

> Example: The cat | chased | the mouse.

Subject = "The cat" (who is doing it). Verb = "chased" (the action). Object = "the mouse" (what it acted on). Put them together: complete thought, complete sentence!

*Tip: Check: Who is doing it? What are they doing? What are they doing it to?*

**We Do — Label & Build**

Let's label each part (S/V/O) in these sentences, then try building our own on the board.
- The goalkeeper saved the penalty.
- A huge wave swallowed the sandcastle.
- My little brother ate the last biscuit.

*Discussion prompt: Point to the subject, verb, and object. What would happen if we removed the verb?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Label each sentence with S (subject), V (verb), O (object). Then write three simple sentences of your own.
- The chef prepared a delicious meal.
- The explorer discovered a hidden cave.
- Lightning struck the tall tree.
- ✦ Bonus: Write an S+V+O sentence about something that happened at school this week.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Simple sentences: S + V + O: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "A simple sentence contains one main clause with a subject and a verb… writers use a variety of sentence structures, including simple, compound, and complex sentences, for effect and clarity." This lesson establishes the simple sentence as the core building block students then extend and combine across the year.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Constructing grammatically correct sentences: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of grammatically correct sentences" and "use knowledge of sentence structure to clarify meaning." Identifying and labelling subject, verb, and object builds the grammatical awareness needed to write with precision and variety.


#### Thursday: Proofreading: capitals & full stops

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation & Writing Process (Knowledge & Practice): sentences begin with a capital letter and end with a full stop, exclamation mark, or question mark; students plan and revise writing so that it is accurate and clear.

**I Do — Be the Editor**

Every sentence starts with a capital letter and ends with a full stop (or ! or ?). Watch me read slowly and fix the mistakes in this passage.

> Example: the sun rose slowly over the mountains. birds began to sing in the trees it was going to be a perfect day the children raced outside.

I read aloud and pause at every sentence end. "the sun" → capital T. After "mountains." ✓ already has one. After "trees" — no full stop! I add one. "it" → capital I. And so on. I'll mark every fix in red.

*Tip: Read out loud — you naturally pause at sentence ends. That pause = full stop.*

**We Do — Fix It Together**

Let's fix this passage together. One student comes up and marks each correction.
- maya loved visiting her grandma on fridays they would bake together grandma always made chocolate biscuits maya's favourite was the ones with icing on top they would eat them while watching the birds in the garden

*Discussion prompt: Where do sentences end? Which words need capitals? How many sentences are in this passage?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this passage correctly, adding all missing capitals and full stops.
- last weekend dad and i went fishing at the lake we caught three fish but we threw them back the water was cold and clear i could see stones on the bottom dad said it was the best fishing spot he knew
- ✦ Bonus: Count how many sentences you created. Write your own 3-sentence paragraph with perfect punctuation.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Capitals & full stops: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Punctuation signals meaning to readers, including capital letters at the start of sentences and for proper nouns, and end-of-sentence punctuation (full stop, exclamation mark, question mark)." These are the foundational conventions students must control accurately before building to more complex punctuation.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Revising for accuracy: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking for correct punctuation and capitalisation." This editing lesson directly builds that revision habit — reading aloud to catch missing punctuation and incorrect capitalisation.


### Week 2

#### Monday: Commas in lists – rules & practice

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): commas are used to separate items in a list; students use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect.

**I Do — Commas Keep Lists Tidy**

When we list three or more things in a sentence, we separate them with commas. The last item uses "and" (not a comma). Watch me build a list sentence step by step.

> Example: In my bag I have a pencil, a ruler, a water bottle and my lunchbox.

I list the items: pencil → comma → ruler → comma → water bottle → "and" → lunchbox. No comma before "and" in NZ/UK style. The commas stop the sentence becoming a jumbled mess!

*Tip: Read your list aloud. Every natural pause (except before "and") = a comma.*

**We Do — Add the Commas**

These list sentences are missing commas. Let's fix them together.
- The market sold fresh tomatoes plump strawberries creamy cheese and warm bread.
- Liam packed his boots his shin pads his water bottle his jersey and his mouthguard.
- The painting used shades of crimson burnt orange golden yellow and deep purple.

*Discussion prompt: Where do the pauses fall? Does "and" need a comma before it?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add commas to the list sentences, then write two of your own.
- At the beach we collected shells pebbles driftwood and sea glass.
- The recipe called for flour sugar butter eggs and a pinch of salt.
- Her favourite animals were dolphins elephants red pandas and otters.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a sentence listing 5 things you would bring on a survival adventure.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Commas in lists: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Commas are used to separate items in a list and to clarify meaning within sentences." Students at Years 5–6 are expected to know and apply comma conventions accurately, including the NZ/UK convention of no serial comma before "and" in a list.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying punctuation accurately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect, including commas." This lesson builds the habit of reading aloud to identify list pauses — a practical revision strategy that reinforces accurate punctuation in students' own writing.


#### Tuesday: Verbs: action vs. state verbs

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): words belong to different classes; verbs express actions, events, or states and are a core building block of every sentence.

**I Do — Two Kinds of Verbs**

Action verbs show physical or mental movement (run, think, build). State verbs describe a condition or state of being (is, seems, belongs, loves). Watch how I spot the difference.

> Example: The hawk soared above the valley. | The valley is very wide.

"soared" — can I picture the hawk doing this? Yes! Action verb. "is" — is the valley doing anything? Not really — it describes its state. State verb. State verbs often can't be continuous: you wouldn't say "the valley is being wide."

*Tip: Can you picture someone physically doing it? → Probably action. Does it describe a condition? → State.*

**We Do — Sort Them**

Let's sort these verbs and sentences into action or state, then discuss why.
- The students sprinted to the finish line.
- This soup smells incredible.
- She believes in always trying her best.
- The puppy chewed through the shoe.

*Discussion prompt: Action or state? Could you say "is ___-ing"? Would that sound strange?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Label each underlined verb as ACTION or STATE. Then write one sentence for each type.
- The river rushes over the boulders in winter.
- My grandmother owns a beautiful garden.
- He remembers every goal he ever scored.
- The children splashed through the puddles.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a sentence that has BOTH an action verb and a state verb.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Word classes: verbs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Words belong to different classes (e.g. nouns, verbs, adjectives, adverbs…) and understanding word classes helps writers make deliberate choices." Distinguishing action verbs from state verbs deepens students' understanding of how verbs function, enabling more precise and purposeful verb selection in their own writing.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Selecting precise verbs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience," including choosing verbs that convey exactly the right meaning. Knowing the difference between action and state verbs helps students avoid weak verb constructions and select words with greater precision.


#### Wednesday: Expanding simple sentences with adjectives

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): adjectives describe or modify nouns; students expand simple sentences by adding detail and description to communicate more precisely.

**I Do — Dress Up Your Sentences**

A simple sentence is fine, but adjectives add colour and detail. Watch how I take a bare sentence and expand it by adding adjectives before nouns.

> Example: Before: A dog barked at the stranger. → After: A scruffy, brown dog barked at the hooded stranger.

I ask: What kind of dog? → scruffy, brown. What kind of stranger? → hooded. I add them before the nouns. Notice I used a comma between two adjectives describing the same noun (scruffy, brown). But "hooded" is alone so no comma needed.

*Tip: Ask "what kind?" or "which one?" before each noun to find adjective spots.*

**We Do — Expand Together**

Let's expand these simple sentences by adding at least one adjective to each noun. Call out your ideas!
- The cat sat on the wall.
- A boat moved through the water.
- The girl opened the door.

*Discussion prompt: What adjectives could we add? Where exactly in the sentence do they go?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Expand each sentence by adding at least two adjectives. You can also add adjectives to the verb (adverbs) as a bonus challenge.
- The horse galloped across the field.
- A bird landed on the fence.
- The student opened the book.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your own expanded sentence about a place you know, using at least 3 adjectives.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Adjectives & noun modification: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Adjectives describe or modify nouns and can be used before or after the noun to add detail and precision." Students also learn that a comma is needed between two or more adjectives describing the same noun, connecting adjective use to punctuation knowledge.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Expanding sentences for detail: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "expand simple sentences by adding words, phrases, and clauses to add detail and description." This lesson builds that core skill of moving from a bare S+V+O sentence to a richer, more detailed construction — a key step in developing writing craft at Years 5–6.


#### Thursday: Editing: fixing run-on sentences

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students reread and revise writing to ensure sentences are correctly demarcated and ideas are clearly separated.

**I Do — Taming the Run-On**

A run-on sentence is when two or more complete sentences crash together without proper punctuation. Watch me find and fix one.

> Example: The rain hammered the roof the windows rattled and the lights flickered on and off everyone ran inside.

I read slowly. "The rain hammered the roof" — full thought. Full stop! "the windows rattled and the lights flickered on and off" — I could keep this as one sentence since "and" joins related ideas. Full stop! "everyone ran inside." — done. Result: Three clear sentences.

*Tip: Say each sentence aloud. If you run out of breath before a pause, it's probably too long.*

**We Do — Fix the Run-Ons**

Let's work through these run-on sentences together. Decide where to split them or what joining word to use.
- Sophia loved drawing she spent every afternoon filling sketchbooks with dragons and stars her mum often had to call her in for dinner.
- The match was tied with five minutes left the striker broke free she shot the ball it curved into the top corner the crowd erupted.

*Discussion prompt: Where do the natural pauses fall? Should we split with a full stop, or join with "and/but/so"?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each run-on as two or three clear sentences. Choose the best punctuation or joining word.
- My dog loves the park he chases every stick he also tries to swim in the fountain the ranger always laughs.
- The volcano had been quiet for years then one morning smoke rose from the crater by afternoon it was glowing red scientists rushed to the site.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 3 sentences on any topic — no run-ons allowed!

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Sentence demarcation: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Sentences are demarcated by capital letters and end punctuation; run-on sentences occur when two or more complete clauses are joined without correct punctuation or an appropriate conjunction." Understanding sentence boundaries is foundational to all writing accuracy at Phase 2.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Revising run-on sentences: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking for correct punctuation." This lesson trains students to slow down, read aloud, and fix the most common structural error in Years 5–6 writing — the run-on sentence.


### Week 3

#### Monday: Synonyms: choosing stronger words

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): the meaning of words can be extended or changed by context; students select synonyms deliberately to match the intensity and precision needed for their purpose and audience.

**I Do — Level Up Your Words**

Synonyms are words with similar meanings — but they're not identical. Choosing the right synonym can completely change the feel of your writing. Watch me use a synonym scale.

> Example: cold → chilly → freezing → glacial → arctic

All mean "cold" — but they get more intense. "The water was cold" is fine. "The water was glacial" paints a sharper picture. I match the word to the intensity of what I'm describing. A slightly cool breeze is "chilly." A swimming hole in winter is "glacial."

*Tip: Think: Is this word strong enough for what I'm describing, or do I need to turn up the dial?*

**We Do — Synonym Scales**

Let's build synonym scales for these words together, then choose the best one for each sentence.
- HAPPY → (let's find 4 synonyms, from mild to intense)
- MOVE → (find synonyms that show different kinds of movement)
- "She was happy when she won the prize." → Which synonym fits best?

*Discussion prompt: Why would you choose "elated" over "pleased" in some sentences but not others?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Replace the plain word in each sentence with a stronger synonym. Then create your own synonym scale for the word "said."
- The athlete was tired after the marathon.
- It was a good book — I finished it in one day.
- The crowd was loud when the team scored.
- ✦ Bonus: Build a synonym scale for "said" with at least 6 words. Which would you use in a mystery story? A speech? An argument?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Synonyms & word intensity: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "The meaning of words can be extended or changed by their context… vocabulary is chosen deliberately for effect, so students consider the connotations and precision of words." Understanding synonym scales — that words with similar meanings carry different intensities and connotations — is a key component of building Tier 2 vocabulary at Years 5–6.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Selecting words for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience," and to "consider the effect of word choice on the reader." This lesson directly builds that skill by teaching students to match word intensity to the meaning they want to convey, rather than defaulting to the first word they think of.


#### Tuesday: Subject-verb agreement (singular/plural)

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): a verb must agree in number with the subject of the clause; students write grammatically correct sentences and identify and correct errors in their own writing.

**I Do — Subjects & Verbs Must Match**

The subject and verb in a sentence must agree in number. Singular subject = singular verb. Plural subject = plural verb. Watch how I check the match.

> Example: The dog barks. ✓ | The dogs bark. ✓ | The dogs barks. ✗

I find the subject first: "the dogs" — plural. Now the verb: "barks" — singular (the -s gives it away). They don't match! Fix: "bark." Rule: when the subject has an -s (plural), the verb usually doesn't, and vice versa.

*Tip: Cross out any words between subject and verb — they can trick you. "The group of students [are/is]..." → subject is "group" (singular) → "is."*

**We Do — Match the Verb**

Choose the correct verb form for each sentence and explain why.
- The bunch of grapes (was / were) left on the bench.
- Her three brothers (play / plays) for the same rugby team.
- Everyone in the class (know / knows) the answer.

*Discussion prompt: What is the true subject? Is it singular or plural?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Circle the correct verb form and rewrite the sentences.
- The flock of birds (move / moves) south in winter.
- My best friend and I (love / loves) going to the movies.
- Each of the sandwiches (need / needs) a label.
- The team (was / were) celebrating their win on the field.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two tricky subject-verb sentences for a partner to solve.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Subject-verb agreement: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "A verb must agree in number and person with the subject of the clause. Errors in subject-verb agreement disrupt the grammatical accuracy of a sentence." Students learn to identify the true subject (ignoring intervening phrases) and select the correct verb form — including tricky cases such as collective nouns and indefinite pronouns.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Identifying and correcting errors: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate." Recognising and fixing subject-verb agreement errors is a core component of that accuracy check, and a skill students are expected to apply independently in their own writing at Years 5–6.


#### Wednesday: Compound sentences using 'and', 'but', 'so'

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): compound sentences join two main clauses using a coordinating conjunction; students use a variety of sentence structures, including compound sentences, for effect and clarity.

**I Do — Join Two Ideas Together**

A compound sentence joins two simple sentences using a coordinating conjunction: "and" (adds), "but" (contrasts), "so" (shows result). Watch me join two short sentences into one smooth compound sentence.

> Example: The bell rang. The students rushed outside. → The bell rang, and the students rushed outside.

I put a comma before the conjunction when joining two full sentences. Let me try "but": "I wanted to go swimming, but the pool was closed." And "so": "It was raining heavily, so we moved the game inside." Each conjunction gives a different meaning!

*Tip: Remember the comma before the conjunction when joining two complete sentences.*

**We Do — Join the Pairs**

Join these pairs of sentences using "and," "but," or "so." Discuss which conjunction fits best and why.
- She studied all weekend. She still felt nervous about the test.
- The campfire burned brightly. Everyone gathered around it.
- Jake forgot his lunch. His friend shared her sandwich with him.

*Discussion prompt: Why does "but" work better than "and" in the first one? Where does the comma go?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Join each pair of sentences with the best conjunction (and/but/so). Remember your comma!
- The explorers were exhausted. They pushed on through the jungle.
- The dog dug a hole under the fence. It escaped into the neighbour's garden.
- She practised every day. She became the best swimmer in her school.
- ✦ Bonus: Write three compound sentences about your last school holiday — use "and" once, "but" once, and "so" once.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Compound sentences & conjunctions: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "A compound sentence consists of two or more main clauses joined by a coordinating conjunction (and, but, or, so, yet). A comma is placed before the conjunction when joining two complete clauses." Students learn that the choice of conjunction shapes the relationship between ideas — addition, contrast, or result.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Using sentence variety for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures — simple, compound, and complex — for effect and clarity." This lesson introduces compound sentences as the first step beyond the simple sentence, giving students a tool for connecting related ideas smoothly and varying the rhythm of their writing.


#### Thursday: Editing: identifying vague words & upgrading them

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Practice) & Writing Process (Practice): students select precise words for effect and reread their writing to identify and replace vague or overused language.

**I Do — Hunt the Vague Words**

Vague words are words so overused they've lost their meaning: nice, good, bad, big, small, went, said, things, got. Watch me find them in this passage and replace them with precise language.

> Example: It was a nice day and we went to a nice beach. The water was nice and cold and we had a good time.

I underline every vague word: nice (×3), went, good. Now I replace: "It was a golden, windless day and we drove to a sparkling beach. The water was refreshingly cold and we had a brilliant time." Far more vivid!

*Tip: After writing, circle every "nice," "good," "went," "said," "got" and challenge yourself to upgrade at least half of them.*

**We Do — Upgrade the Passage**

Let's find and replace all the vague words in this passage together.
- The big dog got up and went to the door. It made a loud noise and looked at us. We thought it was a good dog but it was a bit scary. Then things got better when its owner came.

*Discussion prompt: Which words can we upgrade? What more precise/vivid option works in each spot?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this passage by replacing every underlined vague word with a more precise one.
- The nice teacher said that our project was good. We had worked on it for a long time and got everything ready the night before. The big display looked great and all our stuff was neatly arranged.
- ✦ Bonus: Pull out your last piece of writing and hunt for three vague words to upgrade.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Replacing vague language: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience," and to "consider the effect of word choice on the reader." This lesson targets the most common vocabulary weakness at Years 5–6 — over-reliance on vague, all-purpose words — and builds the habit of deliberate, precise word selection.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Revising for word precision: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." This editing lesson directly practises that revision process: identifying weak language and replacing it with words that create a clearer, more vivid picture for the reader.


### Week 4

#### Monday: Apostrophes for possession

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): apostrophes are used to show possession; the placement of the apostrophe changes depending on whether the owner is singular or plural.

**I Do — Who Does It Belong To?**

An apostrophe + s ('s) shows that something belongs to someone. If the owner is singular, add 's. If the owner is plural and already ends in s, just add the apostrophe after: s'.

> Example: Singular: the cat's collar | Plural: the cats' collars

One cat owns the collar → cat's (apostrophe before s). Multiple cats own collars → cats' (apostrophe after s). The trick: find the owner first. 'The collar belonging to the cat' = cat → cat's. 'The collars belonging to the cats' = cats → cats'.

*Tip: Find the owner. If singular → add 's. If plural ending in s → add just '.*

**We Do — Find the Owner**

Let's rewrite these 'belonging' phrases using apostrophes, and sort them into singular vs. plural owner.
- The jersey belonging to the player → ___
- The homework belonging to the students → ___
- The beak belonging to the parrot → ___

*Discussion prompt: Who is the owner? How many owners are there? Where does the apostrophe go?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each phrase using an apostrophe to show possession.
- The den belonging to the fox → ___
- The shoes belonging to the dancers → ___
- The medal belonging to the swimmer → ___
- The nests belonging to the birds → ___
- ✦ Bonus: Write four sentences about things you can see in the classroom using possessive apostrophes.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Apostrophes for possession: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Apostrophes are used to show possession — singular owners take 's (e.g. the cat's collar) and plural owners already ending in s take an apostrophe after the s (e.g. the cats' collars)." Students at Years 5–6 are expected to use possessive apostrophes correctly and consistently in their own writing.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying apostrophes accurately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect," including apostrophes for possession. This lesson builds a reliable two-step strategy — identify the owner, then count them — that students can apply independently when editing their own writing.


#### Tuesday: Pronouns: personal & possessive

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): pronouns are a word class that replace nouns to avoid repetition; students use personal and possessive pronouns accurately to create cohesion and clarity.

**I Do — Standing In for Nouns**

Pronouns replace nouns so we don't repeat the same word over and over. Personal pronouns (I, you, he, she, it, we, they) and possessive pronouns (my, your, his, her, its, our, their) are the most common. Watch me substitute them in.

> Example: Maya left Maya's bag on Maya's seat when Maya went outside.

This sounds clunky! Replace: "Maya left her bag on her seat when she went outside." The pronoun "her" replaces "Maya's" (possessive). "she" replaces "Maya" (personal). Much smoother!

*Tip: Make sure your pronoun clearly refers back to the right noun — if there are two possible people, the reader gets confused.*

**We Do — Swap the Repeated Nouns**

Replace the repeated nouns with the correct pronouns.
- The team trained hard. The team wanted the team's season to be the team's best ever.
- Tom and Sarah arrived early. Tom and Sarah set up Tom and Sarah's stall at the market.

*Discussion prompt: What pronoun replaces "the team"? What about "Tom and Sarah"? Which is possessive?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence replacing the repeated nouns with correct personal or possessive pronouns.
- The explorer found the explorer's compass in the explorer's coat pocket.
- The dolphins leaped out of the water. The dolphins seemed to enjoy the dolphins' freedom.
- Lily and Ben packed Lily and Ben's bags. Lily and Ben left early for Lily and Ben's trip.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph where you use at least 4 different pronouns correctly.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Word classes: pronouns: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Words belong to different classes… pronouns are words that replace nouns and must agree with the nouns they refer to in number and person." Students learn to distinguish personal pronouns (replacing subjects and objects) from possessive pronouns (showing ownership) — two distinct functions that are frequently confused at Years 5–6.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Cohesion through pronoun use: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "use cohesive devices — including pronouns, synonyms, and connectives — to link ideas within and across sentences and paragraphs." Using pronouns correctly is a primary cohesion strategy: it removes repetition and helps the writing flow smoothly from one sentence to the next.


#### Wednesday: Writing topic sentences for paragraphs

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge & Practice): paragraphs are organised around a main idea expressed in a topic sentence; students structure their writing with clear paragraph organisation.

**I Do — The Anchor Sentence**

A topic sentence is the first sentence of a paragraph. It tells the reader the main idea. All the other sentences in the paragraph support or explain it. Watch me write one.

> Example: Topic: Why exercise is important → Topic sentence: "Exercise is one of the most important habits a person can build for a healthy life."

My topic sentence: (1) introduces the topic, (2) gives the writer's angle on it (not just "exercise is good" — too vague). The sentences that follow will each give a reason or example to support "one of the most important habits." I won't switch to a new topic mid-paragraph.

*Tip: Test your topic sentence: could every other sentence in your paragraph be "evidence" for it? If yes — it works!*

**We Do — Match & Write**

Match each topic sentence to the paragraph that fits it best. Then write a topic sentence for an unpaired paragraph.
- Topic sentence A: "Dogs make loyal and loving companions for all ages."
- Topic sentence B: "The rainforest is home to an astonishing variety of wildlife."
- Paragraph without a topic sentence: "...Thousands of species of insects live under its canopy. Brightly coloured frogs cling to leaves, and jaguars prowl silently below. Even the trees themselves host miniature ecosystems..."

*Discussion prompt: Which topic sentence fits the mystery paragraph? What clues told you?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a strong topic sentence for each group of supporting sentences.
- Supporting sentences: "First, it keeps your heart strong. It also helps you sleep better and boosts your mood. Even a short walk each day makes a difference."
- Supporting sentences: "Rubbish can entangle sea turtles and birds. Plastic bags are mistaken for jellyfish by whales. Tiny particles enter the food chain through fish."
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full paragraph of 4 sentences: topic sentence + 3 supports, on a topic of your choice.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge)* — Paragraph organisation & topic sentences: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge): "Paragraphs group related ideas together, usually organised around a main idea introduced in a topic sentence. Supporting sentences develop, explain, or provide evidence for the main idea." Students at Years 5–6 are expected to understand that each paragraph has a clear focus and that the topic sentence signals this focus to the reader.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Structuring paragraphs effectively: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "organise ideas into paragraphs with a clear main idea, supported by relevant detail." This lesson builds the habit of writing a controlling topic sentence before adding supporting detail — a key structural skill for all text types at Years 5–6, from narrative to argument.


#### Thursday: Editing: checking pronoun-antecedent agreement

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice) & Writing Process (Practice): pronouns must agree with their antecedents in number; students identify and correct pronoun errors when revising their own writing.

**I Do — Does the Pronoun Match?**

A pronoun must agree with its antecedent (the noun it replaces) in number and gender. Singular noun → singular pronoun. Plural noun → plural pronoun. Watch me find and fix the mismatches.

> Example: The team picked up their trophy. ✗ → The team picked up its trophy. ✓

"The team" is singular (one team) so the pronoun should be "its" not "their." Now: "Each student brought their lunch." Here "each student" is singular, so technically "his or her lunch" — but in modern usage "their" is widely accepted for singular subjects. Context matters!

*Tip: Find the noun the pronoun replaces. Count it: one → singular pronoun, many → plural.*

**We Do — Spot & Fix**

Find and fix the pronoun agreement errors in these sentences.
- Every player gave their best, but the team lost their match in the final minute.
- The flock of geese spread their wings and took off across the lake.
- Anyone who finishes early can bring their book to reading corner.

*Discussion prompt: What is the antecedent? Is it singular or plural? Does the pronoun match?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Fix any pronoun agreement errors. Write "correct" if there is no error.
- The committee made their decision before the deadline.
- Each of the birds had lost their feathers in the storm.
- Neither of the girls wanted to give up their spot in the team.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph about a sports team. Use at least 3 different pronouns correctly — and then swap with a partner to check.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Pronoun-antecedent agreement: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "A pronoun must agree with its antecedent — the noun it replaces — in number (singular or plural) and person. Errors in pronoun agreement can confuse the reader about who or what is being referred to." Students also encounter the modern acceptance of singular 'they' and learn to navigate these nuances in context.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing for pronoun accuracy: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate." This editing lesson trains students to check every pronoun against its antecedent — a targeted revision strategy that improves grammatical accuracy and reader clarity in their own writing.


### Week 5

#### Monday: Exclamation marks & question marks – when & why

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): sentences end with a full stop, question mark, or exclamation mark; the choice of end punctuation signals the purpose and tone of the sentence to the reader.

**I Do — The Right Ending**

Every sentence ends with a full stop, question mark, or exclamation mark. The wrong choice changes the reader's experience completely. Watch me demonstrate why the ending matters.

> Example: "Come back." vs "Come back!" vs "Will you come back?"

"Come back." — a calm instruction. "Come back!" — urgent, emotional, even desperate. "Will you come back?" — a genuine question, uncertain. The same two words — completely different meanings from punctuation alone. Exclamation marks are powerful, so use them sparingly or they lose impact.

*Tip: Ask yourself: Am I expressing strong emotion or urgency? → ! | Am I asking a genuine question? → ? | Everything else → .*

**We Do — Choose the Right Mark**

Add the correct end punctuation and explain your choice.
- How did the magician make the coin disappear
- Watch out for the wave behind you
- The library opens at nine o'clock every morning

*Discussion prompt: What emotion or purpose is in each sentence? Would a different mark change the meaning?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add the correct end punctuation to each sentence and write a short explanation of your choice.
- Did you hear that noise in the middle of the night
- Never open that door again
- The museum was closed on the day we planned to visit
- I can't believe we actually won
- ✦ Bonus: Write one sentence where changing the end mark completely changes the meaning. Share with a partner.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — End punctuation: . ? !: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Sentences end with a full stop, question mark, or exclamation mark. The choice of end punctuation signals the sentence's purpose to the reader: statements use full stops, questions use question marks, and exclamations express strong emotion or urgency." Students learn that punctuation is a meaning-making tool, not just a convention to follow.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Punctuation for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect." This includes using exclamation marks sparingly for genuine impact, and understanding that overuse weakens their effect. Deliberate punctuation choices contribute to the tone, pace, and mood of a piece of writing.


#### Tuesday: Adjectives: degrees of comparison (er/est/more/most)

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): adjectives can be modified to express degrees of comparison using comparative (-er/more) and superlative (-est/most) forms, including irregular forms.

**I Do — Comparing with Adjectives**

We use comparative adjectives to compare two things, and superlatives to compare three or more. Short adjectives add -er/-est. Longer adjectives use more/most. Watch me form them.

> Example: fast → faster (comparative) → fastest (superlative)
beautiful → more beautiful → most beautiful

Rule: 1–2 syllable adjectives → -er/-est: tall/taller/tallest. 3+ syllable adjectives → more/most: exciting/more exciting/most exciting. Exceptions: good/better/best, bad/worse/worst, far/further/furthest.

*Tip: Try adding -er: if it sounds natural, use it. If it sounds awkward (intelligenter?), use "more."*

**We Do — Form the Comparatives**

Form the comparative and superlative of each adjective and use each in a sentence.
- cold → ___ → ___
- dangerous → ___ → ___
- good → ___ → ___

*Discussion prompt: Which rule applies? Are there any exceptions here? Can you use each in an example sentence?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Complete the comparison chart and then write sentences using the correct form.
- happy → ___ → ___  |  Use "happiest" in a sentence.
- mysterious → ___ → ___  |  Use "more mysterious" in a sentence.
- bad → ___ → ___  |  Use "worst" in a sentence.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph comparing two animals you know, using at least four comparative or superlative adjectives.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Comparative & superlative adjectives: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Adjectives can be modified to express degrees of comparison. Comparative forms (using -er or 'more') compare two things; superlative forms (using -est or 'most') compare three or more. Some adjectives have irregular comparative and superlative forms (good/better/best)." Students learn and apply the spelling and usage rules for both regular and irregular forms.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Precise comparative language: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience." Using comparative and superlative adjectives precisely — rather than defaulting to vague modifiers like 'very good' — is a key marker of more sophisticated vocabulary use at Years 5–6.


#### Wednesday: Sentence variety: short vs. long sentences

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): students use a variety of sentence lengths deliberately for effect, understanding that mixing short and long sentences creates rhythm and controls the pace of writing.

**I Do — Playing with Rhythm**

Good writers vary their sentence length on purpose. Short sentences create tension and punch. Long sentences slow the pace and build atmosphere. Watch how I mix them for effect.

> Example: Long only: "As the fog rolled silently in off the sea and crept through the narrow streets, wrapping itself around the lampposts and filling every doorway with a pale grey shadow, she stood completely still and listened to the sound of the city slowly disappearing." → Too slow.

Now watch: "The fog rolled in. It crept through the streets, wrapping itself around lampposts, filling every doorway. She stood still. Listened. The city was disappearing." The short sentences (She stood still. Listened.) hit hard. The long one builds atmosphere. Mixing them creates rhythm.

*Tip: Read your writing aloud. Does it have a beat — fast/slow/fast? If everything is the same length, vary it.*

**We Do — Mix It Up**

This passage has all long sentences. Let's break some up to create rhythm together.
- The ancient lighthouse had stood on the cliff for over two hundred years and had guided thousands of ships safely past the rocks and through the treacherous harbour entrance in every kind of weather imaginable. It had survived storms and earthquakes and the battering of winter waves and still it stood, its light still turning, still warning, still saving lives that would otherwise have been lost to the sea.

*Discussion prompt: Where could we cut for impact? Which short sentence would create the most drama?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this all-short paragraph by combining some sentences into longer ones, while keeping 1–2 short ones for effect.
- The fire started. It spread fast. Smoke filled the hall. People ran. They called for help. The engines arrived. The firefighters moved quickly. They brought it under control.
- ✦ Bonus: Write 6 sentences about a storm — 2 short, 2 medium, 2 long. Read them aloud to check the rhythm.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Sentence length for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity," including deliberately mixing sentence lengths. Short sentences create urgency and emphasis; long sentences build atmosphere and detail. This lesson develops students' awareness that sentence length is a craft choice, not an accident.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Controlling pace & rhythm: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader," including controlling pace and rhythm. Varying sentence length is one of the most accessible ways to create a sense of drama, tension, or calm — and this lesson gives students a practical tool to apply immediately in their own writing.


#### Thursday: Editing: peer-checking capitals & punctuation

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students use an editing checklist to review writing for punctuation accuracy; peer editing builds the habit of reading writing critically and giving specific feedback.

**I Do — Using an Editing Checklist**

Professional editors use checklists so they don't miss anything. Watch me use our class punctuation checklist on a short passage — I'll mark each issue as I find it.

> Example: checklist: ☐ Capital at start of every sentence ☐ Full stop/!/? at end ☐ Commas in lists ☐ Proper nouns capitalised ☐ Apostrophes correct

I read the passage once just to understand it. Then I go through with the checklist — one check at a time. I mark issues with a light pencil. I explain why each fix is needed, not just what to change.

*Tip: Never try to check everything at once — work through one rule at a time on separate read-throughs.*

**We Do — Peer Edit in Pairs**

With your partner, use the checklist to edit this passage. One person checks capitals; the other checks full stops and commas.
- last thursday, mr. tane's class visited te papa museum in wellington. the students saw a giant squid a huge whale skeleton and many taonga from around new zealand. aisha's favourite exhibit was the earthquake house she said "it felt so real." everyone agreed it was an amazing trip

*Discussion prompt: What did you each find? Did you agree on every correction? Compare notes.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Edit this passage alone using the full checklist. Mark and rewrite all corrections.
- on saturday morning, emma and her brother luca decided to bake a chocolate cake. they found flour eggs butter sugar and cocoa in the cupboard. the recipe was easy but luca forgot to add the baking powder. the cake came out flat and dense but mum said it still tasted delicious. emma said "next time i'm doing it on my own"
- ✦ Bonus: Swap with a partner and compare corrections. Discuss any differences.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing with a checklist: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking for correct punctuation and capitalisation." This lesson builds the systematic editing habit — one pass per rule — that prevents students from trying to fix everything at once and missing errors as a result.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Peer editing & feedback: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "share writing with others and respond to feedback to improve their work." Peer editing is an explicit Phase 2 expectation: students practise giving specific, rule-based feedback (not just "it's good") and comparing their corrections to develop shared understanding of conventions.


### Week 6

#### Monday: Word families – roots, prefixes, suffixes

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): many English words are built from roots, prefixes, and suffixes; understanding word parts unlocks the meaning of unfamiliar words and expands vocabulary.

**I Do — Words Are Built from Parts**

Most English words are built from parts: a root (the core meaning), a prefix (added to the front), and/or a suffix (added to the end). Knowing the parts unlocks the meaning of unknown words. Watch me break one apart.

> Example: unhappiness → un- (not) + happy (root) + -ness (state of being) = "the state of not being happy"

Let me try "prehistoric": pre- (before) + historic (relating to history) = "before recorded history." Now "transportation": trans- (across) + port (carry) + -ation (process) = "the process of carrying things across." Once you know common roots, you can work out hundreds of words!

*Tip: Common roots to remember: port (carry), rupt (break), vis (see), struct (build), dict (say), graph (write).*

**We Do — Break Them Down**

Let's pull apart these words using our knowledge of prefixes, roots, and suffixes. What does each part tell us?
- impossible → ___ + ___ = meaning?
- prediction → ___ + ___ + ___ = meaning?
- exportable → ___ + ___ + ___ = meaning?

*Discussion prompt: How does knowing the prefix "im-" help you with impossible, impolite, impractical?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Break apart each word into its prefix/root/suffix. Write the meaning of each part, then the whole word.
- invisible → 
- reconstruct → 
- disagreement → 
- photography → 
- ✦ Bonus: Use your knowledge of "port" (carry) to work out: import, export, portable, transport, reporter.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Word roots, prefixes & suffixes: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Many English words are built from Latin and Greek roots, prefixes, and suffixes. Understanding the meaning of word parts enables students to work out the meaning of unfamiliar words and to build their vocabulary systematically." This lesson develops morphological awareness — a key component of Tier 2 and Tier 3 vocabulary development at Years 5–6.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Using word knowledge in writing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "use knowledge of word origins, roots, and word families to extend vocabulary." Understanding that "port" means carry (import, export, transport) gives students a transferable tool for both reading unfamiliar words and selecting more precise vocabulary in their own writing.


#### Tuesday: Adverbs: how, when, where

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): adverbs modify verbs, adjectives, or other adverbs and express manner, time, and place; students use adverbs deliberately to add precision and detail.

**I Do — Adverbs Tell Us More**

Adverbs modify (add information to) verbs, adjectives, or other adverbs. They answer How? When? Where? and To what extent? Watch me add adverbs to upgrade a simple sentence.

> Example: The explorer walked. → The explorer walked cautiously (how) forward (where) yesterday (when).

I add them one at a time and notice how each one changes the meaning. How? → cautiously (tells us the manner). Where? → forward (tells us direction). When? → yesterday (tells us time). I can layer adverbs, but I should be selective — too many and the sentence becomes clunky.

*Tip: Adverbs of manner often end in -ly (quickly, silently) — but not always (fast, hard, late).*

**We Do — Add the Right Adverb**

Add an adverb (how/when/where) to each sentence and label what type it is.
- The hawk circled ___.
- ___ the team gathered for their final practice.
- She whispered ___ so no one would hear.

*Discussion prompt: What question does your adverb answer? Could you add a second adverb of a different type?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add one adverb of each type (how/when/where) to the sentence. Then write your own sentence using all three types.
- The scientist worked ___ (how) ___ (when) ___ (where) to find an answer.
- Add how, when, where: "The wolves hunted."
- ✦ Bonus: Find three adverbs in a book you're reading. Write which verb each modifies and what question it answers.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Word classes: adverbs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Adverbs modify verbs, adjectives, or other adverbs. They can express manner (how), time (when), place (where), and degree (to what extent). Adverbs of manner often end in -ly but not always." Students learn to identify adverb types and understand their grammatical role as modifiers within the sentence.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Adding precision with adverbs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "expand sentences by adding adverbs and adverbial phrases to express manner, time, and place." This lesson builds the habit of asking 'how? when? where?' when editing bare sentences — adding only the adverbial detail that genuinely improves precision, not padding.


#### Wednesday: Adding detail with 'who', 'where', 'when' phrases

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): students expand sentences by adding noun phrases, prepositional phrases, and relative clauses that answer who, where, and when to create a fuller picture for the reader.

**I Do — Dress the Sentence Up**

A bare sentence tells us the basics. Adding WHO/WHERE/WHEN phrases gives the reader a full picture. Watch me build one sentence three ways.

> Example: Base: The girl read a book. → + WHERE: The girl read a book in the shade of an old oak tree. → + WHO: The girl, who had borrowed it from the library that morning, read a book in the shade. → + WHEN: Just as the sun peaked at noon, the girl, who had borrowed it that morning, read a book in the shade.

Each phrase layers in more context. The reader can now picture the scene, the person, and the moment. I don't have to add all three — I choose the ones that matter most for the story.

*Tip: Ask: does my reader know enough to picture this? If not, add one specific WHERE, WHO, or WHEN phrase.*

**We Do — Layer the Detail**

Let's expand these sentences together by adding a who, where, and when phrase.
- The dog barked.
- A boat drifted.
- The children cheered.

*Discussion prompt: Which detail matters most for each sentence? Does every detail need to be there, or can we cut one?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Expand each sentence by adding at least two detail phrases (who/where/when).
- The door slammed.
- A light flickered.
- The crowd fell silent.
- ✦ Bonus: Write an expanded sentence about a character arriving somewhere unexpected. Include who they are, where exactly they arrived, and when.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Expanding sentences with detail phrases: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "expand simple sentences by adding words, phrases, and clauses to add detail and description." Adding who, where, and when phrases is a concrete, scaffolded way to develop this skill — students practise choosing which type of detail best serves each sentence rather than adding detail randomly.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Creating vivid, specific writing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader," including providing specific, concrete detail. This lesson directly addresses the vagueness of bare sentences by giving students a systematic question framework (who? where? when?) to identify and fill gaps in their descriptions.


#### Thursday: Editing: tightening wordy sentences

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice) & Vocabulary (Practice): students revise writing to remove redundant words and replace wordy constructions with precise, concise language.

**I Do — Cut the Fluff**

Good writing is precise. Wordy sentences say in ten words what could be said in five. Watch me find and cut redundant words without losing meaning.

> Example: "Due to the fact that it was raining, we decided to stay inside the house." → "Because it was raining, we stayed inside."

"Due to the fact that" = just "because." "decided to stay" = "stayed." "inside the house" = "inside" (where else would you stay?). I cut 13 words to 7 — same meaning, cleaner punch. Phrases to watch: "in order to" (→ "to"), "at this point in time" (→ "now"), "on a daily basis" (→ "daily").

*Tip: After writing a sentence, ask: can I cut any word without losing meaning? If yes — cut it.*

**We Do — Tighten Together**

Rewrite these wordy sentences concisely. Count the words before and after.
- The reason why the match was cancelled was due to the fact that the field was too wet.
- At this present moment in time, the students are in the process of completing their assignment.
- She made the decision to walk in the direction of the library in order to return the book.

*Discussion prompt: Which words are doing real work? Which are filler? What is the minimum word count that keeps full meaning?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence as concisely as possible. Record the word count before and after.
- In the event that the weather is bad, we will need to make the decision to move the event inside. (___→___words)
- He was a person who always showed a great deal of enthusiasm for all the different things that he was interested in. (___→___words)
- ✦ Bonus: Take a paragraph you've written recently and cut it by 20% — same meaning, fewer words.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Revising for conciseness: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson targets a specific and common revision skill: identifying and cutting wordy constructions that obscure meaning, teaching students that fewer, well-chosen words are almost always stronger than more.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Precise word choice over circumlocution: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience." Replacing wordy phrases ("made the decision to") with single precise verbs ("decided") is a key vocabulary skill at Years 5–6 — it shows students understand that one exact word is more powerful than a cluster of vague ones.


### Week 7

#### Monday: Colons & semicolons – introduction

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): a colon introduces a list or explanation; a semicolon joins two closely related independent clauses; both are used deliberately for clarity and effect.

**I Do — Two Powerful Marks**

A colon (:) introduces something — a list, an explanation, or a dramatic reveal. A semicolon (;) joins two closely related complete sentences without a conjunction. Watch me use both.

> Example: Colon: "She needed three things: courage, patience, and luck."
Semicolon: "The sun set behind the hills; the valley grew cold."

Colon: I use it after a complete sentence when what follows explains or lists. "She had one goal: to finish the race." Semicolon: Both sides must be complete sentences that are closely linked in meaning. "I love summer; the long evenings are my favourite part."

*Tip: Test the colon: could you put "namely" or "that is" there? Test the semicolon: are both halves complete sentences that relate to each other?*

**We Do — Colon or Semicolon?**

Decide whether each gap needs a colon or semicolon and explain why.
- The storm brought everything with it ___ rain, hail, thunder, and a biting wind.
- She loved the city ___ she had lived there all her life.
- He only had one request ___ please don't wake him before 8 o'clock.

*Discussion prompt: Does what follows LIST or EXPLAIN? Or are you joining two complete ideas? That tells you which to use.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add a colon or semicolon to each sentence and write a brief reason for your choice.
- The menu offered four choices pizza, pasta, salad, and soup.
- The hike was long and exhausting the view from the summit was worth every step.
- She packed everything she would need a torch, a map, a compass, and enough food for two days.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two of your own sentences — one using a colon correctly, one using a semicolon correctly.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Colons & semicolons: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "A colon is used after a complete clause to introduce a list, explanation, or quotation. A semicolon joins two independent clauses that are closely related in meaning, without using a conjunction." Students at Years 5–6 are introduced to these higher-level punctuation marks as tools that add clarity and sophistication to their writing.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Using colons & semicolons for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect," progressing beyond basic sentence-end punctuation to more complex marks. This lesson teaches the decision-making process behind each mark: 'Am I introducing something, or joining two related ideas?' — building usage that is intentional rather than guessed.


#### Tuesday: Tense consistency: simple past & present

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge) & Writing Process (Practice): verbs must remain consistent in tense throughout a piece of writing; students identify and correct tense inconsistencies when revising.

**I Do — Pick a Tense and Stay There**

When you write a story or recount, you choose a tense — usually past or present — and stay in it the whole way through. Switching tenses mid-passage confuses the reader. Watch me spot and fix a tense switch.

> Example: Past: "The knight rode into the village. He looks around nervously. The villagers gathered in the square."

"rode" — past. "looks" — suddenly present! "gathered" — back to past. The switch makes the reader stumble. Fix: "The knight rode into the village. He looked around nervously. The villagers gathered in the square." — all past. Or I could rewrite all in present: "The knight rides... He looks... The villagers gather..." — both are valid, just choose one.

*Tip: Circle all your verbs. Are they all the same tense? If not, decide which tense your piece should be in and adjust.*

**We Do — Spot the Switches**

Find and fix all tense inconsistencies in this passage. Rewrite it in consistent simple past.
- The young inventor enters her workshop at dawn. She had spent months designing the machine. She checks each part carefully and placed the final gear. The machine starts to hum. It worked.

*Discussion prompt: Which verbs are in the wrong tense? Rewrite in consistent past tense.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite the passage in consistent simple present tense. Then identify and fix the errors.
- Rewrite in present tense: "The geologist climbed the rocky slope. She examined the layers of sediment. Each layer told a story about the earth's past. She makes careful notes and photographs each formation."
- ✦ Bonus: Write an 8-sentence recount (any topic) in consistent simple past tense. Swap with a partner to check.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Tense consistency: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Verbs must remain consistent in tense throughout a piece of writing. Shifts between past and present tense disrupt the reader's experience and signal a lack of control over the writing. Students choose a tense appropriate to text type and purpose and maintain it throughout." Simple past is standard for recounts and narratives; simple present for procedures and some descriptions.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Auditing tense in revision: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate." Doing a tense audit — underlining every verb and labelling it P (past) or Pr (present) — is a targeted revision strategy that catches the most common grammar error in Year 5–6 narrative and recount writing.


#### Wednesday: Complex sentences: main + subordinate clause

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): a complex sentence contains a main clause and a subordinate clause joined by a subordinating conjunction; students use complex sentences to add detail, show relationships, and vary sentence structure.

**I Do — The Main Idea + Extra Detail**

A complex sentence has a main clause (complete thought) and a subordinate clause (depends on the main clause for meaning). They are joined by a subordinating conjunction: because, although, when, if, since, while, unless, after, before. Watch me build one.

> Example: Main clause: "The crowd cheered." + Subordinating conjunction: "when" + Subordinate clause: "the final whistle blew." → "The crowd cheered when the final whistle blew."

I can also flip it — subordinate clause first: "When the final whistle blew, the crowd cheered." Notice: when the subordinate clause comes first, I add a comma after it. When it comes second, no comma needed.

*Tip: If you start with the subordinate clause, use a comma after it. Starter at the end? No comma.*

**We Do — Build the Complex Sentence**

Join these pairs into complex sentences using an appropriate subordinating conjunction. Try putting the subordinate clause at the start in one version and at the end in another.
- She missed the bus. She left the house late.
- The crops failed. The summer was unusually dry.

*Discussion prompt: Which conjunction fits? Does the meaning change if we swap the clause order? Where does the comma go?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write complex sentences using the given conjunction. Try one with the subordinate clause first, one with it second.
- Use "although": ___ (subordinate clause first, with comma)
- Use "because": ___ (subordinate clause second, no comma)
- Use "unless": ___ (your choice of order)
- ✦ Bonus: Find three complex sentences in a book you're reading. Identify the main clause and subordinate clause in each.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Complex sentences & subordinate clauses: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "A complex sentence contains a main clause and one or more subordinate clauses joined by a subordinating conjunction (e.g. because, although, when, if, since, while, unless). When the subordinate clause is fronted, a comma separates it from the main clause." Understanding the structure of complex sentences is a key Phase 2 grammar expectation.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Using complex sentences for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures — including complex sentences — for effect and clarity," using subordinating conjunctions to express relationships of cause, contrast, condition, and time. This lesson gives students explicit control over clause order and the punctuation that comes with it.


#### Thursday: Editing: checking tense consistency in a passage

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply a systematic verb audit to identify and correct tense inconsistencies in their own and modelled writing.

**I Do — The Tense Audit**

I'll show you a systematic way to check tense in a piece of writing. I underline every verb, then check that they're all in the same tense.

> Example: Step 1: Underline all verbs. Step 2: Label each P (past) or Pr (present). Step 3: Decide which tense the piece should be. Step 4: Fix mismatches.

"The explorer sets out at dawn. She walked for three hours before she reaches the ridge." — sets (Pr), walked (P), reaches (Pr). Two present, one past. I decide: past tense works better for a recount. Fix: "The explorer set out at dawn. She walked for three hours before she reached the ridge."

*Tip: Be especially careful with "was/is" and "said/says" — these are the most common accidental switches.*

**We Do — Tense Audit Together**

Let's audit this passage together. Underline every verb, label its tense, then rewrite it in consistent past tense.
- The market is busy on Saturday mornings. Stalls lined both sides of the street. Vendors called out their prices. The smell of fresh bread drifted through the air. Families stopped to chat and children ran between the stalls. It felt like the whole town was there.

*Discussion prompt: Which verbs switch tense? What is the most common tense here? Rewrite consistently.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Underline all verbs in this passage, label each one P or Pr, then rewrite it in consistent past tense.
- The diver slips below the surface. The water was cold and very clear. She searches for the wreck that locals told her about. After ten minutes, she spots a shadow on the seabed. She swam closer and felt her heart race.
- ✦ Bonus: Take a piece of your own writing and do a full tense audit on it. Note how many switches you find.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Systematic tense audit: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate." This lesson teaches the tense audit as a specific, repeatable revision strategy — underline every verb, label the tense, decide on consistency, fix mismatches. It is a direct application of the Phase 2 expectation that students revise systematically, not just casually re-read.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Identifying tense in context: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Verbs carry tense and must remain consistent throughout a piece of writing." This editing lesson deepens grammatical knowledge by requiring students to identify verb tense in context — distinguishing simple past, simple present, and past continuous forms — before applying that knowledge to make accurate corrections.


### Week 8

#### Monday: Homophones & commonly confused words

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Conventions (Knowledge): homophones are words that sound the same but have different spellings and meanings; students use meaning and grammatical context to select the correct form.

**I Do — Same Sound, Different Meaning**

Homophones sound the same but have different spellings and meanings. Spellcheck won't catch these — you have to know them! Watch me use context to choose the right one.

> Example: there / their / they're | to / too / two | your / you're

"Put it over ___." — "there" (place). "It was ___ idea." — "their" (belonging to them). "___ late." — "they're" (they are). I always check: Does a pronoun meaning "they are" fit? → they're. Does "belonging to them" fit? → their. A place or pointing? → there.

*Tip: Expand contractions to check: "they're" = "they are." "you're" = "you are." If the expansion doesn't make sense — use the other spelling.*

**We Do — Choose Correctly**

Choose the correct homophone for each gap and explain the rule that helped you.
- ___ going to need ___ bring ___ own gear. (they're/their/there | to/too/two)
- I don't know ___ ___ ready yet — ___ bags are still in the hall. (whether/weather | you're/your | they're/their/there)

*Discussion prompt: Can you expand any of them? What meaning clue helped you choose?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Fill in the correct homophone. Write the rule you used in brackets.
- We packed ___ bags the night before. (their/there/they're) [___]
- The ___ yesterday was cold and grey. (weather/whether) [___]
- I've read this book ___ many times. (to/too/two) [___]
- I can't decide ___ to go or stay. (weather/whether) [___]
- ✦ Bonus: Write four sentences of your own, each using a different homophone pair correctly.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Conventions (Knowledge)* — Homophones & spelling in context: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Conventions (Knowledge): "Homophones are words that sound the same but have different spellings and meanings (e.g. there/their/they're, to/too/two, your/you're). Selecting the correct form requires understanding meaning and grammatical function, as spellcheck cannot distinguish between them." Students develop the habit of checking homophone choices against context and meaning.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing for homophone errors: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking spelling." Homophone errors are among the most common and persistent spelling issues at Years 5–6, and this lesson directly addresses them by giving students a meaning-checking strategy (expand the contraction; ask what the word means here) they can apply independently.


#### Tuesday: Prepositional phrases

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): prepositions show relationships of location, time, and direction; prepositional phrases add precision and detail to sentences.

**I Do — Prepositions Set the Scene**

A preposition shows a relationship between a noun and another part of the sentence — often location (in, on, under, beside, behind) or time (before, after, during, since). A prepositional phrase is a preposition + its noun phrase. Watch how it adds precision.

> Example: The cat slept. → The cat slept on the warm windowsill. → The cat slept on the warm windowsill throughout the afternoon.

"on the warm windowsill" = preposition (on) + noun phrase (the warm windowsill). It tells us WHERE. "throughout the afternoon" = preposition (throughout) + noun phrase (the afternoon). Tells us WHEN. I can stack them for a rich, detailed sentence.

*Tip: Prepositions are almost always followed by a noun phrase, never a verb.*

**We Do — Add the Phrase**

Add a prepositional phrase (location, time, or both) to each sentence.
- The scientist worked. → ___
- A small fire burned. → ___
- The children played. → ___

*Discussion prompt: Where is the action happening? When? Can you add both a time and a place phrase?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Expand each sentence by adding at least two prepositional phrases.
- The old man sat.
- A bird sang.
- The storm raged.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 4 sentences about a place you know well, using at least one prepositional phrase in every sentence.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
