- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Prepositional phrases: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "A preposition shows a relationship between a noun or pronoun and another element of the sentence, typically expressing location (in, on, under, beside), time (before, after, during, since), or direction (towards, through, across). A prepositional phrase consists of a preposition and its noun phrase." Students learn to identify and use prepositional phrases as a key tool for adding specific detail.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Using prepositional phrases for precision: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "expand sentences by adding adverbial and prepositional phrases to express location, time, and manner." This lesson teaches students to think of prepositional phrases as scene-setting tools — they answer the reader's question 'but where exactly? but when exactly?' — and to stack them purposefully without overloading the sentence.


#### Wednesday: Fronted adverbials to vary sentence starts

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): a fronted adverbial is an adverbial phrase placed at the start of a sentence; it requires a comma after it and provides an effective way to vary sentence openings.

**I Do — Don't Always Start with the Subject**

Most sentences start with the subject (The dog / She / The team). But you can start with an adverbial phrase instead — this adds variety and can emphasise time, place, or manner. Watch me move the adverbial to the front.

> Example: Normal: "She packed her bag quickly before sunrise."
Fronted: "Before sunrise, she packed her bag quickly."

"Before sunrise" is the fronted adverbial — I moved it to the front and added a comma after it. This is the rule: fronted adverbials need a comma after them. The time/place/manner detail now hits the reader first, setting the scene before the action.

*Tip: After writing a sentence, ask: could I move the time/place/manner phrase to the front? Try it — see if it sounds stronger.*

**We Do — Move It to the Front**

Rewrite each sentence with a fronted adverbial. Remember the comma!
- The team celebrated their victory loudly after the final whistle.
- A vast flock of starlings moved through the valley at dusk.
- She slipped out of the house quietly at midnight.

*Discussion prompt: What is the adverbial phrase? Where does the comma go? Does it change the emphasis?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence with a fronted adverbial, then write two original sentences that begin with a fronted adverbial.
- The sailors battled the storm through the night.
- He suddenly stopped walking at the edge of the cliff.
- They arrived in the village on a cold November morning.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 5 sentences. Make at least 3 of them start with a fronted adverbial.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Fronted adverbials: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "A fronted adverbial is an adverbial word, phrase, or clause placed before the subject at the start of a sentence. It is followed by a comma. Fronted adverbials can express time, place, manner, or condition and are an effective way to vary sentence openings and create emphasis." This is an explicitly named feature of Phase 2 sentence structure knowledge.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Varying sentence openers: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "vary sentence openings to avoid repetition and create effect." Fronted adverbials are the most accessible and impactful tool for this — they immediately change the rhythm of a piece and signal a more controlled, deliberate writing style. The associated comma rule gives students a clear, reliable convention to apply.


#### Thursday: Editing: adding missing detail to bare sentences

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice) & Composing (Practice): students revise bare sentences by identifying what detail is missing and adding only what the reader needs to picture the scene clearly.

**I Do — Bare Bones vs. Fully Dressed**

A bare sentence gives the fact. A dressed sentence gives the experience. When editing, I look for sentences that tell me very little and add just enough detail to make them come alive. Watch me.

> Example: "The man walked to the door." → "The elderly man shuffled to the creaking front door, pausing with his hand on the handle."

I added: WHO more specifically (elderly man), HOW (shuffled), WHICH door (creaking front door), and a bonus action (pausing with his hand). I didn't overwrite — I added what matters. The reader can now see it.

*Tip: Ask bare sentences: WHO exactly? WHAT kind? HOW exactly? WHICH one? Add only what the reader needs to picture it.*

**We Do — Dress the Sentence**

Together, let's add meaningful detail to these bare sentences. Ask WHO? WHAT kind? HOW?
- A bird flew away.
- She opened the box.
- The car stopped.

*Discussion prompt: What detail would create the most vivid picture? What detail is unnecessary?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Expand each bare sentence into a fully detailed one. Add detail that serves the story.
- The dog barked.
- Rain fell.
- He laughed.
- She read the letter.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a scene in 4 bare sentences. Then swap with a partner to dress each other's sentences up.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Revising bare sentences: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson builds a specific revision instinct: recognising a bare sentence, asking the reader's questions (who exactly? what kind? how?), and adding only the detail that genuinely improves the picture — not padding.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Creating vivid, detailed writing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader," including providing specific, concrete detail that allows the reader to visualise the scene. This lesson directly addresses one of the most common weaknesses in Years 5–6 writing — the bare, unelaborated sentence — and gives students a question framework to fix it independently.


### Week 9

#### Monday: Onomatopoeia & sound words in writing

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge) & Composing (Practice): language devices such as onomatopoeia create specific effects; students select and use sound words deliberately to make writing vivid and engaging.

**I Do — Let the Words Make Sound**

Onomatopoeia is when a word sounds like the thing it describes: crash, sizzle, whisper, gurgle. Using sound words makes writing come alive — readers don't just see the scene, they hear it. Watch me layer them in.

> Example: Without: "The kitchen was busy." → With: "Pots clattered, oil sizzled and somewhere above the noise a smoke alarm began to shriek."

I chose sounds that belong to the kitchen — clattered (pots), sizzled (oil), shriek (alarm). Each sound word tells us something is happening AND what it sounds like. I don't need to say "there was a loud noise" — the word IS the noise.

*Tip: Think: what does this place/event actually sound like? Find the word that IS the sound.*

**We Do — Listen & Write**

Let's brainstorm sound words for each setting, then build a sentence that uses at least two.
- Setting: a beach at high tide
- Setting: a forest just after rain
- Setting: a crowded school cafeteria

*Discussion prompt: What sounds would you hear? Which onomatopoeia words capture them best?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write 3 sentences, each using at least one onomatopoeia word. Set each in a different place.
- Sentence 1 — setting: a thunderstorm.
- Sentence 2 — setting: a building site.
- Sentence 3 — setting: a night in the bush.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 4-sentence paragraph about any noisy event. Use at least 4 onomatopoeia words, but don't make them feel forced.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Language devices: onomatopoeia: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Language devices such as onomatopoeia, simile, metaphor, and personification can be used deliberately to create specific effects for the reader. Onomatopoeia uses words that imitate the sounds they describe, making writing more vivid and immediate." Students learn to recognise onomatopoeia as a deliberate vocabulary choice, not just a novelty.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Using language devices for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use language devices, including onomatopoeia, to create specific effects and engage the reader." This lesson develops the skill of selecting sound words that are genuinely apt (not forced) — understanding that an effective onomatopoeia word removes the need to describe the sound separately.


#### Tuesday: Conjunctions: coordinating vs. subordinating

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): coordinating conjunctions join two independent clauses as equals; subordinating conjunctions create a dependent clause; understanding the difference helps students build compound and complex sentences with precision.

**I Do — Two Kinds of Joiners**

Coordinating conjunctions (FANBOYS: For, And, Nor, But, Or, Yet, So) join two equal, independent clauses. Subordinating conjunctions (because, although, when, if, since, while, unless…) create a dependent clause — one that can't stand alone. Watch the difference.

> Example: Coordinating: "She ran fast, but she missed the bus." | Subordinating: "She missed the bus because she ran too late."

In the coordinating example, both halves are complete sentences joined as equals with a comma + "but." In the subordinating example, "because she ran too late" can't stand alone — it depends on the main clause. The meaning shifts: one shows contrast, one shows cause.

*Tip: FANBOYS = coordinating (join equals). Because/although/when/if = subordinating (create dependence).*

**We Do — Sort & Identify**

Label each conjunction as CO (coordinating) or SUB (subordinating) and explain how it affects meaning.
- He trained every day, yet he failed to make the team.
- The game was cancelled because the field was flooded.
- Although the climb was steep, they reached the summit by noon.

*Discussion prompt: Could the underlined part stand alone? Does it show a relationship (cause/contrast/condition)?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Fill in the best conjunction and label it CO or SUB.
- She wanted to stay home, ___ the trip sounded too exciting to miss. (CO/SUB)
- They could not leave ___ the storm passed. (CO/SUB)
- The museum was old, ___ the exhibits were modern and interactive. (CO/SUB)
- ✦ Bonus: Write 4 sentences about a challenge you overcame — use two coordinating and two subordinating conjunctions.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Coordinating vs. subordinating conjunctions: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Coordinating conjunctions (for, and, nor, but, or, yet, so) join two independent clauses of equal grammatical weight. Subordinating conjunctions (because, although, when, if, since, while, unless) introduce a dependent clause that cannot stand alone. Understanding the distinction enables students to build both compound and complex sentences accurately."
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Building compound & complex sentences: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures — simple, compound, and complex — for effect and clarity." This lesson consolidates the distinction between compound sentences (coordinating conjunctions, comma before) and complex sentences (subordinating conjunctions, comma only when fronted) — a key structural knowledge milestone at Phase 2.


#### Wednesday: Paragraph cohesion – linking sentences

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge & Practice): cohesive paragraphs connect sentences using pronouns, repeated key ideas, and connectives; students revise disjointed writing to improve flow and coherence.

**I Do — Making Paragraphs Flow**

A cohesive paragraph doesn't just put sentences next to each other — it connects them. I'll show three linking techniques: (1) repeating a key word/idea, (2) using a pronoun to refer back, (3) using a connective (however, also, in addition, as a result).

> Example: Disconnected: "Honey bees are important. They pollinate flowers. Crops need pollinating. Bees are threatened." → Cohesive: "Honey bees are vital to our food system. They pollinate the flowers of fruit and vegetable crops, making growth possible. However, bee populations worldwide are in decline, threatening the very harvests they support."

"They" links back to "honey bees." "However" signals a contrast. Repeating "pollinate/pollinating" echoes the key idea. The paragraph now reads as connected thought, not a list of facts.

*Tip: After writing a paragraph, draw an arrow from each sentence to the one before it. If you can't find a link, add one.*

**We Do — Connect the Paragraph**

Rewrite this paragraph so sentences flow into each other using pronouns, connectives, and repeated ideas.
- Coral reefs are important ecosystems. Coral reefs have high biodiversity. Many species live in coral reefs. Coral reefs are threatened by warming oceans. Warming oceans cause coral bleaching. Coral bleaching is very damaging.

*Discussion prompt: Which sentences can be linked with a pronoun? Where would "however" or "as a result" work?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this disjointed paragraph, using at least three different linking techniques.
- The ancient library was discovered last year. The library was buried underground. Archaeologists excavated the library. The library contained thousands of scrolls. The scrolls were written in three languages. The scrolls are being studied by experts.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a cohesive 5-sentence paragraph about a natural phenomenon, using at least one of each linking technique.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge)* — Cohesion devices within paragraphs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge): "Cohesion is created within and across paragraphs through the use of pronouns to refer back to nouns, repeated key words and ideas to maintain focus, and connectives and linking adverbs (however, therefore, as a result) to signal relationships between ideas." Students at Years 5–6 are expected to understand these devices and apply them deliberately.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Creating cohesive paragraphs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "use cohesive devices — including pronouns, synonyms, and connectives — to link ideas within and across sentences and paragraphs." This lesson builds the skill of reading a paragraph as a connected whole rather than a list of sentences — and teaches students to add the linking tissue that makes their writing flow.


#### Thursday: Editing: identifying and removing repetition

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice) & Vocabulary (Practice): students revise writing to eliminate unnecessary word repetition, using synonyms, pronouns, and sentence restructuring to create variety and impact.

**I Do — Same Word, Again and Again**

Repetition drains the energy from writing. When a writer repeats the same word in close proximity, it usually means there's a more precise or interesting word available. Watch me identify and fix it.

> Example: "The game was exciting. It was an exciting match with exciting moments right up to the exciting end."

"exciting" appears four times in two sentences. I fix: "The game was thrilling. It was an intense match with breathtaking moments right up to the dramatic finish." Four different words, all meaning roughly the same — but now each adds its own shade of meaning.

*Tip: Use Ctrl+F (find) to search for your most-used words. If any appear more than twice in a paragraph — you have a repetition problem.*

**We Do — Spot & Replace**

Find the repeated words in this passage and replace them with synonyms or restructure the sentence.
- The team walked to the field. The team warmed up on the field. The team captain looked at the other team. The team captain knew the other team was strong but the team was ready.

*Discussion prompt: How many times does "team" appear? What are the best replacements? Can you also vary the sentence structure?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this passage by eliminating unnecessary repetition. Use synonyms, pronouns, or restructuring.
- The mountain was tall. The mountain was covered in snow. The snow on the mountain was deep. The mountain had a steep path. The path up the mountain was dangerous. Climbers who climbed the mountain had to be careful.
- ✦ Bonus: Pull out a paragraph from your writing. Highlight every word that appears more than twice. Revise.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing for repetition: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." Eliminating word repetition is a core revision skill that requires students to read their writing through the reader's eyes — noticing patterns they've become blind to as the writer.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Synonyms & pronoun substitution: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose," and to "use cohesive devices including pronouns and synonyms." This lesson directly applies those two strategies in the context of revision — replacing repeated nouns with pronouns and synonyms to create variety, precision, and flow in their writing.


### Week 10

#### Monday: Review: punctuation marks covered in Term 1

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge & Practice): review and consolidate all punctuation covered in Term 1 — full stops, commas, apostrophes, question marks, exclamation marks, colons, and semicolons.

**I Do — The Term 1 Punctuation Toolkit**

Let's do a full review of every punctuation mark we've learned this term. I'll show each one in action and remind you of the key rule.

> Example: Full stop . | Comma , | Apostrophe ' | Question mark ? | Exclamation mark ! | Colon : | Semicolon ;

Each mark has a job: full stop ends sentences. Comma separates list items and follows fronted adverbials. Apostrophe shows possession or contraction. ? asks a question. ! shows strong emotion. Colon introduces. Semicolon joins two related sentences.

*Tip: For each mark, ask: what job is this doing in this sentence? If it's not doing a job — remove it.*

**We Do — Punctuation Challenge**

Add the correct punctuation to each sentence and name the rule.
- Before the game began the captain gathered the team for a final talk
- She packed the following items a torch a sleeping bag warm clothes and extra food
- My brothers bike was left in the rain it was completely rusted by morning

*Discussion prompt: Which marks are needed and why? Could any sentence use more than one type?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add all missing punctuation to this passage. Name every rule you applied.
- at the end of last term our class held a debate the topic was should schools have longer lunch breaks three teams argued for three argued against some students made excellent points however others needed more evidence after the debate mrs kahu said that everyone had improved
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 6 sentences that uses every Term 1 punctuation mark at least once. Label each one.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Term 1 punctuation review: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): Students consolidate their knowledge of all punctuation covered in Term 1: sentence-end punctuation (. ? !), commas in lists and after fronted adverbials, apostrophes for possession and contraction, and introductory colons and semicolons. Review ensures students can identify the function of each mark, not just recognise its shape.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying punctuation accurately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect." This consolidation lesson asks students to apply all Term 1 punctuation conventions in context — naming the rule behind each choice — building the metacognitive awareness needed to make punctuation decisions independently in their own writing.


#### Tuesday: Review: grammar concepts from Term 1

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): review and consolidate all word classes and grammar concepts from Term 1 — nouns, verbs, adjectives, adverbs, pronouns, conjunctions, and subject-verb agreement.

**I Do — Grammar Gallery Tour**

Let's revisit every grammar concept from Term 1. I'll use a "grammar gallery" — one concept per station with a mini-example.

> Example: Stations: Nouns & proper nouns → Verbs (action/state) → Subject-verb agreement → Adjectives & comparison → Adverbs (how/when/where) → Pronouns → Conjunctions (CO & SUB)

Station 1: Nouns — "The explorer found a map." (nouns: explorer, map). Station 2: Proper noun — "She found it near Lake Taupo." Station 3: SVA — "Each explorer has their role." Station 4: Adjectives — "a tattered, ancient map." ...and so on through each concept.

*Tip: Grammar is about understanding why a sentence works, not just labelling. Always connect the rule to the effect on meaning.*

**We Do — Grammar Challenge Quiz**

I'll call out a sentence. You identify the grammar feature I'm asking about. We'll go through all Term 1 concepts.
- "The exhausted climbers finally reached Base Camp." → Find: noun, adjective, adverb.
- "Neither of the answers is correct." → Identify: subject, verb. Is SVA correct?
- "Because the trail was icy, the rescue team moved slowly." → Label: conjunction type, main clause, subordinate clause.

*Discussion prompt: Hands up for each one — can anyone give a different example using the same grammar feature?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Complete the grammar review tasks.
- Write a sentence containing: a proper noun, an action verb, and a fronted adverbial.
- Write a compound sentence using "but" and a complex sentence using "although."
- Write a sentence with a comparative and one with a superlative adjective.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 6 sentences that demonstrates at least 5 different Term 1 grammar concepts. Label them in the margin.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Term 1 grammar review: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): Students consolidate their understanding of all word classes taught in Term 1: nouns (common, proper, collective, abstract), verbs (action, state, tense), adjectives (comparative, superlative), adverbs (manner, time, place), pronouns (personal, possessive), and conjunctions (coordinating, subordinating). They also review subject-verb agreement across a range of sentence types.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Applying grammar knowledge in sentences: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write grammatically correct sentences using a range of word classes and structures." This review lesson asks students to demonstrate that knowledge by combining multiple features in single sentences — moving from isolated recognition to integrated, purposeful use.


#### Wednesday: Review: sentence types practised in Term 1

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): review and apply the full range of sentence structures from Term 1 — simple, expanded, compound, complex, and fronted adverbial sentences — for effect and variety.

**I Do — Our Sentence Toolkit**

This term we built quite a sentence toolkit. Let me show you every type we practised and how to combine them for maximum effect.

> Example: Simple | Simple + adjectives | Compound (FANBOYS) | Complex (subordinate clause) | Fronted adverbial | Who/where/when expanded

Simple: "The storm broke." | + adjectives: "The fierce, sudden storm broke." | Compound: "The storm broke, and the river rose rapidly." | Complex: "Although residents had been warned, few expected the storm to be this severe." | Fronted: "Just before dawn, the storm broke over the city." | Expanded: "The storm, which had been building for three days, broke over the city just before dawn."

*Tip: The best writing mixes ALL these sentence types. No single type should dominate.*

**We Do — Sentence Type Auction**

I'll read out sentences. You identify the type and explain how you know. Then we'll vote on which version of the same idea works best.
- Version A: "She won." vs Version B: "After months of training, she finally won the regional championship." vs Version C: "She won, but the victory felt hollow."
- "Despite the rain, the festival continued." — what type? What's the effect?

*Discussion prompt: Why might a writer choose Version A over B? When is shorter more powerful?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write one sentence of each type listed below, all on the same topic (the ocean).
- 1. Simple sentence.
- 2. Simple sentence with 2 adjectives.
- 3. Compound sentence (use "yet").
- 4. Complex sentence (start with "Although").
- 5. Fronted adverbial sentence.
- 6. Expanded sentence with who/where/when.
- ✦ Bonus: Arrange your 6 sentences into a mini-paragraph that flows naturally.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Sentence variety: full toolkit: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures — simple, compound, and complex — for effect and clarity," and to "vary sentence openings" to avoid repetition. This consolidation lesson asks students to demonstrate control over the full range of sentence types covered in Term 1, and to make deliberate choices about which type serves each moment in their writing.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Combining sentence types for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader," including controlling rhythm, pace, and emphasis through sentence variety. This lesson reinforces that effective writing is not about using any one sentence type — it is about choosing the right structure for the right moment.


#### Thursday: Editing: full-passage edit using Term 1 checklist

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply all Term 1 editing skills in a systematic multi-pass edit, using the full Term 1 checklist to revise a passage for accuracy, vocabulary, and structure.

**I Do — The Full Edit**

Today I'm going to do a complete multi-pass edit on this passage using our full Term 1 checklist. Watch how I tackle one aspect at a time rather than trying to fix everything at once.

> Example: Pass 1: Capitals & full stops | Pass 2: Comma usage | Pass 3: Apostrophes | Pass 4: Word choice (vague words) | Pass 5: Sentence variety | Pass 6: Repetition

I read the passage once to understand it. Then Pass 1: I check every sentence begins with a capital and ends correctly. Pass 2: I look for lists and compound sentences — are commas right? Pass 3: Any apostrophes? Are they possession or contraction? Pass 4: I highlight "nice," "good," "went," "got" — can I upgrade any? Pass 5: Are all sentences the same length? Pass 6: Any repeated words?

*Tip: Multi-pass editing catches more than single-pass. Each pass has a specific focus — you're not trying to see everything at once.*

**We Do — Class Edit**

Working through each pass together, let's edit this full passage as a class.
- last week my class went on a trip to the botanical gardens in the park. it was a nice day and everyone was excited. we walked around and saw lots of nice plants and flowers. our teacher mrs patel said that the gardens had been there for over one hundred years and that they had lots of rare plants from all over the world. we saw carnivorous plants cacti roses sunflowers and many types of ferns. the carnivorous plants were good and everyone wanted to feed them. after the trip we all wrote about our favourite plants in our science books

*Discussion prompt: Pass by pass — what are we fixing? Count the improvements at the end.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Edit this passage using all six passes of the Term 1 checklist. Rewrite the improved version.
- on friday our school had a sports day the weather was nice so everyone went outside there were lots of different events like running jumping throwing and relay races my friend noah ran really fast and he won his race we all cheered for him. i came second in the long jump which was good. at the end mrs kahu gave out certificates and everyone got a good certificate for trying hard we went home tired but happy
- ✦ Bonus: Apply the same 6-pass edit to a paragraph from your writing journal.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Multi-pass editing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson makes the revision process systematic and visible — a structured, multi-pass approach that builds the habits of a careful, independent editor. Students apply every skill from Term 1 in a single, integrated editing task.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Term 1 skills: integrated application: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): This consolidation lesson asks students to demonstrate mastery of all Term 1 writing skills in one editing task: punctuation conventions, vocabulary precision, sentence variety, and paragraph cohesion. It directly mirrors the Phase 2 expectation that students "apply writing knowledge and skills across a range of text types and purposes" with increasing independence.


---

## Term 2

### Week 1

#### Monday: Tier 2 vocabulary: words for movement & action

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge & Practice): precise verbs eliminate vague movement words; students select verbs that convey manner and quality of movement, reducing reliance on weak constructions.

**I Do — Swap the Vague Movement Verb**

Watch me swap plain movement verbs for precise ones that carry more information.

> Example: "The man went down the street." → "The man strode down the street."

Went → strode, shuffled, sprinted, lurched. Each verb tells us HOW as well as that movement happened. "Strode" tells us purposeful, confident movement. "Shuffled" tells us tired or reluctant movement. One word replaces a verb + adverb.

*Tip: Precise verbs eliminate the need for adverbs: "ran quickly" → "sprinted."*

**We Do — Upgrade the Verb**

Upgrade the underlined verbs with more precise movement words.
- The bird went through the air.
- She moved across the dance floor.
- He went into the room.

*Discussion prompt: What does the precise verb tell us that "went" does not?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Replace each plain verb with the most precise one you can think of.
- The snake went through the grass.
- The toddler moved towards the door.
- She went across the rooftops.
- ✦ Bonus: Write 4 sentences each using a different precise movement verb. No two the same.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Precise verbs for movement: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Vocabulary is chosen deliberately for effect, so students consider the precision and connotations of words." Selecting a precise movement verb (strode, shuffled, lurched) rather than a generic one (went, moved) demonstrates Tier 2 vocabulary development — a core expectation at Years 5–6. Each verb carries manner, intent, and character without requiring additional adverbs.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Selecting verbs fit for purpose: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience." This lesson builds the habit of interrogating verb choices — asking not just 'what is happening?' but 'exactly how is it happening?' — and finding the single word that answers both questions simultaneously.


#### Tuesday: Nouns: collective & abstract nouns

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): nouns include common, proper, collective, and abstract types; understanding the full range of noun classes develops students' grammatical knowledge and expands their writing vocabulary.

**I Do — Two Special Noun Types**

Collective nouns name a group. Abstract nouns name ideas, feelings, or concepts you cannot touch.

> Example: Collective: a flock of birds | Abstract: courage, freedom, justice

Flock, swarm, pride, pod — these are collective. Justice, hope, anger — these are abstract. You can't hold justice in your hand, but it's a noun because it names a concept.

*Tip: If you can't touch it or see it, but it's a thing — it's probably abstract.*

**We Do — Sort the Nouns**

Sort these nouns into collective, abstract, or concrete.
- a herd of cattle
- curiosity
- a bouquet of flowers
- loyalty
- a school of fish

*Discussion prompt: How do you know? Could you take a photo of it? Touch it?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Label each noun: collective (C), abstract (A), or concrete (Co).
- a murder of crows —
- envy —
- a packet of biscuits —
- a pride of lions —
- determination —
- ✦ Bonus: Write a sentence for each type using a noun from today's lesson.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Word classes: collective & abstract nouns: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Words belong to different classes… nouns include common, proper, collective (naming a group), and abstract (naming a concept, idea, or emotion) types." Understanding the full range of noun classes deepens grammatical awareness and expands the vocabulary available to students when composing at Years 5–6.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Using abstract nouns for depth: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience." Using abstract nouns deliberately — courage, justice, determination — is a hallmark of sophisticated writing at Phase 2, allowing students to write about ideas and values, not just concrete events.


#### Wednesday: Simple sentences: revisit with action-verb focus

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): students revisit simple sentence structure with a focus on replacing weak verb constructions (do/make/give + noun) with single powerful action verbs.

**I Do — One Strong Verb Beats Three Weak Words**

Revisiting simple sentences — but this time the focus is on choosing a powerful action verb over a weak "do + noun" construction.

> Example: Weak: "The volcano did an explosion." → Strong: "The volcano erupted."

"Do + noun" is often the weak version. Replace with a direct action verb: made a decision → decided. Gave a performance → performed. Did an investigation → investigated. Each replacement tightens the sentence and strengthens the verb.

*Tip: "Did", "made", "gave", "had" + noun = usually replaceable with one strong verb.*

**We Do — Find the Strong Verb**

Replace the weak "do + noun" construction with one strong verb.
- She made a complaint about the noise.
- They did a celebration when they won.
- He gave a performance that amazed the crowd.

*Discussion prompt: What single verb can replace each phrase?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence using one strong action verb.
- She made a decision to leave early.
- The team gave a demonstration of the new skill.
- They did an investigation into the disappearance.
- ✦ Bonus: Write 5 simple sentences using 5 different powerful action verbs.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Strong action verbs in simple sentences: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write grammatically correct sentences" and to select verbs that convey meaning precisely. Replacing weak "do/make/give + noun" constructions with single powerful action verbs is a key revision skill — it tightens sentence structure and removes redundant words simultaneously.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Precise verb selection: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience." This lesson reinforces that one exact verb (erupted, decided, investigated) is more powerful and precise than a vague verb + noun combination — a foundational principle of effective word choice at Phase 2.


#### Thursday: Proofreading: capitals for proper nouns in context

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge) & Writing Process (Practice): students apply capitalisation rules for proper nouns in complex, multi-sentence text, developing accuracy and consistency in editing.

**I Do — Proper Nouns in Longer Text**

In this term's editing focus, we look specifically at proper noun capitals in longer, more complex text.

> Example: "last tuesday, ms brown took class 5b to the otago museum in dunedin."

Fixed: "Last Tuesday, Ms Brown took Class 5B to the Otago Museum in Dunedin." Every specific name, title, day, place, and class identifier needs a capital.

*Tip: Scan specifically for: days, months, place names, people's names/titles, specific event names.*

**We Do — Capital Letter Hunt**

Find and fix all the proper noun capitalisation errors.
- on anzac day, uncle mike and aunt sarah drove to hamilton for the dawn parade at memorial park.

*Discussion prompt: List every word that needs a capital. How many did you find?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this passage with all proper noun capitals correctly placed.
- my cousin riya is visiting from tauranga next friday. she goes to otumoetai college and is in year 10. on saturday we're going to visit the national aquarium of new zealand in napier. riya wants to see the tuatara display and the kiwi house afterwards we might have lunch at the hawke's bay farmers market.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph about a real or imaginary trip. Include at least 6 proper nouns — all correctly capitalised.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Capitalisation: proper nouns in context: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Capital letters are used at the start of sentences and for all proper nouns — names of specific people, places, days, months, titles, organisations, and events." At Phase 2, students are expected to apply this convention consistently across longer, more complex text where proper nouns appear frequently and in varied contexts.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing for capitalisation accuracy: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking for correct capitalisation." This lesson trains the targeted scanning strategy — reading specifically for proper nouns — that helps students catch capitalisation errors they overlook in a general read-through.


### Week 2

#### Monday: Commas after fronted adverbials

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge & Practice): a fronted adverbial is followed by a comma; students apply this convention accurately and understand why the comma signals a pause before the main clause.

**I Do — The Opener Comma**

When a sentence starts with an adverbial phrase or clause (a fronted adverbial), we put a comma after it before the main clause begins.

> Example: "After the long match, the players collapsed on the grass." | "During the school holidays, we visited Rotorua."

"After the long match" is the fronted adverbial — it sets the scene. The comma after "match" separates it from the main clause. Without the comma, the reader rushes past the opener without pausing.

*Tip: Read aloud. If you naturally pause after the opening phrase before the subject arrives — that pause is your comma.*

**We Do — Add the Comma**

These sentences have fronted adverbials but are missing the comma. Find the opener and add the comma.
- Before the sun had risen the fishing boats left the harbour.
- On the last day of term the students performed their assembly item.
- Despite the heavy rain the match went ahead as planned.

*Discussion prompt: Where does the fronted adverbial end and the main clause begin? That's where the comma goes.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add the missing comma to each sentence and write the type of fronted adverbial (time/place/contrast).
- Throughout the long journey south the waka stayed on course.
- Although the trail was steep the tamariki kept climbing.
- By the time the tide turned the divers were already back on shore.
- ✦ Bonus: Write 5 sentences that each begin with a different type of fronted adverbial. Use at least one time, one place, and one contrast opener.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Comma after fronted adverbials: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "When an adverbial word, phrase, or clause is placed before the subject at the start of a sentence (a fronted adverbial), a comma is used to separate it from the main clause." This is a specific and frequently tested punctuation convention at Years 5–6, appearing across narrative, recount, and persuasive writing.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying fronted adverbial comma: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect." The comma after a fronted adverbial is both a convention (accuracy) and a craft tool (effect) — it signals a deliberate pause that gives the opener its weight. This lesson builds the read-aloud checking strategy: the natural pause reveals where the comma belongs.


#### Tuesday: Future tense: will & going to

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): the future tense is expressed using "will" or "going to"; the choice between them carries different shades of meaning — spontaneous decisions vs planned intentions.

**I Do — Two Ways to Talk About the Future**

English expresses future time in two main ways: "will" + verb, and "going to" + verb. They are not always interchangeable. Watch me use each one and explain the difference.

> Example: "I will help you with that." (spontaneous decision) | "We are going to plant native trees next month." (planned intention)

"Will" is used for spontaneous decisions, offers, predictions, and promises made at the moment of speaking. "Going to" is used for plans already decided and for predictions based on current evidence. "Look at those clouds — it's going to rain." (evidence right there).

*Tip: Planned ahead → going to. Decided right now → will. Evidence in front of you → going to.*

**We Do — Will or Going To?**

Choose "will" or "going to" and explain why.
- The marae committee ___ (will/is going to) hold a hui next Saturday — it's been in the calendar for months.
- "I can't carry that box." "Don't worry, I ___ (will/am going to) help you."
- Look at those storm clouds — the weather ___ (will/is going to) turn soon.

*Discussion prompt: Is this a plan, a spontaneous offer, or a prediction based on evidence?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Complete each sentence with the correct future form. Write a brief reason for your choice.
- The school ___ (will/is going to) hold a matariki celebration — we've been practising for weeks.
- "The printer is broken." "I ___ (will/am going to) fix it." (spontaneous offer)
- That kūmara plant ___ (will/is going to) need water soon — the soil is bone dry.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 5 sentences about a future event you're genuinely planning. Use "going to" for plans and "will" for predictions or promises within it.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Future tense: will & going to: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "The future tense in English is expressed using 'will' + verb (for spontaneous decisions, offers, and promises) or 'going to' + verb (for pre-planned intentions and predictions based on present evidence). Understanding the distinction allows writers to express future time with precision rather than using the two forms interchangeably."
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Using future tense accurately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write grammatically correct sentences" using a range of tenses accurately. Choosing between "will" and "going to" is a nuanced grammar decision that requires students to consider the context and intended meaning — a marker of grammatical sophistication at Years 5–6.


#### Wednesday: Compound sentences: new contexts & conjunctions

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): students use FANBOYS conjunctions in new writing contexts — recount, report, and argument — understanding that the same conjunction carries different weight depending on purpose and audience.

**I Do — FANBOYS in Fresh Contexts**

We know FANBOYS conjunctions (for, and, nor, but, or, yet, so). This week we use them in new writing contexts to see how the same conjunction does different jobs.

> Example: Recount: "We arrived at Tāmaki Makaurau, and the whole whānau was waiting." | Argument: "The policy is popular, yet the evidence does not support it."

"And" adds. "But/yet" contrasts. "So" shows result. "Or" gives alternatives. "For" gives reason (formal). "Nor" adds a negative. The conjunction you choose shapes the relationship between the two ideas.

*Tip: Before picking a conjunction, decide: am I adding, contrasting, showing a result, or giving a reason? That decides which FANBOYS fits.*

**We Do — Choose the Right FANBOYS**

Join each pair of sentences with the best FANBOYS conjunction. Add a comma before the conjunction.
- The hīkoi had been going for three days. The marchers showed no sign of stopping.
- You can present your findings as a poster. You can create a short video.
- The experiment failed the first time. The team learnt something valuable from it.

*Discussion prompt: What is the relationship between the two ideas? That decides the conjunction.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a compound sentence for each context using the conjunction given.
- Write about a kaitiakitanga project using "so."
- Write about a time something went wrong using "yet."
- Write a sentence giving someone two choices using "or."
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph about an environmental issue. Use at least three different FANBOYS conjunctions. Underline each one.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — FANBOYS in varied contexts: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Coordinating conjunctions (for, and, nor, but, or, yet, so) join two independent clauses of equal grammatical weight. Each conjunction signals a different relationship: addition (and), contrast (but/yet), result (so), alternative (or), reason (for), negative addition (nor)." Students learn that the same conjunction carries different register and weight depending on text type and context.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Compound sentences across text types: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity" across different text types. This lesson extends compound sentence use beyond narrative into recount, report, and argument — building students' ability to select the right conjunction for the relationship and context, not just the first one that fits grammatically.


#### Thursday: Editing: fixing comma splices

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge) & Writing Process (Practice): a comma splice joins two independent clauses with only a comma; students identify comma splices and correct them using a full stop, semicolon, or conjunction.

**I Do — The Comma That Tries to Do Too Much**

A comma splice is when two complete sentences are joined with just a comma. A comma is not strong enough to hold two independent clauses together on its own. Watch me find and fix one.

> Example: ✗ "The waka was ready, the crew climbed aboard." → ✓ "The waka was ready. The crew climbed aboard." OR ✓ "The waka was ready, and the crew climbed aboard." OR ✓ "The waka was ready; the crew climbed aboard."

Three fixes: (1) full stop — two separate sentences. (2) comma + conjunction — compound sentence. (3) semicolon — joins two closely related sentences. All three are correct. Choose based on the relationship between the ideas.

*Tip: Test: read each side of the comma. If both could stand alone as sentences — the comma is a splice. Fix it.*

**We Do — Fix the Splice**

Find the comma splice in each sentence and fix it three ways.
- The tide was coming in, we had to leave quickly.
- She had been training for months, the race was finally here.

*Discussion prompt: Which fix sounds best for each sentence? Does the relationship between the ideas affect your choice?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Fix each comma splice using the method shown in brackets.
- The kākāpō is critically endangered, conservation efforts have helped increase numbers. [semicolon]
- The hail hammered the roof, everyone moved away from the windows. [conjunction]
- She had practised the speech a hundred times, she still felt her hands shake. [full stop]
- ✦ Bonus: Write 3 pairs of sentences — then deliberately create a comma splice, and fix it in two different ways each time.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Comma splices: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "A comma splice occurs when two independent clauses are joined with only a comma, which is insufficient punctuation. It can be corrected by: (1) replacing the comma with a full stop; (2) adding a coordinating conjunction after the comma; or (3) replacing the comma with a semicolon." Understanding comma splices deepens students' knowledge of sentence boundaries and punctuation function.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Identifying and correcting comma splices: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate." Recognising and fixing comma splices is a targeted editing skill that requires students to identify sentence boundaries and select the punctuation or conjunction that best expresses the relationship between the two clauses.


### Week 3

#### Monday: Antonyms & shades of meaning

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): antonyms are words with opposite meanings; understanding the spectrum between antonyms develops nuanced vocabulary and helps students select words with the right shade of meaning.

**I Do — The Meaning Spectrum**

Antonyms are opposites — but between most opposites there's a whole spectrum of words. Understanding that spectrum lets you choose exactly the right word for your meaning.

> Example: hot ←——————————————→ cold
scalding / burning / warm / tepid / cool / chilly / freezing / arctic

"Hot" and "cold" are antonyms, but the spectrum between them has eight gradations. "The water was cold" is vague. "The water was arctic" is precise. Choosing from the spectrum — rather than defaulting to the antonym — gives writing its precision and texture.

*Tip: Before writing a describing word, ask: where on the spectrum between the two extremes does this fall? Then find the word that sits exactly there.*

**We Do — Build the Spectrum**

Build meaning spectrums for each pair of antonyms. Aim for at least five words between the two extremes.
- happy ←—————————→ sad
- loud ←—————————→ quiet
- fast ←—————————→ slow

*Discussion prompt: Which word on the spectrum best describes: a library, a sports crowd, a funeral? Why?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Choose the word from the spectrum that best fits each context. Explain your choice.
- The old man's voice was ___ (choose from: booming / loud / clear / soft / faint / inaudible) as he told the story.
- After the marathon, the runner felt ___ (choose from: exhausted / tired / weary / drained / spent / destroyed).
- The stream moved ___ (choose from: rushing / flowing / trickling / seeping / still) through the valley.
- ✦ Bonus: Choose one antonym pair. Build a spectrum of 8 words. Then use three of them in three separate sentences about the same setting.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Antonyms & meaning spectrums: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "The meaning of words can be extended or changed by context; vocabulary is chosen deliberately for effect, so students consider the connotations and precision of words." Understanding antonyms and the spectrum of meaning between them is a key component of Tier 2 vocabulary development — it moves students beyond binary word choice (hot/cold) to nuanced selection along a continuum.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Selecting words for precision: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases, including subject-specific vocabulary, that are fit for purpose and audience" and to "consider the effect of word choice on the reader." This lesson develops the habit of asking 'exactly where on the spectrum does this fall?' before committing to a word — the key discipline of precise vocabulary selection.


#### Tuesday: Subject-verb agreement: tricky cases (everyone, each)

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): indefinite pronouns such as everyone, each, neither, and someone are grammatically singular and require singular verb forms, even when they refer to multiple people.

**I Do — The Tricky Singular Words**

Most subject-verb agreement is straightforward. But some words look plural and feel plural yet are grammatically singular.

> Example: "Everyone in the two classes was invited." (not "were") | "Each of the players has a number." (not "have")

"Everyone," "everybody," "someone," "nobody," "each," "either," "neither" — all singular, even when they refer to many people. The trick: mentally replace with "every single person" — then the singular verb feels natural.

*Tip: Everyone/each/either/neither/nobody = singular. Replace with "every single one" in your head — the right verb follows naturally.*

**We Do — Choose the Right Verb**

Choose the correct verb form for each sentence.
- Everyone in the kapa haka group ___ (know/knows) the words.
- Each of the entries ___ (was/were) judged on three criteria.
- Neither of the referees ___ (was/were) happy with the decision.

*Discussion prompt: Replace the subject with "every single one" — which verb sounds right now?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Correct the subject-verb agreement error in each sentence.
- Everyone in the syndicate have submitted their work.
- Each of the kūmara plants were carefully watered.
- Neither of the answers are correct.
- Somebody have left their bag on the mat.
- ✦ Bonus: Write 5 sentences, each using one of these subjects: everyone, each, neither, somebody, anyone. Make sure agreement is correct in every one.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — SVA: indefinite pronouns: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Indefinite pronouns (everyone, everybody, someone, nobody, each, either, neither) are grammatically singular and require singular verb forms, even when they refer to multiple people or things. Errors in agreement with these pronouns are among the most common grammar mistakes at Years 5–6." Students learn to override the "feels plural" instinct by testing with "every single one."
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing for tricky SVA: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate." Identifying and correcting tricky subject-verb agreement errors — particularly with indefinite pronouns — requires students to understand the grammatical subject of the clause, not just the nearest noun, and is a key editing skill at Phase 2.


#### Wednesday: Expanding sentences using "when" and "because"

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): students expand simple sentences by adding subordinate clauses using "when" and "because" to show time relationships and causation.

**I Do — Add the Why and the When**

Two of the most useful subordinating conjunctions for expanding sentences are "when" (time) and "because" (cause/reason). Watch me use them to transform bare sentences.

> Example: Base: "The crowd cheered." → + when: "The crowd cheered when the final whistle blew." → + because: "The crowd cheered because the home team had just equalised."

"When" tells us the time — the moment the action happened. "Because" tells us the cause — why it happened. Each adds a whole new layer of meaning to the same simple sentence. I can also put the "when/because" clause first: "When the final whistle blew, the crowd cheered."

*Tip: "When" answers: at what moment? "Because" answers: for what reason? Add the one your sentence is missing.*

**We Do — Expand Together**

Expand each sentence by adding a "when" clause AND a "because" clause. Try both.
- The pohutukawa bloomed.
- The students fell silent.
- The explorer turned back.

*Discussion prompt: Which expansion is more interesting for each sentence — the when or the because? Why?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Expand each sentence with the conjunction shown. Try putting the clause at the start AND at the end.
- The river flooded. [because]
- The kiwi came out. [when]
- She changed her mind. [because]
- The lights went out. [when]
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 5 sentences. Use "when" in at least two and "because" in at least two. Vary whether the clause comes first or last.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Expanding with "when" & "because": NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "expand simple sentences by adding words, phrases, and clauses to add detail and description," including using subordinating conjunctions to express time (when) and cause (because). This lesson revisits and deepens Term 1 complex sentence work in a fresh context, reinforcing that clause order is flexible and that the fronted version requires a comma.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Subordinating conjunctions: time & cause: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "'When' is a subordinating conjunction that introduces a clause expressing time; 'because' introduces a clause expressing cause or reason. Both create complex sentences where one clause depends on the other for full meaning." Understanding the semantic difference between these two conjunctions helps students choose the right one for the relationship they want to express.


#### Thursday: Editing: upgrading bland verbs in a passage

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Practice) & Writing Process (Practice): students identify weak, overused verbs in a passage and replace them with precise verbs that carry meaning, character, and energy.

**I Do — Bland Verbs Drain Energy**

Some verbs are so vague they tell us almost nothing: went, got, said, did, made, was. Watch me identify them in a passage and replace them with verbs that do real work.

> Example: "The children went to the beach. They got into the water. Dad said they should come out."

"went" → "raced" | "got" → "plunged" | "said" → "bellowed". New version: "The children raced to the beach. They plunged into the water. Dad bellowed at them to come out." The scene comes alive.

*Tip: Highlight every "went," "got," "said," "did," "made," "was" in a passage. Each one is an upgrade opportunity.*

**We Do — Upgrade the Passage**

Replace every underlined bland verb with a more precise one. The new verb must match the context.
- The kererū went from branch to branch, getting the ripest berries. It made a loud noise with its wings as it went away.

*Discussion prompt: What is actually happening physically? What does a precise verb tell the reader that "went" and "got" don't?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this passage, upgrading every bland verb you can find.
- On field trip day, the class got on the bus early. They went to the wetland reserve near Ōtaki. The guide said something about the different birds. Some students went into the hide and got photos of a bittern. Everyone said it was great.
- ✦ Bonus: Count how many bland verbs you replaced. Write the before and after side by side. Which passage is more engaging and why?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Upgrading bland verbs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience" and to "consider the effect of word choice on the reader." This lesson targets the most common verb weakness in Years 5–6 writing — overuse of generic verbs (went, got, said) — and builds the habit of asking 'what is ACTUALLY happening?' before selecting a more precise alternative.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Verb audit in revision: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." The verb audit — highlighting every weak verb and asking if it can be upgraded — is a targeted revision strategy that directly improves the energy and precision of students' writing at Phase 2.


### Week 4

#### Monday: Apostrophes for contraction

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): a contraction apostrophe replaces omitted letters when two words are joined; students apply contraction apostrophes accurately and distinguish them from possessive apostrophes.

**I Do — The Apostrophe That Replaces Letters**

A contraction apostrophe shows where one or more letters have been removed when two words are joined together.

> Example: "do not" → "don't" (the apostrophe replaces the 'o') | "they are" → "they're" | "I would" → "I'd"

"cannot" → "can't" — the apostrophe replaces "no". "would not" → "wouldn't" — replaces "o". "she is" → "she's" — replaces "i". The apostrophe sits exactly where the missing letters were.

*Tip: The apostrophe goes WHERE THE LETTERS WERE REMOVED — not just anywhere between the words.*

**We Do — Contract and Expand**

Write the contraction for each pair of words, and expand each contraction back to the full form.
- we will → ___  |  it's → ___
- have not → ___  |  they'd → ___
- I am → ___  |  couldn't → ___

*Discussion prompt: Exactly which letters did the apostrophe replace? Could you be confused between "it's" (it is) and "its" (belonging to it)?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Fix the apostrophe errors in these sentences — some are missing, some are in the wrong place.
- Sh'es not sure if theyre coming to the hangi tonight.
- I wo'nt be ready until the bus ha's already left.
- Weve been waiting here for ages — it isnt fair.
- They're dog isnt allowed inside but its a cold night.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a conversation (6 lines of dialogue) between two friends making plans. Use at least 6 different contractions correctly.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Contraction apostrophes: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "A contraction apostrophe replaces the letter or letters omitted when two words are combined (e.g. do not → don't, they are → they're, I would → I'd). The apostrophe is placed exactly where the letters were removed." Students also practise distinguishing contraction apostrophes from possessive apostrophes — a critical and frequently confused distinction at Years 5–6.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying contraction apostrophes accurately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect," including apostrophes for contraction. The key test — expanding the contraction to check whether the apostrophe placement makes sense — gives students a reliable self-checking strategy they can apply independently when editing their own writing.


#### Tuesday: Relative pronouns: who, which, that

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): relative pronouns (who, which, that) introduce relative clauses that give additional information about a noun; the choice of pronoun and use of commas depends on whether the clause defines or adds extra information.

**I Do — Clauses That Give Extra Information**

A relative clause is a type of subordinate clause that gives more information about a noun. It's introduced by a relative pronoun: "who" (for people), "which" (for things), "that" (for people or things in defining clauses).

> Example: "The woman who won the award is our neighbour." | "The bridge, which was built in 1902, is being restored." | "The book that she recommended was brilliant."

"who" → refers back to a person. "which" → refers back to a thing, adds non-essential info (use commas): "The river, which floods every winter, is rising again." "that" → defines which one (no commas): "The path that leads to the pā is steep."

*Tip: "Who" = people. "Which" = things + commas (extra info). "That" = defines which one, no commas.*

**We Do — Choose the Right Relative Pronoun**

Complete each sentence with who, which, or that.
- The rangatira ___ led the iwi was known for her wisdom.
- The old pōhutukawa, ___ had stood for 300 years, was finally protected.
- This is the exact track ___ leads to the summit.

*Discussion prompt: Does the clause define which one, or add extra information? Does it refer to a person or a thing?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add a relative clause to each sentence using the pronoun given.
- The scientist ___ (who) discovered the new species published her findings last month.
- Rotorua, ___ (which) is famous for its geothermal activity, attracts tourists year-round.
- She finally found the notebook ___ (that) she had been searching for all morning.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph about a local place or person. Include at least three relative clauses — one with who, one with which (with commas), and one with that.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Relative pronouns & relative clauses: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Relative pronouns (who, which, that) introduce relative clauses that modify a noun. 'Who' refers to people; 'which' refers to things and introduces non-defining (parenthetical) clauses set off with commas; 'that' introduces defining clauses with no commas." Mastering this distinction is a key grammar milestone at Phase 2, with direct implications for comma use.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Using relative clauses for detail: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "expand sentences by adding clauses to add detail and description." Relative clauses are a sophisticated tool for embedding additional information about a noun within a sentence — avoiding the need for a separate sentence and creating more fluent, complex writing at Years 5–6.


#### Wednesday: Topic sentences for persuasive paragraphs

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge & Practice): persuasive topic sentences state a clear position or claim; students write topic sentences that introduce the paragraph argument and signal the writer's stance.

**I Do — Topic Sentences That Argue**

In Term 1 we wrote topic sentences for informational paragraphs. This week, topic sentences carry an opinion or argument — they tell the reader not just what the paragraph is about, but what position you're taking.

> Example: Informational: "Recycling reduces waste sent to landfill." | Persuasive: "Recycling is not enough — we must stop producing unnecessary plastic altogether."

The persuasive topic sentence does three jobs: (1) introduces the paragraph's main idea, (2) makes the writer's position clear, (3) makes the reader want to know the evidence. "Everyone deserves access to clean water" is both a topic sentence AND an argument.

*Tip: A strong persuasive topic sentence takes a clear position. If someone could disagree with it — it's persuasive. If it's just a fact — it's informational.*

**We Do — Upgrade to Persuasive**

Turn each informational topic sentence into a persuasive one by adding a position or claim.
- Smartphones are used by many young people.
- Schools have canteens that sell food to students.
- Exercise has effects on health.

*Discussion prompt: What position could a writer take on each topic? Add the opinion to make it genuinely persuasive.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a persuasive topic sentence for each argument prompt.
- Topic: whether students should have homework.
- Topic: whether animals should be kept in zoos.
- Topic: whether Aotearoa should have a four-day school week.
- ✦ Bonus: Choose one topic and write the full paragraph — topic sentence + at least two pieces of evidence or reasoning.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge)* — Persuasive topic sentences: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge): "In persuasive writing, each paragraph is organised around a claim or position introduced in the topic sentence. A persuasive topic sentence makes the writer's stance clear and invites the reader to consider the supporting evidence that follows." Students learn that a topic sentence in argument writing must be contestable — if no one could disagree with it, it is not yet persuasive.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Structuring persuasive paragraphs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "organise ideas into paragraphs with a clear main idea, supported by relevant detail" across a range of text types including persuasion. This lesson builds the skill of leading with a clear position before providing evidence — a structural discipline that is foundational to effective persuasive writing at Phase 2.


#### Thursday: Editing: removing unnecessary apostrophes

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge) & Writing Process (Practice): apostrophes are not used to form plurals; students identify and remove apostrophes used incorrectly in plural nouns.

**I Do — The Apostrophe That Shouldn't Be There**

One of the most common apostrophe errors is using one to make a word plural. Apostrophes are NEVER used for plurals — only for possession and contraction. Watch me find and fix the errors.

> Example: ✗ "We have three cat's and two dog's." → ✓ "We have three cats and two dogs."

"Cat's" — is the cat possessing something? No. Is it a contraction? No. So no apostrophe! "Dog's" — same test, same result. The simple rule: to make a word plural, just add -s or -es. Never add an apostrophe.

*Tip: Before adding an apostrophe, ask: is this possession or contraction? If neither — no apostrophe.*

**We Do — Find and Remove**

Remove every incorrect apostrophe from this passage.
- At the market we bought apple's, pear's, and two kind's of cheese. The stall's were busy all morning and the seller's were friendly. By noon, all the basket's were empty.

*Discussion prompt: Which apostrophes are wrong? How do you know? What test did you apply?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence, correcting all apostrophe errors. Some sentences may have no error.
- The team's jerseys were hanging on hook's in the changing room.
- Both runner's crossed the line within second's of each other.
- The children's laughter echoed across the playground.
- Three dog's barked at the cat's on the fence.
- ✦ Bonus: Write 5 sentences that correctly use apostrophes for possession, contraction, and plural — making sure plurals never have apostrophes.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Apostrophes: not for plurals: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Apostrophes are used to show possession and to mark omitted letters in contractions. They are never used to form plurals. The 'greengrocer's apostrophe' (apple's, pear's) is one of the most common punctuation errors in English." Students develop the two-question test: Is this possession? Is this a contraction? If neither — no apostrophe.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing for apostrophe errors: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking punctuation." This lesson builds a targeted apostrophe audit: applying the possession/contraction test to every apostrophe in a passage and removing those that fail both tests — a practical and reliable editing strategy.


### Week 5

#### Monday: Brackets & dashes for parenthetical information

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): brackets and dashes are used to add parenthetical (non-essential) information within a sentence; students choose between them based on the degree of emphasis and formality required.

**I Do — The Aside in a Sentence**

Sometimes we want to add extra information inside a sentence without breaking its flow. Brackets and dashes both do this job — but with different emphasis. Watch me use both.

> Example: Brackets: "The tūī (a native honeyeater) feeds on kōwhai nectar." | Dashes: "The tūī — our boldest garden visitor — feeds on kōwhai nectar."

Brackets add a quiet aside — the reader can almost skip it. Dashes shout for attention — they make the parenthetical feel dramatic or important. Use brackets for factual clarifications. Use dashes when you want the reader to pause and notice.

*Tip: Remove the parenthetical — does the sentence still make sense? Good — it's truly parenthetical. Now choose: quiet (brackets) or dramatic (dashes)?*

**We Do — Choose the Right Mark**

For each sentence, decide whether brackets or dashes are more appropriate. Add them correctly.
- The pōhutukawa also called the New Zealand Christmas tree blooms in December.
- The final score 14 to 12 in our favour was the best result of the season.
- Captain Cook who arrived in 1769 was the first European to chart New Zealand's coastline.

*Discussion prompt: Is this extra information quiet and factual, or dramatic and emphatic? That decides the mark.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add brackets or dashes to each sentence. Write a reason for your choice.
- The Whanganui River legally recognised as a person in 2017 is sacred to the Māori people.
- My brother the one who never reads finished the whole novel in a weekend.
- Mount Taranaki also known as Egmont is one of New Zealand's most symmetrical volcanoes.
- ✦ Bonus: Write 4 sentences of your own — two using brackets and two using dashes. Make sure the parenthetical is genuinely non-essential in each.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Brackets & dashes for parentheticals: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Brackets and dashes are used to add parenthetical (non-essential) information within a sentence. Brackets indicate quiet, factual asides; dashes create emphasis and draw attention to the additional information. Both marks come in pairs, and the sentence must make grammatical sense if the parenthetical is removed." This is an explicitly expected Phase 2 punctuation skill.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Using parenthetical punctuation for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect." Choosing between brackets and dashes is a purposeful punctuation decision — not a random one. This lesson teaches students that punctuation shapes the reader's experience of additional information: brackets whisper, dashes shout. That awareness is the foundation of punctuation as a craft tool.


#### Tuesday: Comparative & superlative adjectives

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): comparative adjectives compare two things; superlative adjectives compare three or more; students apply the rules for regular and irregular forms accurately.

**I Do — More, Most, -er, -est**

Comparative adjectives compare two things. Superlative adjectives compare three or more. Short adjectives add -er/-est; longer adjectives use more/most; some are irregular.

> Example: fast → faster → fastest | beautiful → more beautiful → most beautiful | good → better → best

Rule: 1–2 syllable adjectives → -er/-est: tall/taller/tallest. 3+ syllable adjectives → more/most: exciting/more exciting/most exciting. Exceptions: good/better/best, bad/worse/worst, far/further/furthest. Test: if "-er" sounds natural — use it.

*Tip: Try adding -er: if it sounds natural, use it. If it sounds awkward ("intelligenter?"), use "more."*

**We Do — Form the Comparatives**

Form the comparative and superlative of each adjective and use each in a sentence.
- cold → ___ → ___
- dangerous → ___ → ___
- good → ___ → ___

*Discussion prompt: Which rule applies? Are there any exceptions here? Can you use each in an example sentence?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Complete the comparison chart and write sentences using the correct form.
- happy → ___ → ___  |  Use "happiest" in a sentence.
- mysterious → ___ → ___  |  Use "more mysterious" in a sentence.
- bad → ___ → ___  |  Use "worst" in a sentence.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph comparing two animals, using at least four comparative or superlative adjectives.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Comparative & superlative adjectives: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Adjectives can be modified to express degrees of comparison. Comparative forms (using -er or 'more') compare two things; superlative forms (using -est or 'most') compare three or more. Some adjectives have irregular comparative and superlative forms (good/better/best, bad/worse/worst)." Students learn and apply both the rules and the exceptions.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Precise comparative language: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience." Using precise comparative and superlative forms — rather than vague modifiers ("more good," "more bad") — is a key marker of vocabulary accuracy at Years 5–6. This lesson also reinforces that form choice follows a rule, not guesswork.


#### Wednesday: Combining short sentences for variety

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): students combine multiple short sentences into smoother compound and complex sentences, eliminating choppiness and creating better flow.

**I Do — Stitch the Choppy Sentences**
