Too many short sentences in a row creates choppy, monotonous writing. Watch me combine short sentences into longer, smoother ones using conjunctions, relative clauses, and participial phrases.

> Example: Choppy: "The kererū landed. It ate the berries. It flew away. It was very fast." → Smooth: "The kererū landed, ate the berries, and flew away with surprising speed."

I combined four sentences into one by removing repeated subjects and joining the actions with commas and "and." I also replaced "very fast" with "surprising speed" — more precise and vivid. The rhythm is now smooth rather than clipped.

*Tip: If three or more sentences in a row are short and start with the same subject — they can probably be combined.*

**We Do — Combine Together**

Combine each group of short sentences into one or two smooth sentences.
- The storm arrived quickly. The wind was strong. Trees bent. Branches snapped.
- She practised every day. She was nervous. The performance was coming soon.

*Discussion prompt: Which sentences share a subject and can be combined? Which conjunction or relative clause best links the ideas?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Combine each group into one well-constructed sentence.
- The kaiako walked in. She smiled. She placed a pile of books on the desk. She waited.
- The volcano was silent. It had been silent for years. Scientists were watching it. They were worried.
- The dog sat by the door. It was whining. It wanted to go outside. It had been waiting for an hour.
- ✦ Bonus: Find a paragraph in your own writing that has more than three consecutive short sentences. Combine at least two pairs.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Combining sentences for flow: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity," including combining short sentences to improve rhythm and flow. Eliminating choppy repetition by merging sentences with conjunctions, relative clauses, and shared verb phrases is a key revision skill at Years 5–6 — it shows control over sentence construction, not just sentence production.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Revising for sentence variety: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson practises a specific revision strategy: identifying runs of short sentences, diagnosing why they feel choppy, and selecting the best combining method — conjunction, relative clause, or shared verb chain.


#### Thursday: Peer editing: punctuation variety

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply a punctuation variety checklist to their own and a partner's writing, giving and receiving specific feedback on comma, colon, semicolon, and dash use.

**I Do — Reading for Punctuation Variety**

Today I'm going to show you how to read a piece of writing specifically to assess its punctuation variety — not just accuracy.

> Example: Checklist: ☐ At least one comma after a fronted adverbial ☐ At least one colon ☐ At least one semicolon ☐ At least one pair of brackets or dashes ☐ No comma splices

I read the passage once for meaning. Then I read again specifically for punctuation. I mark each type I find. I notice what's missing. Then I give specific feedback: "You've used commas well in lists, but there are no semicolons — try joining two of these related sentences with one."

*Tip: Specific feedback is helpful. "Your punctuation is good" is not. "You need a comma after your fronted adverbial on line 2" is.*

**We Do — Punctuation Variety Audit**

Let's audit this passage together for punctuation variety.
- The science fair was held on Thursday. Every class had an entry. Our class made a working volcano. It erupted three times. The judges were impressed. We won first place. It was a good day.

*Discussion prompt: Where could a semicolon replace a full stop? Where could a dash add drama? Is there a list that needs a colon?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Swap a piece of your writing with a partner. Edit each other's work for punctuation variety using the checklist.
- ✦ Checklist: At least one semicolon used correctly.
- ✦ Checklist: At least one colon introducing a list or explanation.
- ✦ Checklist: At least one dash or pair of brackets for parenthetical information.
- ✦ Bonus: Rewrite your partner's weakest paragraph — the one with the least punctuation variety — as a model of what it could look like.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Peer editing for punctuation variety: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "share writing with others and respond to feedback to improve their work." Peer editing for punctuation variety is a specific application of this expectation — students give targeted, rule-based feedback (not just "it's good") and practise reading writing through an editor's eye rather than a writer's eye.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying a punctuation checklist: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect." This lesson consolidates all Term 2 punctuation learning — commas after fronted adverbials, colons, semicolons, brackets, and dashes — in a peer editing context where students must both recognise correct use and explain the rule behind each mark.


### Week 6

#### Monday: Greek & Latin roots in everyday words

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): many English words are built from Greek and Latin roots; understanding common roots enables students to decode unfamiliar words and expand their vocabulary systematically.

**I Do — Crack the Code**

Many English words are built from Greek and Latin roots. Once you know a root, you can work out the meaning of dozens of words you've never seen before.

> Example: "tele" (far) → telescope, television, telepathy | "aqua" (water) → aquatic, aquarium, aqueduct | "bio" (life) → biology, biography, biodiversity

If you see "submarine," you might not know it — but "sub" means under and "marine" means sea. So submarine = under the sea. Same with "microphone" — "micro" (small) + "phone" (sound) = small sound amplifier. Roots unlock words like a key.

*Tip: When you hit an unknown word, look for a root you recognise. Even a partial match gives you a clue.*

**We Do — Decode Together**

Use the roots provided to work out what each word means. Then check by looking at the context sentence.
- "geo" (earth): geology, geography, geothermal — what does "geothermal" likely mean?
- "script/scribe" (write): manuscript, inscription, transcribe — what does "transcribe" likely mean?
- "spec/spect" (look/see): inspect, spectator, spectacular — what does "spectator" likely mean?

*Discussion prompt: Can you find other words that share these roots? How does knowing the root help you read unfamiliar texts?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Use your knowledge of roots to answer each question.
- The root "port" means carry. What do these words mean: export, import, transport, portfolio?
- The root "vis/vid" means see. Use it to work out: visible, invisible, vision, evidence.
- The root "terra" means land/earth. What do: terrain, territory, terrarium, extraterrestrial mean?
- ✦ Bonus: Find 5 words in a book, magazine, or website that share a Greek or Latin root. List the root, its meaning, and the words that use it.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Greek & Latin roots: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Many English words are built from Latin and Greek roots, prefixes, and suffixes. Understanding the meaning of word parts enables students to work out the meaning of unfamiliar words and to build their vocabulary systematically." This lesson develops morphological awareness — knowing that "geo" means earth unlocks geology, geography, geothermal, and geocentric simultaneously.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Using root knowledge in writing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "use knowledge of word origins, roots, and word families to extend vocabulary." Understanding that "port" means carry (import, export, transport) gives students a transferable tool — they can both decode unfamiliar words when reading and select more precise vocabulary when writing.


#### Tuesday: Adverbial phrases for time & manner

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): adverbial phrases extend single adverbs by adding precision and texture; students replace weak single adverbs with richer adverbial phrases that tell the reader more.

**I Do — Beyond Single Adverbs**

A single adverb tells us how or when. An adverbial PHRASE does the same job but with more precision and colour.

> Example: "She spoke quietly." → "She spoke in a hushed, urgent whisper." | "He left quickly." → "He left before anyone could ask questions."

"Quietly" tells us the manner. "In a hushed, urgent whisper" tells us manner AND gives texture — we can picture it. "Before anyone could ask questions" tells us time AND hints at motivation. Adverbial phrases carry more weight than single adverbs.

*Tip: Spot a lone adverb. Ask: can I replace it with a phrase that tells the reader more? If yes — upgrade it.*

**We Do — Upgrade Together**

Replace each underlined adverb with an adverbial phrase that adds more information.
- The tūī sang loudly in the pōhutukawa.
- The tamariki waited nervously for the results.
- The boat left early.

*Discussion prompt: What kind of adverbial phrase fits — one of manner, time, or place? Can you add detail and stay true to the context?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence by replacing the adverb in brackets with a precise adverbial phrase.
- The kaitiaki worked [hard] to restore the wetland.
- The team celebrated [happily] after the final whistle.
- She finished her speech [suddenly].
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 5 sentences about an event or journey. Use at least three different adverbial phrases — one of time, one of manner, one of place.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Adverbial phrases for precision: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "expand sentences by adding adverbial and prepositional phrases to express location, time, and manner." This lesson advances from single adverbs to adverbial phrases — teaching students that a phrase ("in a hushed, urgent whisper") carries more information and creates a richer picture than a single word ("quietly") ever can.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Replacing weak adverbs with phrases: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience." Replacing a generic adverb with a specific adverbial phrase is a key vocabulary refinement skill at Phase 2 — it demonstrates that the student is thinking about the reader's experience and choosing language that creates a precise and vivid picture.


#### Wednesday: Who/where/when phrases in persuasive writing

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Composing (Practice): specific who, where, and when details make persuasive arguments more credible and harder to dismiss; students embed precise detail in persuasive sentences and paragraphs.

**I Do — Detail That Persuades**

In Term 1 we used who/where/when phrases to add descriptive detail. This week we use the same technique in persuasive writing — where specific detail makes arguments more convincing.

> Example: Vague: "Schools should have gardens." → Specific: "Schools across Aotearoa, from urban Auckland to rural Northland, should have gardens where students can grow and harvest their own kai."

The added phrases — "across Aotearoa, from urban Auckland to rural Northland" (where) and "where students can grow and harvest their own kai" (who/what) — make the claim feel specific and grounded. Vague arguments are easy to dismiss. Specific ones are harder to ignore.

*Tip: In persuasion, specific detail builds credibility. WHO would benefit? WHERE would this happen? WHEN should it change? Answer those questions inside your sentences.*

**We Do — Make It Specific**

Add who, where, or when detail to each persuasive sentence to make it more convincing.
- Students should have access to nature.
- Communities need to protect waterways.
- Young people deserve to have their voices heard.

*Discussion prompt: Which type of detail makes each argument stronger — who, where, or when? Can you add more than one?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Expand each bare persuasive claim using who/where/when phrases.
- Claim: "Aotearoa needs more native tree planting." Add a where and a when phrase.
- Claim: "Students learn better outdoors." Add a who and a where phrase.
- Claim: "Schools should start later." Add a who and a when phrase.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a persuasive paragraph (5–6 sentences) on a topic you care about. Every sentence must include at least one who, where, or when detail.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Specific detail in persuasion: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader" and to "provide specific, concrete detail that supports the purpose." In persuasive writing, specificity is a core argument strategy — claims supported by precise who, where, and when details are more credible and more resistant to dismissal than vague generalisations.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Building convincing persuasive paragraphs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "organise ideas into paragraphs with a clear main idea, supported by relevant detail" in persuasive writing. This lesson directly builds the ability to support a persuasive topic sentence with specific, embedded detail — the who, where, and when that transforms a claim into a convincing argument.


#### Thursday: Editing: replacing overused words ('nice', 'good')

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Practice) & Writing Process (Practice): students identify and replace the most overused and vague words in their writing with precise, purposeful alternatives.

**I Do — The Vague Word Hit List**

Some words appear in almost every piece of student writing: nice, good, bad, big, little, went, got, said, things, stuff. They're not wrong — they're just empty. Watch me hunt them down and replace them.

> Example: "It was a nice day and we had a good time doing lots of fun things." → "The sun blazed over the harbour and we spent the afternoon kayaking, laughing until our arms gave out."

"Nice day" → specific weather detail. "Good time" → specific activity. "Lots of fun things" → actual examples. The second version creates a picture. The first creates nothing.

*Tip: The vague word hit list: nice, good, bad, big, little, went, got, said, things, stuff, very, really. Highlight every one. Each is an upgrade opportunity.*

**We Do — Upgrade Together**

Find every vague word in this passage and replace it with something precise.
- We went to the nice beach and it was really good. We did lots of fun things and got really tired. The food was nice and everyone said it was a good day.

*Discussion prompt: How many vague words can you find? What specific, precise word or phrase could replace each one?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence, replacing every vague word with a precise alternative.
- The movie was really good and the things that happened were very exciting.
- She is a nice person who always says good things to people.
- It was a bad situation and lots of bad things went wrong.
- ✦ Bonus: Take a paragraph from your own writing. Highlight every word from the vague word hit list. Rewrite the paragraph with every vague word upgraded.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Replacing vague words: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience" and to "consider the effect of word choice on the reader." This lesson targets the most pervasive vocabulary weakness at Years 5–6 — over-reliance on generic, high-frequency words — and builds the habit of interrogating each word choice against a concrete alternative.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Vocabulary audit in revision: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." The vague word audit — highlighting every word from the hit list and challenging each one — is a practical revision strategy that directly improves the precision and vividness of students' writing at Phase 2.


### Week 7

#### Monday: Colons to introduce lists

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): a colon introduces a list only when a complete sentence precedes it; students apply this rule accurately and correct common misuses of the colon.

**I Do — The Colon That Sets Up a List**

We met colons in Term 1. This week we focus specifically on using them to introduce lists — and on the rule about what must come BEFORE the colon.

> Example: ✓ "The kete contained three items: a tī kōuka leaf, a piece of bone, and a carved pounamu." | ✗ "The kete contained: a tī kōuka leaf, a piece of bone, and a carved pounamu."

The colon introduces the list — but only when a complete sentence comes before it. "The kete contained three items" is a complete sentence ✓. "The kete contained" is NOT a complete sentence ✗ — so no colon. The rule: a complete sentence must precede the colon.

*Tip: Before the colon = a complete sentence. After the colon = the list. Test: can the part before the colon stand alone? If not — remove the colon.*

**We Do — Fix or Confirm**

Decide whether the colon is used correctly in each sentence. Fix those that aren't.
- The scientist needed: a microscope, slides, and a sample jar.
- The rescue team brought the following equipment: ropes, a first-aid kit, and torches.
- To make rewena bread you will need: flour, potato water, sugar, and salt.

*Discussion prompt: Is the part before the colon a complete sentence? If not, what needs to change?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add a colon in the correct place, or rewrite so the colon is used correctly.
- The pā required three things to defend effectively high ground, a clear view, and strong palisades.
- There are many native birds in the Waikato region including: tūī, kererū, and pīwakawaka.
- She packed everything she would need for the tramp: food, a sleeping bag, a compass, and wet-weather gear.
- ✦ Bonus: Write five sentences, each using a colon to introduce a list. Make sure every sentence before the colon is grammatically complete.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Colons before lists: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "A colon is used after a complete clause to introduce a list, explanation, or quotation. A common error is placing a colon after an incomplete clause (e.g. 'The bag contained:') — the part before the colon must be a grammatically complete sentence." Students learn to test for completeness before adding a colon, developing both accuracy and understanding of clause structure.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying colons accurately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect," including colons. This lesson develops the completeness test as a reliable self-checking strategy — students identify whether the part before the colon could stand alone as a sentence before applying the mark, preventing the most common colon error at Years 5–6.


#### Tuesday: Progressive tenses: past & present continuous

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): past and present progressive tenses express ongoing actions; students form and use these tenses accurately, understanding how past progressive sets a background scene interrupted by simple past events.

**I Do — Actions in Progress**

The progressive (or continuous) tense shows an action that was happening over a period of time — not just a single completed event.

> Example: Present progressive: "The kākāpō is climbing the tree right now." | Past progressive: "The kākāpō was climbing when we first spotted it."

Present progressive = is/are + verb-ing: "She is writing." Past progressive = was/were + verb-ing: "She was writing when the bell rang." Key use: past progressive sets a scene that another action interrupts. "He was reading when the power cut out."

*Tip: Past progressive sets the background scene. Simple past is the event that interrupts it. Together: "She was sleeping when the earthquake struck."*

**We Do — Form and Use**

Complete each sentence with the correct progressive form.
- The students ___ (prepare) their speeches when the fire alarm went off.
- Look out the window — the kererū ___ (eat) all the berries!
- While the waka ___ (cross) the harbour, the wind suddenly changed direction.

*Discussion prompt: Is this happening right now (present) or was it in progress at a past moment (past)? That decides was/were vs is/are.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write sentences using the progressive tense as directed.
- Write a sentence using past progressive to set a scene, then use simple past for an interrupting event.
- Describe what three different people are doing right now using present progressive.
- Rewrite in past progressive: "The rangatira spoke to the crowd. The sun set behind the hills."
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short recount (5 sentences) of a real or imagined event. Use past progressive at least twice to set the scene before key moments.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Progressive tenses: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "The present progressive tense (is/are + verb-ing) describes an action happening at the moment of speaking. The past progressive tense (was/were + verb-ing) describes an action that was in progress at a past moment, often interrupted by another event in simple past. Both forms require the auxiliary verb 'be' and the present participle (-ing form)."
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Using progressive tense for narrative effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write grammatically correct sentences using a range of tenses." In narrative writing, past progressive is particularly powerful for scene-setting — it creates a sustained background action ("The village was sleeping") that simple past then disrupts ("when the alarm sounded"). This lesson develops students' control of tense as a narrative craft tool.


#### Wednesday: Complex sentences using 'although', 'unless', 'while'

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): 'although', 'unless', and 'while' are subordinating conjunctions that express contrast, condition, and simultaneity; students use them accurately to create nuanced complex sentences.

**I Do — Conjunctions That Add Nuance**

"Although," "unless," and "while" are subordinating conjunctions that add nuance — they create contrast, condition, and simultaneous action.

> Example: "Although it was raining, the match continued." | "The match will continue unless there is lightning." | "While the rain fell, the players kept their positions."

"Although" = contrast (it happened despite something). "Unless" = condition (this won't happen IF something else does). "While" = at the same time. Each creates a different relationship — choosing the right conjunction is part of a writer's craft.

*Tip: "Although" = despite this. "Unless" = except if. "While" = at the same time as. Choose based on the relationship between your two ideas.*

**We Do — Choose the Right Conjunction**

Complete each sentence with although, unless, or while.
- ___ the journey was exhausting, Mere never once complained.
- The kaiako won't begin ___ everyone is seated and listening.
- ___ the kettle boiled, she set out the cups and the tea.

*Discussion prompt: What relationship exists between the two clauses — contrast, condition, or simultaneity?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write two sentences for each conjunction — one with the subordinate clause first, one with it second.
- although (contrast)
- unless (condition)
- while (simultaneous)
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 6 sentences about a challenge or difficult situation. Use although, unless, and while at least once each.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Subordinating conjunctions: although, unless, while: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "'Although' expresses contrast (despite the subordinate clause, the main clause is still true); 'unless' expresses condition (the main clause won't happen except under the stated condition); 'while' expresses simultaneity (both actions happen at the same time). Each conjunction creates a different logical relationship between the clauses — understanding these distinctions is a key Phase 2 grammar expectation."
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Complex sentences for nuance: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures — including complex sentences — for effect and clarity." This lesson extends complex sentence use to three conjunctions that require genuine understanding of logical relationships: contrast, condition, and simultaneity — moving students beyond the familiar "because" and "when" into more nuanced subordination.


#### Thursday: Editing: checking tense consistency

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice) & Sentence Structure (Knowledge): students apply a systematic verb audit to identify and correct tense inconsistencies in modelled and their own writing.

**I Do — The Verb Audit**

A tense audit means underlining every verb in a passage, labelling it as past or present, and then correcting any that break the pattern.

> Example: "The explorer sets out at dawn. She walked for three hours before she reaches the ridge." — sets (Pr), walked (P), reaches (Pr).

Two present, one past — inconsistent. I decide: past tense works better for a recount. Fix: "The explorer set out at dawn. She walked for three hours before she reached the ridge." — all past, all consistent.

*Tip: Be especially careful with "was/is" and "said/says" — these are the most common accidental tense switches.*

**We Do — Audit Together**

Underline every verb, label each P (past) or Pr (present), then rewrite in consistent past tense.
- The market is busy on Saturday mornings. Stalls lined both sides of the street. Vendors called out their prices. The smell of fresh bread drifts through the air. Families stopped to chat and children run between the stalls.

*Discussion prompt: Which verbs switch tense? What is the dominant tense here? Rewrite consistently.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Underline all verbs, label each one P or Pr, then rewrite in consistent past tense.
- The diver slips below the surface. The water was cold and very clear. She searches for the wreck that locals told her about. After ten minutes, she spots a shadow on the seabed. She swam closer and felt her heart race.
- ✦ Bonus: Take a piece of your own writing and do a full tense audit on it. Note how many switches you find.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Tense audit as revision strategy: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate." The tense audit — underline every verb, label the tense, decide on consistency, fix mismatches — is a specific, repeatable revision strategy that goes beyond casual re-reading. It is a direct application of the Phase 2 expectation that students revise systematically.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Identifying tense in context: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Verbs carry tense and must remain consistent throughout a piece of writing. Tense shifts disrupt the reader's experience and signal a lack of control over the writing." This editing lesson deepens grammatical knowledge by requiring students to identify and label verb tense in context before applying corrections — connecting knowledge to practice.


### Week 8

#### Monday: Spelling: tricky high-frequency words

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Conventions (Knowledge): certain high-frequency words have irregular or non-phonetic spellings that must be learned; students apply strategies to memorise and recall these words accurately.

**I Do — Words That Break the Rules**

Some high-frequency words don't follow phonetic patterns — they must be memorised. Watch me demonstrate three strategies for locking these tricky spellings in.

> Example: receive, separate, necessary, occurrence, privilege, conscience, rhythm, liaison

Strategy 1 — Look, Cover, Write, Check: study the word, cover it, write it from memory, check. Strategy 2 — Find the word within the word: "sep-a-rate" (not sep-er-ate) — there's "a rat" in separate! Strategy 3 — Mnemonic: "Rhythm Helps Your Two Hips Move" — first letters spell RHYTHM.

*Tip: When you spell a word wrong twice, look for a mnemonic or pattern that makes the correct spelling stick.*

**We Do — Spelling Strategies**

Use at least one strategy to help you learn each of these tricky spellings.
- necessary (one c, two s) — find a mnemonic.
- occasion (two c, one s) — find the pattern.
- definitely (not "definately") — break it into parts.

*Discussion prompt: Which strategy worked best for each word? Can you teach your strategy to a partner?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Use Look, Cover, Write, Check for each word. Then write a sentence using each one correctly.
- accommodate (two c, two m)
- embarrass (two r, two s)
- government (silent n before m)
- privilege (not "priviledge")
- ✦ Bonus: Create a personal spelling list of 5 words you consistently misspell. Apply one strategy to each and test yourself tomorrow.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Conventions (Knowledge)* — Tricky high-frequency spellings: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Conventions (Knowledge): "Some high-frequency words have irregular or non-phonetic spellings that must be memorised through repeated practice and strategic learning. Students develop spelling knowledge through a range of strategies including visual memory (Look, Cover, Write, Check), morphological analysis (breaking into meaningful parts), and mnemonics." Accurate spelling of high-frequency words is a core expectation at Years 5–6.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Checking and correcting spelling: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking spelling." This lesson builds students' metacognitive awareness of their own spelling weaknesses and gives them a toolkit of strategies — rather than relying on spellcheck — for identifying and fixing errors in their writing.


#### Tuesday: Prepositions of direction & movement

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): directional prepositions express movement through, across, towards, and along specific paths; students use them precisely to create clear, vivid spatial descriptions.

**I Do — Which Way? Prepositions of Direction**

Some prepositions express static location (in, on, under). Others express direction and movement — they tell us where something is going. Watch me use directional prepositions to make movement vivid.

> Example: "The waka moved across the harbour, through the channel, and towards the shore."

"Across" — movement over a surface from one side to another. "Through" — movement from one side of something to the other by passing inside it. "Towards" — movement in the direction of something. "Along" — movement following the length of something. Each is precise about the geometry of the movement.

*Tip: Visualise the movement and ask: is it over a surface (across), inside something (through), in a direction (towards), or following a line (along)?*

**We Do — Choose the Right Preposition**

Choose the most accurate directional preposition for each gap.
- The children ran ___ (across/through/along) the beach to the water's edge.
- The tuna moved ___ (across/through/towards) the dark water of the river.
- She walked ___ (along/towards/through) the ridge, keeping the sea in sight.

*Discussion prompt: Picture the movement precisely. Which preposition describes the path most accurately?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add the correct directional preposition and then rewrite the sentence with more specific detail.
- The kererū flew ___ the canopy and ___ the clearing.
- The river flows ___ the gorge and ___ the sea.
- She moved ___ the crowd and ___ the stage.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 5 sentences describing a journey — by foot, waka, or bike. Use at least four different directional prepositions precisely.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Directional prepositions: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Prepositions of direction (across, through, towards, along, past, into, out of) express movement in relation to a surface, an interior, or a destination. Choosing the correct directional preposition requires visualising the geometry of the movement — is it over a surface, inside something, towards a target, or along a path?" Students learn to distinguish between similar prepositions by thinking spatially.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Precise spatial writing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "expand sentences by adding prepositional phrases to express location" and direction. Using directional prepositions precisely — rather than vague alternatives ("went near," "moved by") — is a key skill in descriptive and narrative writing at Phase 2, enabling students to place the reader inside the movement they are describing.


#### Wednesday: Making vague sentences specific

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Composing (Practice) & Vocabulary (Practice): students transform vague, general sentences into specific, detailed ones by adding precise nouns, verbs, and modifiers that answer the reader's implicit questions.

**I Do — Vague vs. Specific**

Vague sentences tell the reader almost nothing. Specific sentences give them something to picture. Watch me transform a vague sentence into a specific one by answering the reader's questions.

> Example: Vague: "A bird made a sound in the tree." → Specific: "A tūī burst into a cascading trill from the topmost branch of the pōhutukawa."

Reader's questions: WHAT bird? → tūī. WHAT sound? → cascading trill. WHERE in the tree? → topmost branch. WHAT tree? → pōhutukawa. Answering each question turns a generic sentence into one that only this moment could have produced.

*Tip: For every noun, ask: what exactly? For every verb, ask: how exactly? For every place, ask: where exactly?*

**We Do — Make It Specific**

Rewrite each vague sentence by answering every implied reader question.
- A person walked past.
- The weather changed.
- Something fell.

*Discussion prompt: What are the reader's questions for each sentence? What specific detail answers each one?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Transform each vague sentence into a specific one. Aim for a sentence only THIS moment could produce.
- A vehicle went by.
- The animal made a noise.
- She did something with the object.
- The food smelled good.
- ✦ Bonus: Write 4 intentionally vague sentences, then rewrite each one with maximum specificity. Compare the two versions.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Creating specific, vivid writing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader," including providing specific, concrete detail that allows the reader to visualise the scene. This lesson teaches the "reader's question" technique — systematically asking 'what exactly? how exactly? where exactly?' to identify and fill gaps that leave writing vague and generic.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Precise nouns and verbs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience," choosing Tier 2 vocabulary that is precise and cross-curricular. Replacing generic nouns ("bird") with specific ones ("tūī") and generic verbs ("made a sound") with precise ones ("burst into a cascading trill") is the most direct application of this principle — specificity IS vocabulary.


#### Thursday: Editing: strengthening narrative openings

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice) & Composing (Practice): students evaluate narrative openings against criteria for engagement and revise weak openings to draw the reader immediately into the story.

**I Do — First Sentences That Work**

A weak opening loses the reader before the story begins. Watch me evaluate three narrative openings against criteria, then revise the weakest one.

> Example: Weak: "My name is Jake and this is a story about something that happened to me last summer." | Strong: "The day I found the box, I almost walked straight past it."

The weak opening: tells rather than shows, introduces the narrator self-consciously, contains no tension or mystery. The strong opening: drops us into a specific moment, creates immediate curiosity ("what was in the box?"), and doesn't explain itself. Criteria for a strong opening: specific moment, no explaining, creates a question in the reader's mind.

*Tip: Test: does the opening make the reader ask a question? If not — rewrite until it does.*

**We Do — Diagnose and Revise**

Evaluate each opening against our three criteria. Then revise the weakest one together.
- "Once upon a time there was a girl who wanted to be an explorer."
- "The engine coughed twice, then fell silent. In the distance, the lights of the mainland blinked out."
- "I am going to write about the best day I ever had which was when we went to the beach."

*Discussion prompt: Which opening drops you into a specific moment? Which creates a question? Which explains too much?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each weak opening so it meets all three criteria.
- "This is the story of how I found out my neighbour was not who she said she was."
- "One day something strange happened and I will tell you about it in this story."
- "It was a morning like any other until it wasn't."
- ✦ Bonus: Write three alternative openings for a story of your choice. Choose the best one and explain why.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Evaluating and revising narrative openings: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson applies that revision process to the highest-stakes part of any narrative — the opening sentence. Students evaluate against explicit criteria (specific moment, no explaining, reader question) and revise until the opening works.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Engaging the reader from the first sentence: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader." Narrative opening strategy is a key composing skill at Phase 2 — students learn that the most engaging openings drop the reader directly into a specific moment, create immediate curiosity, and resist the urge to explain or introduce the narrator self-consciously.


### Week 9

#### Monday: Alliteration & assonance

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge) & Composing (Practice): alliteration (repeated consonant sounds) and assonance (repeated vowel sounds) are sound devices that create rhythm, mood, and texture in writing.

**I Do — Making Writing Sound as Good as It Reads**

Alliteration repeats the same consonant sound at the start of neighbouring words. Assonance repeats the same vowel sound in the middle of words. Both create rhythm and texture that the reader can hear.

> Example: Alliteration: "The silent snake slipped through the sedge." | Assonance: "The rain-slain plain."

Alliteration: the "s" sound creates a hissing, slithering effect — perfect for a snake. Assonance: the "ai" sound gives a slow, mournful quality — good for a grey, flat landscape. Both devices work best when they reinforce the meaning or mood, not when they're forced.

*Tip: Sound devices should serve the meaning. If the reader notices the device more than the content — it's too obvious.*

**We Do — Hear the Sound**

Brainstorm sound words for each setting, then build a sentence using alliteration or assonance.
- Setting: a beach at high tide
- Setting: a forest just after rain
- Setting: a crowded school cafeteria

*Discussion prompt: What sounds would you hear? What sounds could you create with alliteration or assonance?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write sentences using the device specified.
- Write an alliterative sentence about a NZ animal using the "k" sound (kiwi, kererū, kākāpō, kārearea).
- Write a sentence about the sea using assonance with the "ō" or "oo" sound.
- Write a description of a storm using both alliteration and assonance in the same sentence.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 4-line descriptive poem about a NZ landscape. Use alliteration in at least two lines and assonance in at least one.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Sound devices: alliteration & assonance: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Language devices such as alliteration (repeated consonant sounds) and assonance (repeated vowel sounds) create specific effects for the reader — rhythm, mood, and texture. They are most effective when they reinforce the meaning of the writing rather than being applied for their own sake." Students learn to use sound devices deliberately and discriminatingly, not mechanically.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Using sound devices for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use language devices to create specific effects and engage the reader." Alliteration and assonance are sound-based composing tools — they work on the reader's ear as well as their mind. This lesson develops the discipline of asking 'what mood am I creating?' before selecting which sound to repeat and how often.


#### Tuesday: Coordinating vs. subordinating conjunctions: review

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): students consolidate their understanding of coordinating (FANBOYS) and subordinating conjunctions, applying the distinction to build and identify compound and complex sentences accurately.

**I Do — Two Types, Two Jobs**

We've studied both types of conjunction across Terms 1 and 2. Today we consolidate: coordinating conjunctions join equal clauses; subordinating conjunctions create a main clause and a dependent clause.

> Example: Coordinating: "The hīkoi was long, but the marchers kept going." | Subordinating: "The marchers kept going although the hīkoi was exhausting."

FANBOYS (for, and, nor, but, or, yet, so) = coordinating — both sides could stand alone. AAAWWUBBIS (after, although, as, when, while, until, because, before, if, since) = subordinating — the dependent clause leans on the main clause.

*Tip: Test: remove the conjunction and split the sentence in two. If both halves make sense alone — coordinating. If one half sounds incomplete — subordinating.*

**We Do — Classify and Use**

Identify whether the underlined conjunction is coordinating or subordinating, then explain how you know.
- The kaiako waited until the class was completely silent.
- The experiment failed, yet the students learnt something important.
- Because the tide was out, we could walk across to the island.

*Discussion prompt: Could both sides stand alone? What type of relationship does each conjunction create?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write sentences using the conjunction type shown.
- Write a compound sentence using a coordinating conjunction that shows contrast.
- Write a complex sentence using a subordinating conjunction that shows cause.
- Write a complex sentence where the subordinate clause comes FIRST (with a comma after it).
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 5 sentences about a place you know well. Use at least two coordinating and two subordinating conjunctions. Label each one.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Coordinating vs. subordinating conjunctions: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Coordinating conjunctions (FANBOYS: for, and, nor, but, or, yet, so) join two independent clauses of equal grammatical weight — both sides can stand alone. Subordinating conjunctions (because, although, when, unless, while, until, since, as, before, after, if) introduce a dependent clause that cannot stand alone. Understanding this distinction is essential for building compound and complex sentences accurately."
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Compound & complex sentence consolidation: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures — simple, compound, and complex — for effect and clarity." This review lesson consolidates the distinction between compound sentences (coordinating conjunctions, comma before) and complex sentences (subordinating conjunctions, comma only when fronted) — integrating Term 1 and Term 2 sentence structure learning at Phase 2.


#### Wednesday: Paragraph cohesion: pronouns as linking devices

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Text Structure (Practice): students use personal pronouns, demonstrative pronouns ("this," "these"), and pronoun reference to create cohesion within paragraphs, linking sentences into a connected whole.

**I Do — Pronouns That Hold a Paragraph Together**

We used connective words in Term 1. This week, we focus specifically on pronouns as cohesive devices — how "he," "she," "they," "it," "this," and "these" reach back to a previous sentence and pull ideas together.

> Example: "The tuatara is one of New Zealand's most ancient reptiles. It has barely changed in 200 million years. This makes it a living link to the age of the dinosaurs."

"It" in sentence 2 refers back to "tuatara" — no need to repeat the noun. "This" in sentence 3 refers back to the whole idea of sentence 2. Each pronoun acts like a thread, stitching sentences into a paragraph rather than a list.

*Tip: After writing a paragraph, draw an arrow from each pronoun to what it refers to. Every pronoun should have a clear, unambiguous referent.*

**We Do — Stitch It Together**

Replace the repeated nouns with appropriate pronouns to improve paragraph cohesion.
- The pūkeko is a bold and territorial bird. The pūkeko will often chase away much larger animals. The pūkeko's bright red beak makes the pūkeko easy to spot in wetland areas. Despite the pūkeko's aggression, the pūkeko is a beloved part of the New Zealand landscape.

*Discussion prompt: Which nouns can be replaced with pronouns? Are there any where replacing would cause confusion?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this paragraph so pronouns link the sentences without causing ambiguity.
- The kākāpō is a large, flightless parrot. The kākāpō is critically endangered. Conservation teams monitor every single kākāpō. Each kākāpō has a name and a tracking pack. The tracking pack helps conservation teams follow each kākāpō's movements across the island.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 5 sentences about a NZ animal or place. Use a different pronoun in at least three sentences to link back to the subject. Make sure every pronoun is unambiguous.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Pronoun cohesion within paragraphs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "use cohesive devices — including pronouns, synonyms, and connectives — to link ideas within and across sentences and paragraphs." This lesson focuses specifically on pronoun cohesion: using personal pronouns (it, they), possessive pronouns (its, their), and demonstrative pronouns (this, these) to refer back to nouns and ideas — creating flow rather than repetition.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Pronoun reference & ambiguity: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Pronouns must refer unambiguously to their antecedent. When two or more nouns are present, pronouns can create confusion if the referent is unclear." This lesson develops the habit of checking pronoun reference — drawing arrows from each pronoun to its antecedent — and revising when the reference is ambiguous.


#### Thursday: Editing: identifying weak sentence openings

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice) & Sentence Structure (Practice): students diagnose monotonous sentence opening patterns and revise them using fronted adverbials, adjective openers, and rearranged clause order.

**I Do — The Weak Opening Pattern**

When every sentence starts the same way — especially with "The" or "I" — writing becomes monotonous. Watch me diagnose a passage with weak openings and revise it.

> Example: "The sky was dark. The clouds gathered quickly. The wind picked up. The rain began to fall. The thunder rolled in from the south."

Five sentences. Every one starts with "The." Revision: "Dark clouds gathered quickly. As the wind picked up, the first drops of rain began to fall. From the south, thunder rolled in — low and threatening." Varied openers: adjective, adverbial, fronted phrase. Immediately more dynamic.

*Tip: List the first word of every sentence in your paragraph. If more than two start the same way — vary them.*

**We Do — Diagnose Together**

List the first word of each sentence. Identify the pattern. Then rewrite three sentences with different openers.
- I arrived at school early. I put my bag in my locker. I went to find my friends. I sat down and waited for the bell. I felt nervous about the day ahead.

*Discussion prompt: What pattern do you see? What three different ways could you begin these sentences instead?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this paragraph, varying the sentence openings so no two consecutive sentences start the same way.
- The match started well. The team scored early. The crowd cheered loudly. The other team fought back. The score was level at half time. The players looked tired. The coach spoke to them firmly. The second half began.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 6 sentences about any topic. Make a rule: no two sentences may start with the same word. List the opening word of each sentence underneath.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Revising for sentence opener variety: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson teaches a specific diagnostic strategy — listing the first word of every sentence — that makes repetitive opener patterns immediately visible, enabling targeted revision.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Varying sentence openers: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "vary sentence openings to avoid repetition and create effect," using fronted adverbials, adjective openers, and rearranged clause order. This lesson consolidates all sentence variety strategies from Terms 1 and 2, applying them in a revision context where students must diagnose the problem before selecting the solution.


### Week 10

#### Monday: Review: Term 2 punctuation

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge & Practice): students consolidate all punctuation covered in Term 2 — commas after fronted adverbials, colons, semicolons, brackets, dashes, apostrophes for contraction, and comma splices.

**I Do — Term 2 Punctuation Gallery**

Let's revisit every punctuation mark from Term 2 and its rule.

> Example: Comma after fronted adverbial | Colon before list | Semicolon joining related sentences | Brackets for quiet aside | Dashes for emphatic aside | Contraction apostrophe | No apostrophe for plurals | Comma splice fix

"Before the match began, the referee called the captains together. Each team needed the following things: a captain, a strategy, and confidence. The home team was ready; the visitors looked nervous. One player — the one who had trained hardest — stepped forward."

*Tip: For each mark, ask: what job is it doing here? If it's not doing a job, remove it.*

**We Do — Punctuation Challenge**

Add the correct punctuation to each sentence and name the rule.
- Despite the strong wind the waka race continued as planned.
- The pā required three things a strong location high walls and clear sightlines.
- The tī kōuka also known as the cabbage tree is found throughout Aotearoa.

*Discussion prompt: Which marks are needed and why? Could any sentence use more than one type?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add all missing punctuation to this passage. Name every rule you applied.
- although the weather was overcast the school cross-country went ahead as planned. the course covered three different terrains the playing field a bush track and the school road. hazel our fastest runner had trained every morning before school. she wasnt nervous she was ready. after two kilometres hazel was still in front the other runners couldnt keep up with her pace.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 6 sentences that uses every Term 2 punctuation mark at least once. Label each one.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Term 2 punctuation review: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): Students consolidate all punctuation covered in Term 2: commas after fronted adverbials, colons to introduce lists or explanations, semicolons to join related independent clauses, brackets and dashes for parenthetical information, apostrophes for contraction (not for plurals), and the identification and correction of comma splices. Review ensures students can name the function of each mark and apply the relevant rule.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying full range of punctuation: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect." This consolidation lesson asks students to apply all Term 2 punctuation conventions in an extended passage — naming the rule behind each choice — building the metacognitive awareness needed to make punctuation decisions independently across all writing contexts at Phase 2.


#### Tuesday: Review: Term 2 grammar

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): students consolidate all grammar covered in Term 2 — collective and abstract nouns, future tense, SVA tricky cases, relative pronouns, progressive tenses, and conjunction types.

**I Do — Term 2 Grammar Gallery**

Let's revisit every grammar concept from Term 2 — one mini-example each.

> Example: Collective & abstract nouns | Future tense: will/going to | SVA: everyone/each | Relative pronouns: who/which/that | Progressive tenses: was-ing/is-ing | Conjunction types: CO vs SUB

"A pride of lions (collective) shows remarkable loyalty (abstract). Each lion (SVA: singular) has its own role. The pride is going to move (future: planned) at dusk. The leader, who guides (relative: person) the hunt, was circling (past progressive) the herd when the weakest animal stumbled (simple past interrupts)."

*Tip: Grammar is about understanding why a sentence works, not just labelling. Always connect the rule to the meaning it creates.*

**We Do — Grammar Challenge**

For each sentence, identify the grammar feature underlined and explain what rule it follows.
- A flock of starlings was swooping across the estuary.
- Neither of the answers is correct.
- The taonga, which had been in the whānau for generations, was carefully restored.

*Discussion prompt: What type of noun/pronoun/verb is underlined? What rule governs it?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Complete the grammar review tasks.
- Write a sentence using a collective noun and an abstract noun.
- Write two future tense sentences — one with "will" (spontaneous) and one with "going to" (planned).
- Write a sentence using a relative clause with "which" (remember the commas).
- Write a sentence using past progressive interrupted by simple past.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of 6 sentences demonstrating at least 5 different Term 2 grammar concepts. Label them in the margin.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Term 2 grammar review: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): Students consolidate their understanding of all grammar covered in Term 2: collective and abstract nouns, future tense using will/going to, subject-verb agreement with indefinite pronouns (everyone, each, neither), relative pronouns (who, which, that), past and present progressive tenses, and the distinction between coordinating and subordinating conjunctions. Review connects knowledge to meaning and use.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Integrated grammar application: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write grammatically correct sentences using a range of word classes and structures." This review lesson asks students to demonstrate all Term 2 grammar knowledge in context — combining multiple features in single sentences — moving from isolated recognition to integrated, purposeful use at Phase 2.


#### Wednesday: Review: Term 2 sentence structures

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): students consolidate all sentence structures from Term 2 — compound sentences in new contexts, complex sentences using although/unless/while, sentence combining, and fronted adverbial sentences.

**I Do — The Term 2 Sentence Toolkit**

This term we added powerful new tools to our sentence toolkit. Let me show each one in action.

> Example: FANBOYS in context | Complex: although/unless/while | Combined sentences | Fronted adverbials | Who/where/when expanded

"Although the hīkoi had been going for three days, the marchers showed no sign of stopping (although). They would not rest unless they reached the marae (unless). While the leaders held talks, the rest of the group set up camp (while). Before dawn the next morning, they set out again (fronted adverbial)."

*Tip: Variety is the goal. Read your writing aloud — if it has a rhythm that changes and shifts, you're using your toolkit well.*

**We Do — Sentence Type Identification**

Identify the sentence type and explain the structural feature that defines it.
- Despite the forecast, the outdoor event went ahead without a hitch.
- She wanted to give up, yet something kept her moving forward.
- The waka, which had been carved by hand over two years, was finally blessed.

*Discussion prompt: What type is each sentence? What structural feature tells you?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write one sentence of each type, all on the topic of the sea.
- Compound sentence using "yet."
- Complex sentence starting with "Although."
- Complex sentence using "unless" — subordinate clause second.
- Complex sentence using "while."
- Fronted adverbial sentence.
- ✦ Bonus: Combine all five sentences into a cohesive paragraph about the sea. Edit for flow and variety.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Term 2 sentence structure review: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students consolidate all sentence structures from Term 2: FANBOYS compound sentences in varied contexts, complex sentences using although/unless/while, combining choppy sentences for flow, and fronted adverbial sentences with correct comma placement. Students demonstrate the ability to identify structural features by name and produce each type on demand.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Sentence variety across a paragraph: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader," including controlling rhythm and variety through sentence structure. This consolidation task — writing five different sentence types on the same topic and combining them into a paragraph — directly tests whether students can select the right structure for each moment rather than defaulting to a favourite pattern.


#### Thursday: Full-passage edit: Term 2 checklist

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply all Term 2 editing skills in a systematic multi-pass edit, using the full Term 2 checklist to revise a passage for punctuation, grammar, vocabulary, and structure.

**I Do — The Full Term 2 Edit**

Today I do a complete multi-pass edit using our full Term 2 checklist. One pass per focus — never try to fix everything at once.

> Example: Pass 1: Comma after fronted adverbials | Pass 2: Colons, semicolons, brackets, dashes | Pass 3: Apostrophes (possession AND contraction — no plurals) | Pass 4: Comma splices | Pass 5: Vague verb/word upgrades | Pass 6: Sentence opener variety

I read the passage once for meaning. Then Pass 1: check every sentence that starts with an adverbial phrase — is the comma there? Pass 2: look for lists — do they use colons correctly? Pass 3: every apostrophe — possession, contraction, or error? And so on.

*Tip: Multi-pass editing catches more than single-pass. Each pass has a specific focus — you're not trying to see everything at once.*

**We Do — Class Edit**

Work through each pass together on this passage.
- despite the rain our school fair went ahead last saturday. the organisers needed three things good volunteers lots of stalls and the right attitude. the fair included many activities a cake stall a bouncy castle a talent show and a silent auction. it wasnt perfect but everyones effort paid off. by the end of the afternoon the school had raised over two thousand dollars for the librarys new books.

*Discussion prompt: Pass by pass — what are we fixing? Count the total improvements.*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Edit this passage using all six passes of the Term 2 checklist. Rewrite the improved version.
- on thursday afternoon the year 5 and 6 students went to the wetland reserve near school. the reserve contained many things native plants birds insects and a small stream. although the track was muddy the students werent put off. the guide who had worked there for twenty years showed them a bittern its one of new zealands rarest birds. everyone said it was the best trip theyd ever been on.
- ✦ Bonus: Apply the full 6-pass edit to a paragraph from your own writing journal.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Multi-pass editing: Term 2 checklist: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson makes the revision process systematic and visible — a structured, six-pass approach that integrates every editing skill from Term 2. Students apply punctuation, grammar, and vocabulary knowledge simultaneously in a single extended editing task.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Term 2 skills: integrated application: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): This consolidation lesson asks students to demonstrate mastery of all Term 2 writing skills in one editing task: fronted adverbial commas, colons and semicolons, brackets and dashes, apostrophes, comma splice correction, vocabulary precision, and sentence opener variety. It directly mirrors the Phase 2 expectation that students "apply writing knowledge and skills across a range of text types and purposes" with increasing independence.

## Term 3

### Week 1

#### Monday: Similes for vivid comparison

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): figurative language such as similes compares two unlike things using 'like' or 'as' to create a vivid image; students select figurative language deliberately to enhance meaning for the reader.

**I Do — Comparing Two Unlike Things**

A simile compares one thing to something else using 'like' or 'as', to help the reader picture it more vividly. Watch me build one.

> Example: The waves crashed. → The waves crashed like a thousand drums.

I ask: what does this remind me of? Crashing waves — loud, rhythmic, powerful. 'A thousand drums' captures the sound and force. A weak simile (waves like water) tells us nothing new — a strong one adds a fresh image.

*Tip: Avoid overused similes (cold as ice, brave as a lion). Push for an original comparison instead.*

**We Do — Build the Comparison**

Let's build a simile for each sentence together. Reject the first cliché answer and push for something fresher.
- The thunder was loud.
- Her smile was bright.
- The old house was quiet.

*Discussion prompt: What does this remind you of? Is your first idea a cliché — can you find something more original?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add an original simile to each sentence using 'like' or 'as'.
- The wind howled through the trees.
- The river moved slowly.
- His hands were rough.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two sentences describing the school playground, each using a different, original simile.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Figurative language: similes: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Figurative language, including similes, compares two unlike things using 'like' or 'as' to create a vivid image for the reader. Students consider the connotations and freshness of a comparison rather than relying on clichés." This lesson establishes simile as a deliberate, controllable craft tool.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Selecting original comparisons: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases, including figurative language, that are fit for purpose and audience." This lesson builds the habit of rejecting an overused first idea in favour of an original comparison.


#### Tuesday: Punctuating direct speech

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): direct speech is enclosed in speech marks, with a comma separating the reporting clause, end punctuation inside the speech marks, and a capital letter starting the spoken words; a new speaker begins a new line.

**I Do — The Rules of Speech Marks**

When we write exactly what someone says, we use speech marks (" "). There are four rules: 1) speech marks go around the spoken words only, 2) a capital letter starts the spoken words, 3) end punctuation goes inside the speech marks, 4) a comma separates the speech from the reporting clause. Watch me apply all four.

> Example: the boy said i am not going home yet

Fix: "I am not going home yet," the boy said. — Capital 'I' starts the speech. Comma after 'yet' (inside the marks) before closing them. Lower-case 'the boy said' since it continues the sentence. If the reporting clause comes first: The boy said, "I am not going home yet."

*Tip: Read the spoken words only — would a capital letter naturally start them? Then check the comma and end mark sit inside the speech marks.*

**We Do — Punctuate the Dialogue**

Add speech marks and correct punctuation to these lines together.
- the teacher said sit down please
- wait for me called Aroha
- I dont understand he whispered

*Discussion prompt: Where do the speech marks go? Where does the comma sit — inside or outside?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each line with correct speech-mark punctuation.
- are we there yet asked the little girl
- the captain shouted all hands on deck
- I will never give up she said quietly
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 4-line dialogue between two characters, starting a new line for each new speaker.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Punctuating direct speech: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Direct speech is enclosed in speech marks. A capital letter begins the spoken words, end punctuation sits inside the speech marks, and a comma separates the speech from the reporting clause. A new line begins each time the speaker changes." This is an explicit Phase 2 punctuation expectation.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying dialogue conventions accurately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect," including the conventions of direct speech, which are tested through both editing and their own narrative writing.


#### Wednesday: Sentence openers: -ing clauses

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): a sentence can open with a present participle (-ing) clause that describes an action happening at the same time as the main clause, adding variety to sentence beginnings.

**I Do — Starting with an Action**

Instead of always starting with the subject, we can open a sentence with an -ing clause describing an action happening at the same time as the main action. Watch me build one.

> Example: Normal: The dog burst through the door. It was barking loudly. → Combined: Barking loudly, the dog burst through the door.

Both actions (barking, bursting through) happen at the same time, so I can combine them. The -ing clause goes first, followed by a comma, then the main clause. This creates a more dynamic opening than 'The dog burst through the door, barking loudly.'

*Tip: Check: are both actions truly happening at the same moment? If one happens before the other, this structure won't make sense.*

**We Do — Combine with an -ing Opener**

Combine each pair of sentences using an -ing clause opener.
- The climber gripped the rock face. She was gasping for breath.
- The crowd surged forward. They were cheering wildly.
- The cat crept along the fence. It was watching the bird.

*Discussion prompt: Are both actions happening at the same time? Where does the comma go?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Combine each pair using an -ing opener, remembering the comma.
- The waves pounded the shore. They were roaring furiously.
- The toddler reached for the toy. She was giggling with excitement.
- The soldiers marched into camp. They were singing a marching song.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two original sentences that begin with an -ing clause.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — -ing clause sentence openers: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "A sentence can open with a present participle (-ing) clause describing an action simultaneous with the main clause, followed by a comma. This is one of several techniques for varying sentence openings beyond the subject." Students learn the simultaneity condition that makes this structure work.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Varying sentence openings for effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity," including varied sentence openers. This builds directly on Term 1's fronted adverbial work, adding a new opener type to the student's toolkit.


#### Thursday: Editing: show, don't tell

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Composing (Practice) & Writing Process (Practice): students revise writing by replacing 'telling' statements about emotion or quality with specific sensory and behavioural detail that allows the reader to infer it themselves.

**I Do — Let the Reader Work It Out**

'Telling' states an emotion directly (she was sad). 'Showing' gives details that let the reader infer the emotion themselves. Watch me convert one to the other.

> Example: Telling: She was nervous before the race.

What does nervousness look like physically? Showing: Her hands trembled as she rubbed them on her shorts, and she couldn't stop glancing at the starting line. I haven't used the word 'nervous' once — but the reader feels it.

*Tip: Ask: what would a camera see? Convert the emotion word into an action, gesture, or physical sensation.*

**We Do — Convert Together**

Convert each 'telling' sentence into 'showing' detail.
- He was angry.
- She was excited about the trip.
- The dog was scared of the storm.

*Discussion prompt: What physical signs would show this emotion? What would the character do or say?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each telling sentence using show-don't-tell.
- The old man was tired.
- The children were bored in class.
- She was proud of her painting.
- ✦ Bonus: Take a sentence from your own writing that 'tells' an emotion, and rewrite it to 'show' instead.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Show, don't tell: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader," including conveying emotion through specific, concrete, sensory detail rather than direct statement. This is a key narrative craft technique at Years 5–6.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Revising for engagement: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson gives students a specific, repeatable revision question — 'what would a camera see?' — to apply to their own narrative writing.


### Week 2

#### Monday: Personification

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): personification gives human qualities or actions to non-human things, creating atmosphere and making descriptive writing more vivid and engaging.

**I Do — Giving Objects Human Behaviour**

Personification gives a non-human thing a human action or feeling. Watch me transform a flat description using it.

> Example: The wind blew through the trees. → The wind whispered secrets through the trees.

Wind can't literally whisper — but giving it that human action creates atmosphere (something mysterious or gentle). I match the personification to the mood I want: a storm might 'scream' or 'tear', a calm wind might 'sigh' or 'tiptoe'.

*Tip: Choose a human verb that matches the mood you're creating — gentle, threatening, playful.*

**We Do — Bring It to Life**

Add personification to each sentence to create the mood shown.
- The fire (mood: angry/threatening) ___
- The moon (mood: calm/watchful) ___
- The old gate (mood: tired/creaky) ___

*Discussion prompt: What human action or feeling matches this mood? Does it fit the object?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add personification to each sentence.
- The rain fell on the roof.
- The shadows stretched across the lawn.
- The kettle began to whistle.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two sentences describing a storm, using personification in each to build a frightening mood.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Figurative language: personification: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Figurative language, including personification, gives human qualities to non-human things to create atmosphere and engage the reader. Word choice should be deliberate and matched to the intended mood." This extends Term 3 Week 1's simile work into a second figurative device.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Creating mood and atmosphere: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies, including figurative language, to create texts that engage the reader." This lesson connects vocabulary choice directly to the deliberate creation of mood and atmosphere in descriptive and narrative writing.


#### Tuesday: Active and passive voice

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): in the active voice the subject performs the action; in the passive voice the subject receives the action. Students identify and form both, understanding when each is appropriate.

**I Do — Who Is Doing What?**

In the active voice, the subject does the action (The dog chased the cat). In the passive voice, the subject receives the action (The cat was chased by the dog). Watch me convert one to the other.

> Example: Active: The storm destroyed the bridge. → Passive: The bridge was destroyed by the storm.

I move the object ('the bridge') to the front as the new subject, use a form of 'to be' + past participle ('was destroyed'), and the original subject ('the storm') becomes optional, introduced by 'by'. Active voice is usually punchier; passive voice is useful when the action matters more than who did it.

*Tip: Test for passive voice: can you add '...by zombies' after the verb and it still makes sense? (was destroyed by zombies ✓) — that's a sign of passive voice.*

**We Do — Convert Together**

Convert each active sentence to passive, and discuss why a writer might choose one over the other.
- The council planted the trees.
- A thief stole the painting.
- The scientists discovered the fossil.

*Discussion prompt: What is the new subject? What form of 'to be' do we need? Does this sentence sound better active or passive?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Convert each sentence as instructed.
- Convert to passive: 'The chef prepared the meal.'
- Convert to active: 'The window was broken by the storm.'
- Convert to passive: 'The librarian organised the books.'
- ✦ Bonus: Write one active and one passive sentence about the same event — which version do you prefer, and why?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Active & passive voice: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "In the active voice, the subject performs the action of the verb; in the passive voice, the subject receives the action, formed using 'to be' plus the past participle. Writers choose between active and passive voice deliberately, depending on what they want to emphasise." This is a key Phase 2 grammar concept underpinning formal and explanation writing.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Choosing voice for purpose: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity" and to make deliberate grammatical choices appropriate to text type — active voice typically suits narrative, while passive voice often suits formal explanation and report writing.


#### Wednesday: Joining ideas with semicolons

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): a semicolon joins two closely related independent clauses without a conjunction, creating a more sophisticated link between balanced ideas than a full stop or comma splice.

**I Do — A Sharper Link Than a Full Stop**

We met the semicolon in Term 1. Today we focus on using it to join two closely related, balanced ideas for sophisticated effect.

> Example: The crowd fell silent. The referee blew the whistle. → The crowd fell silent; the referee blew the whistle.
