Both sides are complete sentences, closely linked in time and meaning — a full stop works, but the semicolon shows the reader these two moments are connected. A comma alone here would be a comma splice (an error) — only a semicolon, full stop, or conjunction can join two complete sentences.

*Tip: Test: could both halves stand alone as separate sentences? If yes, and they're closely related — a semicolon is a good choice.*

**We Do — Join with a Semicolon**

Join each pair using a semicolon. Discuss why these two ideas are closely related enough to deserve one.
- The sky darkened. The first drops of rain began to fall.
- She trained for months. Her hard work finally paid off.
- The engine spluttered. Then it died completely.

*Discussion prompt: Are both halves complete sentences? Are they closely related in meaning?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Join each pair with a semicolon.
- The team celebrated. They had won the championship.
- The fire alarm rang. Everyone filed outside calmly.
- He apologised sincerely. She decided to forgive him.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two of your own semicolon sentences about a sporting event.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Semicolons for sophisticated links: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect," including semicolons to join closely related independent clauses. This Term 3 lesson revisits and deepens Term 1's introduction, focusing on the judgement of when two ideas are 'closely related enough' to deserve one.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Avoiding comma splices: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Two independent clauses cannot be joined by a comma alone (a comma splice); they must be joined by a full stop, a semicolon, or a coordinating conjunction." This lesson reinforces that boundary while extending students' control of the semicolon as a deliberate stylistic choice.


#### Thursday: Editing dialogue punctuation

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply the rules of direct speech punctuation systematically when revising a passage of dialogue, checking speech marks, capitalisation, end punctuation, and new lines for new speakers.

**I Do — The Dialogue Edit**

I'll show you how to check a whole passage of dialogue against our four rules: speech marks around spoken words only, capital letter starting speech, end punctuation inside the marks, comma before the reporting clause, and a new line for each new speaker.

> Example: hello said Sam how are you today im fine thanks replied Mia

I read slowly and apply one rule at a time. Fix: "Hello," said Sam. "How are you today?" — new line — "I'm fine, thanks," replied Mia. Notice Sam's two sentences both need their own speech marks since 'said Sam' interrupts.

*Tip: If the reporting clause interrupts mid-speech, close the speech marks before it and reopen them after, with their own punctuation each time.*

**We Do — Fix the Dialogue**

Fix the punctuation in this short dialogue together, one rule at a time.
- where are you going asked dad im going to the library said Leo can I come too he asked sure replied Leo

*Discussion prompt: Where does each speaker change? Where do the speech marks, capitals, and commas go?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this dialogue with full, correct punctuation and new lines for each speaker.
- did you finish your homework asked mum not yet I was about to start said Tom you'd better hurry she said the bus leaves soon
- ✦ Bonus: Write your own 4-line dialogue exchange between two characters arguing about something small, with perfect punctuation.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Systematic dialogue editing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking for correct punctuation." This lesson applies that revision process specifically to direct speech — one of the most error-prone punctuation areas for Years 5–6 writers.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying speech punctuation accurately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect," including the conventions of direct speech. Editing a multi-line dialogue passage consolidates the rule set introduced in Week 1 into an independent, transferable checking strategy.


### Week 3

#### Monday: Metaphors

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): a metaphor describes one thing directly as another (without 'like' or 'as') to create a powerful, often surprising image; students use metaphor deliberately and avoid mixing metaphors.

**I Do — Saying One Thing Is Another**

Unlike a simile, a metaphor says something IS something else — no 'like' or 'as'. It's bolder and can be more powerful. Watch me build one.

> Example: Simile: Her eyes were like diamonds. → Metaphor: Her eyes were diamonds, glittering in the firelight.

Removing 'like' makes the comparison feel more absolute and dramatic. I extend it slightly ('glittering in the firelight') to make the image fuller. Careful: I shouldn't then say her eyes 'sparkled like stars' in the same sentence — that mixes two different metaphors and confuses the reader.

*Tip: Stick to ONE metaphor per image. Mixing metaphors (her eyes were diamonds, twinkling like a lighthouse) confuses rather than clarifies.*

**We Do — Build the Metaphor**

Turn each description into a metaphor.
- The classroom (mood: chaotic) was ___
- The mountain (mood: ancient, unmoving) was ___
- Her voice (mood: soft, calming) was ___

*Discussion prompt: What single bold image captures this mood? Are you keeping it to one consistent image?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a metaphor for each prompt.
- Describe a busy motorway using a metaphor.
- Describe a quiet library using a metaphor.
- Describe an angry sky before a storm using a metaphor.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two sentences about the ocean — one using a simile, one using a metaphor. Which is more powerful, and why?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Figurative language: metaphor: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "A metaphor describes one thing directly as another, without using 'like' or 'as', to create a powerful and often surprising image. Effective metaphors are sustained and not mixed with unrelated comparisons." This builds on Week 1's simile work to complete the simile/metaphor distinction.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Avoiding mixed metaphors: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases, including figurative language, that are fit for purpose and audience," and to consider the coherence of the images they create — recognising and avoiding mixed metaphors is part of controlling figurative language deliberately.


#### Tuesday: Modal verbs

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): modal verbs (can, could, may, might, must, should, will, would) express degrees of possibility, certainty, permission, or obligation; students select modal verbs deliberately to convey precise meaning.

**I Do — Shades of Possibility**

Modal verbs (can, could, may, might, must, should, will, would) change how certain or possible something sounds. Watch how the same sentence shifts meaning with different modals.

> Example: It will rain tomorrow. (certain) | It might rain tomorrow. (possible) | It must have rained last night. (strong inference)

'Will' = confident prediction. 'Might'/'may' = uncertain possibility. 'Must' = strong logical conclusion based on evidence. 'Should' = expectation or recommendation. Choosing the right modal tells the reader exactly how sure you are.

*Tip: Before choosing a modal, ask: how certain am I, really? Match the word to your actual confidence.*

**We Do — Choose the Right Modal**

Choose the modal verb that best fits the level of certainty described.
- (Strong recommendation) You ___ wear a helmet when cycling.
- (Pure possibility, 50/50) The bus ___ be late today.
- (Near-certain conclusion from evidence) She ___ have left already — her bag is gone.

*Discussion prompt: How certain is the speaker? Which modal verb matches that level of certainty exactly?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Complete each sentence with the most precise modal verb, and explain your choice.
- Based on the dark clouds, a storm ___ be coming.
- Students ___ complete their homework by Friday (obligation).
- I ___ be free this weekend, but I'm not certain yet.
- ✦ Bonus: Write three sentences about a mystery, using a different modal verb in each to show increasing certainty.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Modal verbs and certainty: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Modal verbs (can, could, may, might, must, should, will, would) express degrees of possibility, permission, obligation, or certainty. Precise modal choice allows a writer to convey exactly how confident or tentative a claim is." This is an important grammar tool for persuasive and explanatory writing at Phase 2.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Precision in modal choice: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience." Choosing the correct modal verb is a precision skill directly relevant to persuasive argument and explanation writing, where overstating or understating certainty weakens credibility.


#### Wednesday: Sentence openers: -ed clauses

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): a sentence can open with a past participle (-ed) clause describing a completed state or action affecting the subject of the main clause, adding further variety to sentence beginnings.

**I Do — Starting with a Completed State**

An -ed clause opener describes a state or completed action affecting the subject, before the main action. Watch me build one.

> Example: Normal: The hikers were exhausted. They collapsed at the summit. → Combined: Exhausted, the hikers collapsed at the summit.

'Exhausted' describes their state before/as they collapsed. I drop the subject and verb from the first sentence, keep just the -ed word (or phrase), add a comma, then the main clause. Try a longer one: 'Battered by the storm, the old fishing boat limped into harbour.'

*Tip: -ed openers often describe a state (tired, battered, convinced) rather than an ongoing action — that's the key difference from -ing openers.*

**We Do — Combine with an -ed Opener**

Combine each pair using an -ed clause opener.
- The team was defeated. They trudged off the field in silence.
- The cake was burnt. It was thrown straight in the bin.
- The students were convinced by the argument. They voted unanimously in favour.

*Discussion prompt: What -ed word or phrase captures the state? Where does the comma go?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Combine each pair using an -ed opener.
- The dog was soaked from the rain. It shook itself violently in the hallway.
- The old bridge was weakened by years of neglect. It finally collapsed in the flood.
- The crowd was thrilled by the performance. They leapt to their feet.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two original sentences that begin with an -ed clause.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — -ed clause sentence openers: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "A sentence can open with a past participle (-ed) clause describing a completed state affecting the subject of the main clause, followed by a comma. This complements -ing clause openers as a further technique for varying sentence beginnings." Students learn to distinguish state (-ed) from ongoing action (-ing) openers.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Sentence opener variety: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity." This lesson completes a two-part sequence (Week 1: -ing, Week 3: -ed) giving students two further reliable, controllable sentence-opening techniques beyond fronted adverbials.


#### Thursday: Editing for active voice

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice) & Sentence Structure (Practice): students identify overused passive voice constructions in a passage and convert them to active voice where this strengthens clarity and energy.

**I Do — Hunting the Passive**

Passive voice has its uses, but overusing it makes writing feel vague and distant. Watch me find and convert unnecessary passive sentences.

> Example: The ball was kicked by the striker. It was caught by the goalkeeper.

Test: can I add '...by zombies' and it still works? Yes — passive. Who is doing the action? The striker, the goalkeeper. Convert: The striker kicked the ball. The goalkeeper caught it. Much more direct and energetic — better for action narrative.

*Tip: Highlight every 'was/were + past participle' in a passage. Ask: does the reader need to know WHO did it? If yes, switch to active.*

**We Do — Convert Together**

Find and convert the unnecessary passive sentences in this passage.
- The ball was thrown by Mia. It was caught by Sam. A point was scored by the team. The crowd was excited by the win.

*Discussion prompt: Which sentences would be stronger as active voice? Who is doing each action?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this passage, converting passive sentences to active where it strengthens the writing.
- The trophy was lifted by the captain. Photos were taken by the parents. Cheers were given by everyone. The team was congratulated by the coach.
- ✦ Bonus: Find a piece of your own writing with passive voice. Decide if it should stay passive (formal/unknown doer) or switch to active (energy, clarity).

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing overused passive voice: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson teaches the 'by zombies' test as a quick, memorable way to spot unnecessary passive constructions during self-editing.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Choosing voice deliberately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to make deliberate choices between active and passive voice depending on purpose. This lesson reinforces Week 2's grammar knowledge by applying it as a genuine editing decision rather than a grammar exercise alone.


### Week 4

#### Monday: Connectives for persuasive writing

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): cohesive connectives (firstly, furthermore, however, therefore, in addition, on the other hand) signal the relationship between ideas and structure an argument logically for the reader.

**I Do — Signposting Your Argument**

Persuasive connectives tell the reader how one idea relates to the next: adding (furthermore, in addition), contrasting (however, on the other hand), or concluding (therefore, as a result). Watch me use them to structure an argument.

> Example: Schools should start later. Students get more sleep. Some people disagree. Later starts may clash with parents' work hours. We should still consider it.

With connectives: Schools should start later. Firstly, students would get more sleep. However, later starts may clash with parents' work hours. Nevertheless, the benefits to student wellbeing make this worth considering. The connectives turn a list of ideas into a structured argument.

*Tip: Match the connective to the job: adding evidence? furthermore/moreover. Contrasting a counter-point? however/on the other hand. Drawing a conclusion? therefore/as a result.*

**We Do — Choose the Right Connective**

Choose the connective that best links each pair of ideas.
- Recycling reduces landfill waste. ___, it conserves natural resources. (adding)
- Exercise has many benefits. ___, not everyone has time for it. (contrasting)
- Plastic pollutes the ocean. ___, we should reduce single-use plastic. (concluding)

*Discussion prompt: Is this adding, contrasting, or concluding? Which connective fits that job?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add an appropriate connective to link each pair of sentences.
- Homework helps students practise skills. ___, too much homework can cause stress.
- Cities need more cycle lanes. ___, this would reduce traffic congestion.
- Some students prefer working alone. ___, group work builds important teamwork skills.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 4-sentence persuasive paragraph on a topic of your choice, using at least three different connectives.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Persuasive connectives: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Cohesive connectives signal the logical relationship between ideas — addition, contrast, cause, or conclusion — and are essential to structuring a persuasive argument clearly for the reader." Students learn to categorise connectives by function rather than memorising them as a random list.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Structuring an argument: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "use cohesive devices to link ideas within and across sentences and paragraphs" in persuasive writing. This lesson gives students a functional connective bank organised by the job each one does within an argument.


#### Tuesday: Coordinating vs subordinating conjunctions

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): coordinating conjunctions (and, but, or, so) join equal ideas to form compound sentences, while subordinating conjunctions (because, although, when, if) join a dependent clause to a main clause to form complex sentences.

**I Do — Equal Partners vs. Unequal Partners**

Coordinating conjunctions (and, but, or, so, yet) join two equal, independent ideas — a compound sentence. Subordinating conjunctions (because, although, when, if, since) join a dependent clause to a main clause — a complex sentence. Watch the difference.

> Example: Compound: She trained hard, and she won the race. | Complex: She won the race because she trained hard.

In the compound sentence, both halves could stand alone as full sentences — they're equal partners joined by 'and'. In the complex sentence, 'because she trained hard' cannot stand alone — it depends on the main clause for its full meaning.

*Tip: Test: read the clause after the conjunction alone. If it makes complete sense by itself — coordinating. If it sounds incomplete — subordinating.*

**We Do — Sort and Join**

Decide whether each conjunction is coordinating or subordinating, then use it to join the sentence pair correctly.
- because — The match was cancelled. The field was flooded.
- but — She wanted to go outside. It was raining heavily.
- although — He was tired. He kept practising.

*Discussion prompt: Is this conjunction joining two equal ideas, or making one idea depend on the other?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Join each pair using the conjunction given, and label the sentence as compound or complex.
- Use 'so': The bus was late. We missed the start of the film.
- Use 'when': The bell rang. The students rushed to lunch.
- Use 'or': You can hand in your work today. You can hand it in tomorrow.
- ✦ Bonus: Write one compound and one complex sentence about the same event — point out which conjunction made each one which type.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Coordinating vs subordinating conjunctions: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Coordinating conjunctions (and, but, or, so, yet) join two independent clauses of equal grammatical weight to form a compound sentence. Subordinating conjunctions (because, although, when, if, since) join a dependent clause to an independent main clause to form a complex sentence." This lesson consolidates Term 1–2 sentence work into one explicit comparison.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Choosing sentence type for purpose: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures — simple, compound, and complex — for effect and clarity," and to understand the grammatical reasoning behind each type, not just produce them by imitation.


#### Wednesday: PEEL paragraphs for persuasive writing

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge & Practice): the PEEL structure (Point, Evidence, Explain, Link) organises a persuasive paragraph so that each claim is supported by evidence, explained for the reader, and linked back to the overall argument.

**I Do — Building an Argument Paragraph**

PEEL stands for Point (the claim), Evidence (proof or example), Explain (why this evidence supports the point), and Link (connect back to the argument or next paragraph). Watch me build one.

> Example: Point: Schools should have longer lunch breaks. Evidence: Studies show students concentrate better after proper rest. Explain: This means more break time would directly improve learning in afternoon classes. Link: For this reason, schools should reconsider their lunch schedules.

Each sentence does one specific job. Point states the claim clearly. Evidence backs it with something concrete. Explain bridges the evidence to the claim — this is the step many writers skip! Link closes the loop back to the argument.

*Tip: If your 'explain' sentence just repeats the evidence, it's not doing its job — it must say WHY the evidence proves the point.*

**We Do — Build a PEEL Paragraph**

Build a PEEL paragraph together for this point.
- Point: Students should be allowed to choose their own reading books.
- Evidence: ___ (what proof or example supports this?)
- Explain: ___ (why does this evidence support the point?)
- Link: ___ (connect back to the argument)

*Discussion prompt: What evidence is convincing? Does your 'explain' sentence actually explain, or just repeat?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a full PEEL paragraph for the given point, labelling each sentence P/E/E/L.
- Point: Recycling should be compulsory in all schools.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a second PEEL paragraph arguing the opposite side of any topic you choose — practise constructing a balanced argument.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge)* — PEEL paragraph structure: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge): "Persuasive paragraphs are commonly structured using Point, Evidence, Explain, Link — each sentence performing a distinct function: stating a claim, providing supporting evidence, explaining the connection between evidence and claim, and linking back to the overall argument." This is the central organisational structure expected for persuasive writing at Phase 2.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Constructing reasoned argument: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "organise ideas into paragraphs with a clear main idea, supported by relevant detail and reasoning." PEEL gives students an explicit, repeatable framework for building this structure independently in persuasive writing.


#### Thursday: Editing PEEL paragraphs

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students evaluate a persuasive paragraph against the PEEL structure, identifying missing or weak elements (point, evidence, explain, link) and revising accordingly.

**I Do — Diagnosing a Weak Paragraph**

I'll show you how to check a paragraph against the PEEL structure and find what's missing.

> Example: Recycling is good for the environment. Lots of countries recycle a lot. It really helps.

Point ✓ (recycling is good). Evidence — weak ('lots of countries recycle a lot' is vague, not real evidence). Explain — missing entirely! 'It really helps' doesn't say HOW or WHY. Link — missing. Fix: add specific evidence (e.g. a statistic or example), explain the connection, and link back to the argument.

*Tip: Read your paragraph and label each sentence P, E, E, or L in the margin. Any letter missing or weak is your revision target.*

**We Do — Diagnose Together**

Label each sentence P/E/E/L in this paragraph. What's missing or weak?
- Students should have more PE time at school. Exercise is good for you. Schools should change their timetables.

*Discussion prompt: Which part is missing? Is the evidence specific enough? Where's the explain sentence?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

This paragraph is missing its Explain and Link sentences. Add them.
- Point: Libraries should stay open later. Evidence: Many students don't have a quiet place to study at home. [Add an Explain sentence] [Add a Link sentence]
- ✦ Bonus: Take a PEEL paragraph you wrote on Wednesday and check it against the four labels — strengthen any weak part.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Diagnosing paragraph structure: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to structure to improve clarity and impact." Labelling each sentence against the PEEL framework gives students a precise diagnostic tool for identifying structural weaknesses in persuasive paragraphs.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Strengthening argument paragraphs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "organise ideas into paragraphs with a clear main idea, supported by relevant detail and reasoning." This editing lesson consolidates Wednesday's PEEL construction work by practising the reverse skill: recognising and repairing a paragraph that doesn't yet meet the structure.


### Week 5

#### Monday: Connotation: positive vs negative word choice

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): words with similar denotations can carry different connotations (positive, negative, or neutral associations); writers select words deliberately to shape the reader's attitude towards a subject.

**I Do — The Same Fact, Different Feelings**

Connotation is the feeling or association a word carries, beyond its dictionary meaning. Two words can describe the same thing but make the reader feel differently about it. Watch me compare.

> Example: He was stubborn. vs. He was determined. | She was nosy. vs. She was curious.

'Stubborn' and 'determined' both mean someone won't change their mind — but 'stubborn' sounds negative, 'determined' sounds admirable. Same fact, opposite feeling. Writers choose connotation deliberately depending on whether they want the reader to sympathise or disapprove.

*Tip: Ask: do I want the reader to feel positively, negatively, or neutrally about this? Choose your word to match.*

**We Do — Choose the Connotation**

For each neutral idea, choose a positive word AND a negative word with the same basic meaning.
- A person who saves money carefully → positive: ___ negative: ___
- A person who speaks their mind → positive: ___ negative: ___
- A child who asks lots of questions → positive: ___ negative: ___

*Discussion prompt: What word makes the reader admire this trait? What word makes them disapprove of it?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each neutral sentence twice — once with positive connotation, once with negative — describing the same event.
- The politician changed her opinion on the policy. (positive / negative)
- The team took a long time to finish the project. (positive / negative)
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short paragraph describing the same character twice — once so the reader likes them, once so the reader dislikes them — using only connotation, not different facts.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Connotation and word choice: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Words with similar meanings can carry different connotations — positive, negative, or neutral associations — and writers select vocabulary deliberately to shape the reader's attitude." This is an advanced application of the precision vocabulary work begun in Term 1 and Term 2.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Shaping reader attitude: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "consider the effect of word choice on the reader." Understanding connotation is essential for persuasive and characterisation writing, where the writer deliberately steers how the reader judges a person, action, or idea.


#### Tuesday: Nominalisation

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): nominalisation turns a verb or adjective into a noun (decide → decision, careless → carelessness), producing the more formal, condensed sentence style typical of explanation and report writing.

**I Do — Turning Actions into Things**

Nominalisation changes a verb or adjective into a noun. It creates a more formal, compact sentence — common in reports, explanations, and academic writing.

> Example: Informal/verb-based: The council decided to build a new park, which many people were happy about. → Nominalised: The council's decision to build a new park led to widespread happiness.

'Decided' (verb) → 'decision' (noun). 'Happy' (adjective) → 'happiness' (noun). The nominalised version packs the idea into a noun phrase, sounding more formal and report-like — useful for explanation writing, but can feel stiff if overused in narrative.

*Tip: Common pattern: verb + -ion/-ment/-ation, adjective + -ness/-ity. Try it, then ask: does this sentence still sound natural?*

**We Do — Nominalise Together**

Rewrite each sentence using a nominalisation of the underlined word.
- The scientists discovered a new species.
- The students were curious about the experiment.
- The government announced new road safety rules.

*Discussion prompt: What noun form of this word exists? Does the new sentence still make sense?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence using nominalisation to create a more formal tone.
- The team improved their performance over the season.
- Many people are concerned about climate change.
- The company expanded into new markets.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two versions of a sentence about a school event — one informal/verb-based, one nominalised and formal. Which suits a newsletter? Which suits a casual conversation?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Nominalisation: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Nominalisation converts a verb or adjective into a noun (decide → decision, careless → carelessness), producing a more condensed, formal sentence style typical of explanation and report text types." This is an advanced Phase 2 grammar concept connecting word formation to register.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Register-appropriate word forms: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience." Recognising when nominalisation suits formal writing (and when it makes narrative writing feel stiff) builds students' awareness of register as a deliberate choice.


#### Wednesday: Cause and effect sentences

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): cause-and-effect connectives (because, as a result, consequently, due to, this means that) construct sentences that clearly link an action or event to its outcome, essential for explanation writing.

**I Do — Linking Cause to Effect**

Explanation writing relies on clear cause-and-effect links. Watch me build sentences using a range of cause-and-effect connectives, not just 'because'.

> Example: Cause: Deforestation removes trees that absorb carbon dioxide. Effect: Greenhouse gases increase. → Deforestation removes trees that absorb carbon dioxide; as a result, greenhouse gases increase.

I could also write: 'Because deforestation removes trees... greenhouse gases increase' or 'Deforestation removes trees... Consequently, greenhouse gases increase.' Varying the connective (because, as a result, consequently, due to, this means that) avoids repetition across a whole explanation text.

*Tip: If you've used 'because' three times already in a paragraph, reach for 'as a result', 'consequently', or 'this means that' instead.*

**We Do — Link Cause and Effect**

Join each cause and effect using a different connective each time.
- Cause: Ice caps are melting. Effect: Sea levels are rising.
- Cause: Plastic takes hundreds of years to break down. Effect: It pollutes oceans for generations.
- Cause: Bees pollinate crops. Effect: Many food sources depend on them.

*Discussion prompt: Which connective haven't you used yet? Does the sentence still make logical sense?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a cause-and-effect sentence for each pair, using a different connective in each.
- Cause: Native forests are cleared for farmland. Effect: habitats are lost.
- Cause: Regular exercise strengthens the heart. Effect: overall health improves.
- Cause: Overfishing reduces fish populations. Effect: fishing communities suffer economically.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 4-sentence explanation paragraph about why recycling matters, using at least three different cause-and-effect connectives.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Cause-and-effect connectives: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of connectives to link cause and effect within explanation writing," varying beyond 'because' to include 'as a result', 'consequently', 'due to', and 'this means that'. This is a core grammatical requirement of explanation text type.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge)* — Explanation text structure: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge): "Explanation texts are structured around clear cause-and-effect relationships, typically organised sequentially or logically." This lesson builds the sentence-level tool that underpins the larger explanation text-type structure students are expected to control by Years 5–6.


#### Thursday: Editing for formal register

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice) & Vocabulary (Practice): students identify informal language (contractions, slang, casual connectives) inappropriate for formal writing and revise it to match a formal register.

**I Do — Matching Language to Audience**

Formal writing (reports, persuasive essays, explanations) avoids contractions, slang, and casual phrasing. Watch me formalise an informal passage.

> Example: Informal: Kids shouldn't have to do tons of homework, it's just not fair and it's really stressful.

Fix: Students should not be required to complete excessive amounts of homework, as it places unnecessary stress upon them. 'Shouldn't' → 'should not'. 'Kids' → 'students' (more formal noun). 'tons of' → 'excessive amounts of'. 'it's just not fair' → cut, replaced with a more formal justification.

*Tip: Hunt for contractions (don't, it's, can't), slang (kids, tons, stuff), and casual fillers (just, really, like) — these are the first things to formalise.*

**We Do — Formalise Together**

Rewrite this passage in a formal register, removing contractions and casual language.
- I don't think we should have so much plastic packaging, it's bad for the environment and it's just wasteful.

*Discussion prompt: Which words are too casual for formal writing? What more formal word or phrase could replace each one?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence in a formal register.
- Schools shouldn't ban phones, it's not really fair on kids who need them.
- I reckon the council should fix the playground, it's been broken for ages.
- ✦ Bonus: Take an informal sentence you might say to a friend and rewrite it as if it were appearing in a formal letter to your school principal.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Formal vs informal register: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience," including matching register (formal/informal) to text type and audience. This editing lesson targets the most common register slip in Years 5–6 persuasive and explanation writing — contractions and casual phrasing.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing for audience and purpose: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to vocabulary… to improve clarity and impact," considering audience and purpose. This lesson builds a specific register-checking strategy students can apply to any formal writing task.


### Week 6

#### Monday: Formal vs informal register

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): register refers to the level of formality in language, signalled through vocabulary, sentence length, and contractions; writers match register to audience and purpose.

**I Do — Two Ways to Say the Same Thing**

Register is how formal or informal language is. The same message can be said very differently depending on audience and purpose. Watch me compare.

> Example: To a friend: Hey, can you grab me a drink? | To a stranger/formal: Excuse me, would you mind passing me a drink, please?

Both ask the same thing! But word choice ('hey'/'excuse me'), contractions, and politeness markers ('would you mind', 'please') signal formality. I choose register based on WHO I'm writing to and WHY.

*Tip: Ask: would I say this to my principal? If not, it's probably too informal for formal writing — and vice versa.*

**We Do — Identify the Register**

Decide if each sentence is formal or informal, and explain the clues.
- Yo, that movie was awesome!
- The committee respectfully requests your attendance at the meeting.
- I reckon we should just go for it.

*Discussion prompt: What words or phrases gave away the register? Could you rewrite it in the opposite register?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each informal sentence in formal register, and each formal sentence in informal register.
- Informal → Formal: 'Can you tell the boss I'm gonna be late?'
- Formal → Informal: 'We would be grateful if you could submit your assignment by Friday.'
- ✦ Bonus: Write the same short message (e.g. asking for help with a project) twice — once to a teacher (formal), once to a friend (informal).

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Register: formal and informal language: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Register refers to the level of formality in language, signalled through word choice, contractions, sentence length, and politeness markers. Writers match register deliberately to audience and purpose." This is a key Phase 2 expectation underpinning all text types.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Matching language to audience: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies appropriate to audience and purpose." This lesson makes register an explicit, comparable choice rather than an instinctive one, preparing students for the formal-register editing work later in the term.


#### Tuesday: Reported (indirect) speech

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): reported speech conveys what someone said without quotation marks, requiring a backshift in tense and changes to pronouns and time/place words; students convert between direct and reported speech accurately.

**I Do — Speech Without the Speech Marks**

Reported speech tells us what someone said without quoting their exact words — no speech marks needed. But several things change: the verb tense usually shifts back, and pronouns/time words adjust. Watch me convert.

> Example: Direct: Maya said, "I am going to the shop now." → Reported: Maya said that she was going to the shop then.

'am going' (present) → 'was going' (past) — tense shifts back. 'I' → 'she' — pronoun changes to match the new speaker perspective. 'now' → 'then' — time word shifts since we're reporting it later. No speech marks needed at all.

*Tip: Three changes to check: 1) does the tense shift back? 2) do pronouns need to change? 3) do time/place words need to change (now→then, here→there, today→that day)?*

**We Do — Convert to Reported Speech**

Convert each direct speech sentence to reported speech.
- Tom said, "I am tired."
- She said, "I will finish my homework tomorrow."
- "We are leaving now," they said.

*Discussion prompt: Does the tense shift back? Do the pronouns change? Do any time words need to change?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Convert each sentence as instructed.
- Convert to reported speech: Aroha said, "I love this book."
- Convert to reported speech: "I can't come today," he said.
- Convert to direct speech: She said that she was going home then.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short conversation in direct speech, then rewrite the whole thing as a reported-speech summary.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Direct and reported (indirect) speech: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "Reported speech conveys what someone said without quotation marks, typically requiring a backshift in verb tense and corresponding changes to pronouns and time or place adverbs." This consolidates Week 1's direct speech punctuation knowledge by introducing its grammatical counterpart.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Converting between direct and reported speech: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write grammatically correct sentences using a range of tenses" and structures. Reported speech is a common feature of recount and news-style writing, requiring students to apply tense and pronoun agreement knowledge simultaneously.


#### Wednesday: Balancing sentence rhythm

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): writers deliberately balance the length and structure of sentences across a paragraph to control pace, building on short-vs-long sentence variety with attention to rhythm across a whole passage.

**I Do — Listening to a Paragraph's Rhythm**

We've practised varying single sentences. Today, listen to the rhythm of a WHOLE paragraph and learn to balance it across multiple sentences.

> Example: All medium-length paragraph: 'The waves crashed against the rocks. The wind howled through the cliffs. Seabirds circled overhead. The storm grew worse by the minute.' → all the same length — monotonous.

Rebalanced: 'The waves crashed against the rocks, and the wind howled through the cliffs. Seabirds circled. The storm grew worse and worse, the sky darkening with every passing minute.' Now there's a long-short-long pattern — a rhythm, not a drumbeat.

*Tip: Read your paragraph aloud. If every sentence takes the same breath to say, vary at least one to break the pattern.*

**We Do — Rebalance Together**

This paragraph has a flat, repetitive rhythm. Let's rebalance it by combining some sentences and keeping others short.
- The fire spread quickly. The smoke filled the room. The alarm rang loudly. Everyone ran outside. The firefighters arrived fast.

*Discussion prompt: Which sentences could combine? Which single short sentence should stay for impact?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rebalance this paragraph's rhythm by combining at least two sentences while keeping one short for impact.
- The race began. The runners sprinted forward. The crowd cheered loudly. One runner pulled ahead. She crossed the line first.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 5-sentence paragraph about an exciting moment, deliberately mixing short and long sentences for a clear rhythm. Read it aloud to check.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Balancing sentence rhythm across a paragraph: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity," extended here to consider rhythm across a whole paragraph rather than a single sentence. This builds on Term 1's short-vs-long sentence work with a wider lens.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Controlling pace across a passage: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader," including controlling pace and rhythm across an extended piece of writing, not just within a single sentence.


#### Thursday: Editing for cohesion

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Text Structure (Practice) & Writing Process (Practice): students revise a multi-paragraph passage to add cohesive devices (connectives, pronouns, repeated key words) that link ideas between paragraphs.

**I Do — Linking Paragraphs Together**

Cohesion means ideas flow smoothly between sentences AND paragraphs. Watch me fix a passage where paragraphs feel disconnected.

> Example: Para 1 ends: ...recycling reduces landfill waste. Para 2 starts: Pollution is also a big problem.

The jump from 'recycling' to 'pollution' feels abrupt — no link. Fix: 'In addition to reducing landfill waste, recycling also helps tackle a second major issue: pollution.' I added a connective ('in addition to') and referenced the previous idea before introducing the new one.

*Tip: At the start of a new paragraph, briefly nod back to the previous idea before introducing the new one — this is what creates flow.*

**We Do — Add the Links**

These two paragraph openings feel disconnected. Add a cohesive link between them.
- Para 1 ends: ...native birds are losing their habitats. Para 2 starts: ___ Predators are another major threat to their survival.

*Discussion prompt: How can you connect the idea of habitat loss to the new idea of predators?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add a cohesive link sentence or phrase between each pair of paragraph endings/openings.
- Para 1 ends: ...exercise improves physical health. Para 2 starts: ___ Mental health benefits are just as important.
- Para 1 ends: ...plastic pollution harms marine life. Para 2 starts: ___ Solutions are within our reach.
- ✦ Bonus: Take two paragraphs from a piece of your own writing and check whether they connect smoothly — add a link if they don't.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Cohesion across paragraphs: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "use cohesive devices — including connectives, pronouns, and repeated key words — to link ideas within and across sentences and paragraphs." This lesson extends sentence-level cohesion (Term 1) to the more demanding skill of linking entire paragraphs.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Revising for flow: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson gives students a specific revision technique — briefly referencing the previous idea before introducing a new one — to apply when their writing feels disjointed between paragraphs.


### Week 7

#### Monday: Onomatopoeia

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): onomatopoeia uses words that imitate the sound they describe, adding sensory immediacy to descriptive and narrative writing.

**I Do — Words That Sound Like What They Mean**

Onomatopoeia words imitate sounds (crash, sizzle, hiss). They make writing more sensory and immediate. Watch me weave one into a sentence.

> Example: The pan was hot. The bacon went in. → The bacon sizzled as it hit the scorching pan.

'Sizzled' lets the reader almost hear the sound, instead of just being told something happened. I choose the precise onomatopoeic word to match the exact sound — not just 'made a noise', but the specific noise (sizzle, not crackle, not hiss).

*Tip: Don't overuse onomatopoeia — one well-placed sound word is more powerful than five crammed into one sentence.*

**We Do — Add the Sound**

Add an onomatopoeic word to bring each sentence to life.
- The branch ___ as it broke underfoot.
- The balloon ___ when the pin touched it.
- Rain ___ against the tin roof.

*Discussion prompt: What exact sound would this make? Is there a more precise onomatopoeic word?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add an onomatopoeic word to each sentence.
- The old door ___ open slowly.
- Bees ___ around the flowers.
- The fireworks ___ overhead.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 3-sentence description of a thunderstorm using at least three different onomatopoeic words.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Onomatopoeia: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Onomatopoeia uses words that imitate the sound they describe, adding sensory immediacy to writing. Like other figurative and sound devices, it is most effective when used sparingly and precisely." This continues the term's exploration of figurative and sensory language devices.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Sensory detail in description: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies, including sensory and figurative language, to create texts that engage the reader." Onomatopoeia adds an auditory dimension to the sensory toolkit students are building across the term.


#### Tuesday: Expanded noun phrases

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): a noun phrase can be expanded with pre-modifiers (adjectives before the noun) and post-modifiers (prepositional or relative clauses after the noun) to add precise, layered detail.

**I Do — Building a Noun Phrase from Both Sides**

A noun phrase can be expanded BEFORE the noun (pre-modifiers: adjectives) and AFTER the noun (post-modifiers: prepositional phrases or relative clauses). Watch me build one from a bare noun.

> Example: Bare: the dog → Pre-modified: the scruffy, three-legged dog → Fully expanded: the scruffy, three-legged dog that lived under the bridge.

'Scruffy, three-legged' goes before the noun (pre-modification). 'That lived under the bridge' goes after (post-modification, a relative clause). Together they build a rich, specific noun phrase — much more detailed than 'the dog' alone.

*Tip: Pre-modifiers answer 'what kind?'. Post-modifiers answer 'which one, more specifically?' — use both for maximum detail.*

**We Do — Expand the Noun Phrase**

Expand each bare noun phrase with both a pre-modifier and a post-modifier.
- the house
- the teacher
- the river

*Discussion prompt: What adjectives go before? What phrase or clause could go after to add more specific detail?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Expand each noun phrase using both pre- and post-modification.
- the car
- the mountain
- the book
- ✦ Bonus: Write one sentence using a fully expanded noun phrase (pre- and post-modified) as the subject of the sentence.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Expanded noun phrases: pre- and post-modification: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): "A noun phrase can be expanded through pre-modification (adjectives placed before the noun) and post-modification (prepositional phrases or relative clauses placed after the noun), allowing writers to layer precise detail around a single noun." This is an advanced Phase 2 grammar skill combining several earlier concepts (adjectives, prepositional phrases, relative clauses).
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Building detailed noun phrases: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "expand sentences by adding words, phrases, and clauses to add detail and description." This lesson draws together adjective, prepositional phrase, and relative clause knowledge from across the year into a single, powerful descriptive tool.


#### Wednesday: Rhetorical questions

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): a rhetorical question is asked for effect rather than to elicit an answer, used in persuasive writing to provoke thought and draw the reader into agreeing with the writer's position.

**I Do — Asking Without Expecting an Answer**

A rhetorical question isn't asked to get an answer — it's asked to make the reader think, and often the answer is obvious. Watch me use one to open a persuasive paragraph.

> Example: Plain: Plastic pollution is a serious problem. → Rhetorical: Do we really want future generations to inherit oceans choked with plastic?

The rhetorical question forces the reader to imagine the consequence themselves — more persuasive than simply stating a fact. The implied answer ('No, of course not') pulls the reader towards agreeing with the writer.

*Tip: Use rhetorical questions sparingly — one per paragraph at most, usually at the start or end, or they lose their power.*

**We Do — Turn Statements into Rhetorical Questions**

Convert each plain statement into a rhetorical question that makes the same point more persuasively.
- Animals should not be kept in tiny cages.
- Wasting food is unacceptable when so many people go hungry.
- Every student deserves access to a good education.

*Discussion prompt: What question could you ask whose obvious answer makes your point for you?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a rhetorical question for each persuasive topic.
- Topic: protecting native wildlife.
- Topic: reducing screen time for children.
- Topic: keeping local libraries open.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short persuasive paragraph that opens AND closes with a rhetorical question.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Rhetorical questions for persuasive effect: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "use a variety of sentence structures and types — including questions — for effect," specifically rhetorical questions in persuasive writing, where they are used to provoke thought rather than seek information.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Engaging the reader in argument: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader," particularly in persuasive writing where drawing the reader into actively considering the argument (rather than passively receiving it) strengthens persuasive impact.


#### Thursday: Editing repetitive sentence openers

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students identify repeated sentence-opening patterns (especially repeated subjects) across a passage and revise using the variety of openers learned this term.

**I Do — Spotting the Repetition**

If every sentence in a paragraph starts the same way, it feels monotonous. Watch me find and fix repeated openers using this term's toolkit (fronted adverbials, -ing/-ed clauses, varied subjects).

> Example: The dog ran across the field. The dog jumped over the fence. The dog barked at the cat.

Every sentence starts with 'The dog' — repetitive. Fix using variety: 'Barking excitedly, the dog ran across the field and jumped over the fence to chase the cat.' I combined ideas AND used an -ing opener instead of repeating the subject three times.

*Tip: Underline the first two or three words of every sentence in a paragraph. If you see the same pattern twice in a row — vary it.*

**We Do — Fix the Repetition**

This paragraph repeats its sentence openers. Let's vary them together using techniques from this term.
- The rain started to fall. The rain grew heavier. The rain flooded the streets. The rain finally stopped at midnight.

*Discussion prompt: Which sentences could combine? Which opener type (fronted adverbial, -ing, -ed) could replace a repeated subject?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite this paragraph, fixing the repetitive sentence openers using at least two different techniques from this term.
- The girl walked into the room. The girl looked around nervously. The girl saw her friends waiting. The girl smiled with relief.
- ✦ Bonus: Check a paragraph of your own writing for repeated sentence openers and fix at least two.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing for sentence opener variety: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing… making changes to structure to improve clarity and impact." This lesson trains students to scan specifically for repeated sentence openings — a common Years 5–6 weakness — and apply the term's full toolkit (fronted adverbials, -ing/-ed clauses) to fix it.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Applying sentence opener variety: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity." This editing lesson consolidates every sentence-opener technique taught this term into one applied skill.


### Week 8

#### Monday: Idioms and figurative expressions

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): idioms are fixed figurative expressions whose meaning cannot be worked out literally from the individual words; students use idioms appropriately and understand their effect on tone.

**I Do — Expressions That Don't Mean What They Say**

An idiom is a fixed expression whose meaning isn't literal — you can't guess it just from the individual words. Watch me use one correctly and explain its effect.

> Example: 'It's raining cats and dogs' doesn't mean animals are falling from the sky — it means it's raining very heavily.

Idioms add personality and a conversational tone, which suits informal narrative or dialogue — but they can sound out of place in formal writing. 'She was over the moon about her results' fits a casual recount, but not a formal report.

*Tip: Idioms suit informal/narrative writing and dialogue. Avoid them in formal persuasive or explanation writing, where literal precision matters more.*

**We Do — Use Idioms Appropriately**

Decide whether each idiom fits the writing context given, and explain why or why not.
- 'It cost an arm and a leg' — in a casual recount about a holiday.
- 'It cost an arm and a leg' — in a formal report on government spending.
- 'Break the ice' — in a story where two characters meet for the first time.

*Discussion prompt: Does this idiom suit the tone and formality of the writing? What might be a more precise alternative for formal writing?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Use each idiom correctly in a sentence, matching it to an appropriately informal context.
- Use the idiom 'once in a blue moon' in a sentence.
- Use the idiom 'hit the nail on the head' in a sentence.
- Use the idiom 'under the weather' in a sentence.
- ✦ Bonus: Find an idiom you've heard before. Explain its literal words, its real (figurative) meaning, and write a sentence using it correctly.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Idioms and figurative expressions: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Idioms are fixed figurative expressions whose meaning cannot be derived literally from their individual words. Their use is generally suited to informal or conversational registers." This completes the term's run of figurative vocabulary devices (simile, personification, metaphor, onomatopoeia, idiom).
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Matching figurative language to register: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases… that are fit for purpose and audience." This lesson connects directly to the term's register work (Week 5–6), reinforcing that even figurative language must be matched to the formality of the text.


#### Tuesday: Parenthesis review: commas, brackets, dashes

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): commas, brackets, and dashes can all mark parenthetical information; students choose between the three based on the level of formality and emphasis required, consolidating a full term's worth of punctuation knowledge.

**I Do — Three Tools, One Job**

Commas, brackets, and dashes can all set off extra (parenthetical) information — but each creates a different feel. Watch me use all three for the same information.

> Example: Commas (neutral): The kiwi, our national bird, is flightless. | Brackets (quiet aside): The kiwi (our national bird) is flightless. | Dashes (emphatic): The kiwi — our national bird — is flightless.

Same information, three different effects. Commas are the most neutral and common choice. Brackets feel like a quiet, almost whispered aside. Dashes draw attention and create drama. The choice depends on how much emphasis the information deserves.

*Tip: Default to commas unless you have a reason to choose otherwise: brackets for a quiet factual aside, dashes for emphasis or drama.*

**We Do — Choose the Right Mark**

Add parenthetical punctuation to each sentence, and justify whether commas, brackets, or dashes suit it best.
- Mount Cook the highest peak in New Zealand attracts climbers worldwide.
- My brother who is usually quite shy gave a brilliant speech.
- The final score a stunning 3-0 surprised everyone.

*Discussion prompt: Is this a neutral aside, a quiet fact, or something worth emphasising? Which punctuation matches that?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add the most appropriate parenthetical punctuation to each sentence and explain your choice.
- The Treaty of Waitangi signed in 1840 is central to New Zealand's history.
- My dog who never listens finally came when called.
- The exam worth 50% of the final grade is next week.
- ✦ Bonus: Write three sentences about the same fact, using commas in one, brackets in the second, and dashes in the third — explain how the feeling changes each time.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Parenthesis: commas, brackets, and dashes compared: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): "Commas, brackets, and dashes can all mark parenthetical (non-essential) information within a sentence. The choice between them signals different levels of formality and emphasis: commas are neutral, brackets are understated, and dashes are emphatic." This consolidates parenthesis punctuation taught separately across Term 1 and Term 2.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Choosing punctuation deliberately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to "use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect." This review lesson requires students to make an explicit, justified choice between three previously-separate punctuation tools, deepening their control over punctuation as a craft decision.


#### Wednesday: Embedding clauses for sophistication

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): writers combine multiple clause types (relative, subordinate, and participial) within a single sentence to create sophisticated, information-dense sentences appropriate for Years 5–6 writing.

**I Do — Stacking Clauses Together**

A sophisticated sentence can combine several clause types — relative, subordinate, and participial — within one sentence. Watch me build one, layer by layer.

> Example: Base: The scientist made a discovery. → + relative clause: The scientist, who had studied the reef for ten years, made a discovery. → + subordinate clause: The scientist, who had studied the reef for ten years, made a discovery that would change marine biology forever, because the species had never been recorded before.

I started simple, then added a relative clause (who had studied...), then a subordinate clause (because the species...). Each addition adds genuine information, not padding. The sentence stays grammatically correct throughout — I check it still makes sense after each addition.

*Tip: Add one clause at a time and re-read the whole sentence. If it becomes confusing or ungrammatical, simplify — don't just keep piling clauses on.*

**We Do — Build a Layered Sentence**

Build a sentence together, adding one clause type at a time.
- Base: The bridge collapsed. (add a relative clause about its age, then a subordinate clause explaining why)

*Discussion prompt: Does each addition add real information? Is the sentence still grammatically correct and clear?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Build a layered sentence for each base, adding at least two different clause types.
- Base: The expedition succeeded.
- Base: The painting sold for a record price.
- ✦ Bonus: Write one fully layered sentence (at least three clauses) about a topic of your choice. Read it aloud to check it's still clear, not just complicated.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Embedding multiple clause types: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity," including sentences that combine relative, subordinate, and participial clauses. This is one of the most demanding Phase 2 sentence-construction skills, drawing together a full year of grammar learning.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Checking complexity against clarity: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense." This lesson explicitly teaches that sentence sophistication must not come at the cost of clarity — students learn to test each addition against comprehensibility.


#### Thursday: Self-editing with a full checklist

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply a comprehensive, multi-pass editing checklist (punctuation, grammar, vocabulary, sentence variety, paragraph cohesion) to independently revise a piece of writing.

**I Do — One Pass Per Rule**

Professional writers don't try to fix everything at once. I'll model a full self-edit using one pass per category: punctuation, grammar, vocabulary, sentence variety, cohesion.

> Example: Checklist: ☐ Capitals & end punctuation ☐ Subject-verb agreement ☐ No vague words (nice, good, went) ☐ Varied sentence openers ☐ Paragraphs linked with connectives

Pass 1: I read only for punctuation, marking fixes. Pass 2: I read only for grammar errors. Pass 3: I hunt vague words and upgrade them. Pass 4: I check sentence openers for repetition. Pass 5: I check paragraph links. Five short, focused passes catch far more than one rushed read-through.

*Tip: Trying to fix everything in one read is why errors get missed. Slow down — one category, one pass.*

**We Do — Edit Together, One Pass at a Time**

Let's edit this paragraph together, doing one checklist pass at a time as a class.
- the dog ran fast it was a nice dog it went to the park the park was nice too everyone said it was a good day

*Discussion prompt: What did punctuation pass find? What did the vague-word pass find? What about sentence variety?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Edit this paragraph using the full five-pass checklist. Mark and rewrite all your corrections.
- my family went to the beach it was a good day we did lots of fun things the weather was nice we had a good time and went home tired
- ✦ Bonus: Apply the full five-pass checklist to a piece of your own writing from earlier this term.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Comprehensive self-editing checklist: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking punctuation, grammar, vocabulary, and structure." This lesson teaches the multi-pass strategy — one category per read-through — as the most reliable method for thorough self-editing.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge)* — Systematic revision strategies: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge): "Effective revision involves applying a systematic checklist across multiple read-throughs, rather than attempting to address all aspects of writing quality simultaneously." This consolidates every editing strategy taught across the year into one transferable habit.


### Week 9

#### Monday: Expanding noun phrases for detail

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): students apply pre- and post-modification of noun phrases within full sentences and paragraphs, choosing which nouns deserve expansion for maximum descriptive impact.

**I Do — Choosing Which Nouns Deserve Detail**

Not every noun needs expanding — that would be exhausting to read. Today we practise choosing the most important nouns in a passage to expand. Watch me decide.

> Example: Plain: A man walked into a room and saw a painting. → Selectively expanded: A tall, weathered man, his coat dripping from the storm outside, walked into the dim room and saw a painting that seemed to watch him back.

I didn't expand every noun — 'the room' got a little detail (dim), but I focused most of my effort on 'the man' (important character) and 'the painting' (the focus of the scene). Expanding everything would slow the pace too much.

*Tip: Ask: which 1–2 nouns in this sentence matter most to the reader? Save your detail for those.*

**We Do — Choose and Expand**

In this sentence, decide which noun is most important and expand only that one fully.
- A dog ran towards a boy near a tree.
- A ship sailed into a harbour past a lighthouse.
- A girl opened a door into a room.

*Discussion prompt: Which noun is the focus of this sentence? How much detail does it deserve compared to the others?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence, selectively expanding only the most important 1–2 nouns.
- A bird landed on a branch above a river.
- A car stopped outside a shop on a street.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 3-sentence description of an arrival scene (someone or something arriving somewhere), selectively expanding only the most important nouns in each sentence.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Selective noun phrase expansion: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "expand sentences by adding words, phrases, and clauses to add detail and description," while also exercising judgement about which elements of a sentence most need expansion. This extends Week 7's expanded noun phrase work into a genuine compositional choice.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Controlling pace through selective detail: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader." Over-expanding every noun slows pace and overwhelms the reader — this lesson teaches students to use detail strategically, a hallmark of more mature writing.


#### Tuesday: Passive voice in explanation writing

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): passive voice is the conventional choice in explanation and procedural writing when the process or outcome matters more than who performed the action.

**I Do — When Passive Voice Is the Right Choice**

We learned passive voice can weaken narrative writing — but in explanation and procedural writing, it's often the BEST choice, because the process matters more than who did it. Watch me apply it correctly.

> Example: Active (narrative feel): Workers mix the concrete and pour it into moulds. → Passive (explanation feel): The concrete is mixed and poured into moulds.

In an explanation of how concrete is made, we don't care WHO mixes it — we care about the process. Passive voice removes the unnecessary 'workers' and keeps focus on the steps. This is the opposite lesson from our earlier 'cut the passive voice' editing work — context decides which is correct.

*Tip: Ask: does this text type focus on a process/outcome (passive often works) or on characters taking action (active usually works better)?*

**We Do — Choose the Right Voice for the Text Type**

Decide whether each sentence works better as active or passive, based on its text type, and convert if needed.
- Explanation: A chef adds the yeast to the flour. (how bread is made)
- Narrative: The flour was added by the chef as the storm raged outside.
- Explanation: People recycle the bottles at the depot.

*Discussion prompt: Is this explanation (focus on process) or narrative (focus on character)? Which voice suits it better?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence in the voice that best suits its text type, and explain your choice.
- Explanation: People filter the water before it reaches our taps.
- Explanation: Engineers test the bridge for safety every year.
- Narrative: The cake was baked by Grandma with so much love.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 3-sentence explanation of how something works (e.g. how rain forms), using passive voice deliberately and correctly throughout.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Passive voice in explanation text type: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to make "deliberate grammatical choices appropriate to text type," recognising that passive voice is the conventional and often preferred construction in explanation and procedural writing, where process matters more than agent.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge)* — Voice and text-type convention: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge): "Different text types favour different grammatical conventions; explanation writing typically favours passive constructions that foreground process over agent." This lesson deliberately revisits and complicates Term 3 Week 2's active-voice editing lesson, teaching students that 'rules' depend on text-type purpose.


#### Wednesday: Sequencing sentences in recount and procedure

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge & Practice): time connectives (firstly, next, after that, meanwhile, finally) sequence events clearly in recount and procedural writing, signalling the order in which actions occur.

**I Do — Guiding the Reader Through Time**

Recount and procedural writing need clear time connectives to guide the reader through the sequence of events. Watch me sequence a set of jumbled steps.

> Example: Jumbled: We arrived at the beach. We packed our togs. We went swimming. We had lunch.

Sequenced: Firstly, we packed our togs. Then, we arrived at the beach. After that, we went swimming. Finally, we had lunch. The connectives make the order crystal clear and stop the reader from having to guess what happened when.

*Tip: Vary your time connectives — don't just use 'then' for every step. Try firstly, next, after that, meanwhile, eventually, finally.*

**We Do — Sequence Together**

Put these jumbled events in a logical order, adding a time connective to each.
- The seeds were watered every day. The seeds were planted in the soil. The seedlings were transplanted outside. The plant finally flowered.

*Discussion prompt: What is the correct order? Which connective best introduces each step?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a sequenced procedure or recount using these jumbled events, with a different time connective for each step.
- Jumbled: The bread rose. The dough was kneaded. The bread was baked. The ingredients were mixed.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 5-step recount of your morning routine, using a different time connective to introduce each step.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge)* — Time connectives in recount and procedure: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge): "Recount and procedural texts are organised sequentially, signalled to the reader through a range of time connectives (firstly, next, after that, meanwhile, finally). Sequencing connectives should be varied rather than repetitive." This is a core text-type structural feature at Phase 2.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Sequencing events clearly: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "organise ideas… using a range of connectives" appropriate to text type. This lesson gives students a varied bank of time connectives to replace the over-reliance on 'then' common in Years 5–6 recount writing.


#### Thursday: Peer editing for text-type structure

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply text-type-specific structural checklists when peer editing, checking that a partner's writing matches the expected structure for narrative, persuasive, or explanation text types.

**I Do — Editing for Structure, Not Just Sentences**

Today's edit isn't about commas or spelling — it's about whether the WHOLE piece follows its text type's expected structure. Watch me check a persuasive paragraph against a structural checklist.

> Example: Checklist for persuasive paragraph: ☐ Clear position stated early ☐ PEEL structure followed ☐ At least one persuasive connective ☐ Formal register maintained

I read the whole paragraph once for structure only — ignoring small grammar issues for now. Does it open with a clear position? Yes. Does it follow Point-Evidence-Explain-Link? Partially — there's no clear Explain sentence. I note this as the priority fix, more important right now than a missing comma.

*Tip: Structural problems matter more than small errors — fix the shape of the piece first, then polish sentence-level details.*

**We Do — Structural Check Together**

Check this persuasive paragraph against the structural checklist together. What's missing?
- Plastic bags should be banned. They take years to break down. Many shops have already stopped using them.

*Discussion prompt: Is there a clear position? Does it follow PEEL? Is anything structurally missing?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Swap a piece of persuasive or explanation writing with a partner. Check it against the relevant structural checklist and give one specific piece of feedback.
- ✦ Checklist (persuasive): clear position, PEEL structure, persuasive connectives, formal register.
- ✦ Checklist (explanation): clear introduction, logical cause-and-effect sequence, time/cause connectives.
- ✦ Bonus: Identify the single most important structural fix your partner's piece needs, and explain why it matters more than smaller errors.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Peer editing for structure: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "share writing with others and respond to feedback to improve their work," including feedback on text-type structure specifically, not only sentence-level accuracy. This lesson trains students to prioritise structural feedback over minor error-spotting.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Applying text-type checklists: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "organise ideas into paragraphs with a clear main idea" matching the conventions of the text type being written. This consolidates the term's persuasive (PEEL) and explanation (cause-effect) structural work into an applied peer-review skill.


### Week 10

#### Monday: Term review: choosing the best figurative device

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge & Practice): students review and select the most effective figurative device (simile, metaphor, personification, onomatopoeia, idiom) for a given descriptive purpose, consolidating the term's vocabulary work.

**I Do — One Sentence, Many Possible Devices**

This term we learned five figurative devices: simile, metaphor, personification, onomatopoeia, and idiom. Today, watch me choose the BEST one for a specific purpose, not just any one.

> Example: Describing a fierce wind: Simile — the wind howled like a wolf. Metaphor — the wind was a wolf, snarling at the windows. Personification — the wind tore at the rooftops in fury.
