All three work, but personification feels most powerful here because it makes the wind an active, threatening force throughout a whole passage — not just one comparison. I choose based on which device best sustains the mood across multiple sentences.

*Tip: Don't just pick the first device that comes to mind — try two or three, then choose the one that creates the strongest, clearest image.*

**We Do — Try Multiple Devices**

For each description, try at least two different figurative devices, then decide which works best and why.
- A calm lake at sunrise
- A crowded, chaotic market
- An old, creaking ship

*Discussion prompt: Which device created the clearest, most original image? Why does it suit this scene better than the others?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Choose the best figurative device for each scene and write one strong sentence using it.
- A roaring waterfall.
- A sleepy, quiet village at dusk.
- A blazing, uncontrolled bushfire.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short paragraph (4–5 sentences) describing a thunderstorm, deliberately combining at least three different figurative devices from this term.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Figurative language: term review: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): "Figurative language devices — including simile, metaphor, personification, onomatopoeia, and idiom — each create different effects for the reader. Skilled writers select the device best suited to their purpose rather than defaulting to a single familiar technique." This consolidates the full range of figurative language taught across Term 3.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Deliberate device selection: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to "select and use words and phrases, including figurative language, that are fit for purpose and audience." This review lesson moves students from being able to use each device individually to making a genuine craft choice between them.


#### Tuesday: Term review: mixed grammar

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): students apply and distinguish between this term's grammar concepts — active/passive voice, modal verbs, direct/reported speech, and conjunction types — in a mixed review.

**I Do — Spotting Which Rule Applies**

This term covered active/passive voice, modal verbs, direct/reported speech, and conjunction types. Today's challenge is recognising which rule applies to a given sentence. Watch me work through a mixed set.

> Example: "The trophy must have been won by the strongest team," she said.

I check each feature: 'must have been' = a modal verb (must) combined with passive voice (been won). 'she said' with speech marks = direct speech. This single sentence uses three of our term's grammar concepts at once — recognising them is the skill we're practising today.

*Tip: Work through one grammar category at a time: first check voice, then modals, then speech type, then conjunctions — don't try to spot everything in one glance.*

**We Do — Identify the Features**

Identify which grammar features (voice, modal verbs, speech type, conjunction type) appear in each sentence.
- She said that she might be late.
- The cake was baked by my grandmother, although she rarely cooks.
- "We could win this," the coach shouted, "if we work together."

*Discussion prompt: Is this active or passive? Is there a modal verb? Direct or reported speech? Coordinating or subordinating conjunction?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Identify the grammar features in each sentence, then write one original sentence combining at least two features from this term.
- Identify the features: "The match was cancelled because of the storm, and everyone went home disappointed."
- Identify the features: "You should always check your work," the teacher reminded them.
- ✦ Bonus: Write one sentence that combines passive voice, a modal verb, AND reported speech.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Mixed grammar review: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): consolidates Term 3's grammar strand — voice (active/passive), modal verbs, direct and reported speech, and conjunction types — requiring students to recognise multiple grammatical features operating within a single sentence, a key indicator of secure grammatical understanding at the end of Phase 2.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Applying grammar knowledge flexibly: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write grammatically correct sentences using a range of tenses and structures." This review lesson tests flexible application rather than isolated practice, mirroring how grammar features combine in real, independent writing.


#### Wednesday: Term review: constructing a flexible paragraph

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice) & Composing (Practice): students construct a single paragraph that deliberately applies a range of this term's sentence-building techniques — varied openers, embedded clauses, semicolons, and rhetorical questions.

**I Do — Bringing It All Together**

Today we build one paragraph using as many of this term's sentence techniques as possible, while keeping it clear and purposeful. Watch me plan and write one.

> Example: Topic: arriving at a new school.

I plan first: opener type? (-ing clause). A semicolon link? A rhetorical question to close? Draft: "Clutching her new backpack tightly, Mere stepped through the gates for the first time; everything felt unfamiliar, from the unfamiliar faces to the strange smell of fresh paint. Would anyone even notice her standing there?" I checked off three techniques deliberately, not by accident.

*Tip: Plan your techniques BEFORE drafting — decide which 2–3 you'll use, then write with that plan in mind, rather than hoping they appear naturally.*

**We Do — Plan and Build Together**

As a class, plan which techniques to use, then build a paragraph together about this topic.
- Topic: a sudden change in weather during a sports match. Plan: opener type? semicolon or colon? a figurative device? a rhetorical question?

*Discussion prompt: Which techniques work together naturally? Does the paragraph still read smoothly, or does it feel forced?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Plan and write a paragraph (5–6 sentences) on the topic given, deliberately including at least four different techniques from this term (e.g. varied opener, semicolon, figurative device, rhetorical question, modal verb).
- Topic: discovering something unexpected.
- ✦ Bonus: Swap with a partner and identify which techniques they used — did you find all of them?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Deliberate combination of sentence techniques: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to "write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity," demonstrated here through planned, deliberate combination of multiple techniques within a single coherent paragraph — the culmination of the term's sentence-building strand.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Planning before drafting: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to "use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader," including planning specific techniques before drafting rather than relying on them to occur incidentally. This is a key independent-writing habit for Years 5–6.


#### Thursday: Whole-piece edit: applying the term's checklist

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply the full Term 3 editing checklist (figurative language, voice, register, cohesion, sentence variety, punctuation) to a complete piece of their own writing as the term's culminating revision task.

**I Do — The Complete Edit**

Today is the term's biggest edit — applying everything we've learned to a whole piece of writing. Watch me model the full checklist on a sample paragraph.

> Example: Full checklist: ☐ Figurative language used purposefully ☐ Voice (active/passive) suits text type ☐ Register matches audience ☐ Paragraphs cohesively linked ☐ Sentence openers varied ☐ Punctuation accurate (including speech, semicolons, parenthesis)

I work through each item on a sample paragraph, one at a time, making at least one specific improvement per category where needed — not just ticking boxes, but genuinely revising. This is exactly the process a published writer goes through before a piece is finished.

*Tip: Don't rush the final categories because you're tired — punctuation and cohesion are just as important as the first checks.*

**We Do — Edit a Sample Together**

Apply the full checklist to this short paragraph as a class, fixing at least three issues.
- The fire was started by accident. It was bad. Everyone got out ok. Firefighters came and put it out it was a relief for everyone.

*Discussion prompt: What does each checklist category reveal? Which fix matters most?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Apply the full Term 3 editing checklist to a piece of your own writing from this term (or the paragraph you wrote on Wednesday). Mark every change you make.
- ✦ Work through each checklist category in turn: figurative language, voice, register, cohesion, sentence variety, punctuation.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short reflection — which checklist category improved your writing the most this term, and why?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Whole-piece systematic editing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to "reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking vocabulary, punctuation, and structure." This culminating lesson requires students to apply a comprehensive checklist independently to a complete piece, demonstrating the full range of Phase 2 writing process skills.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge)* — Reflecting on growth as a writer: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge): "Students reflect on their development as writers, identifying which strategies have most improved their writing." This metacognitive reflection closes the term, connecting explicit skill instruction to students' growing self-awareness as writers.


---

## Term 4

### Week 1

#### Monday: Precise verb choice: replacing weak verbs

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): precise, specific verbs carry more meaning than vague general ones; replacing weak verbs (went, said, did, got, moved) with precise alternatives makes writing more vivid and economical.

**I Do — One Strong Verb Does the Work of Three Weak Ones**

Weak verbs like 'went', 'said', 'did', 'moved', and 'got' are placeholder verbs — they tell us almost nothing about HOW something happened. Watch me replace them with precise alternatives that carry the scene.

> Example: She went quickly across the room and said in a loud voice that she was angry. → She stormed across the room and snapped that she was furious.

'Went quickly' = two words doing a weak job. 'Stormed' = one word that gives direction, speed, AND mood. 'Said in a loud voice that she was angry' = seven words. 'Snapped' = one word that gives volume, emotion, AND manner. Precise verbs make writing leaner and more powerful simultaneously.

*Tip: When you find a weak verb, ask: HOW did they do it? The answer is usually your precise verb (crept, bolted, muttered, slammed).*

**We Do — Upgrade the Verb**

Replace the weak verb in each sentence with a more precise one.
- The cat moved along the fence in the dark.
- He said something under his breath and went out.
- She did the maths quickly and got the right answer.

*Discussion prompt: What does HOW it happened tell us — is it fast, slow, fearful, angry? What single verb captures that?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Replace every weak verb (went, said, got, did, moved, looked) in each sentence with a precise alternative.
- The dog went to the door and looked at us.
- She said she was going and got her bag.
- He moved through the crowd and did his best to get past.
- ✦ Bonus: Take three consecutive sentences from a piece of your own writing. Find every weak verb and upgrade each one.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Precise verb choice: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to select and use words and phrases that are fit for purpose and audience, specifically choosing precise, specific verbs over vague placeholders. Precise verb choice is one of the highest-leverage vocabulary skills for Years 5–6 writing quality.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Economy and precision in word choice: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including selecting vocabulary that is economical — doing more work with fewer words. This lesson establishes precise verb choice as the cornerstone vocabulary skill of Term 4.


#### Tuesday: Colons — introducing lists and elaboration

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): a colon introduces what follows — a list, an explanation, or an elaboration of the idea in the main clause; the clause before the colon must be a complete sentence.

**I Do — The Colon Announces What's Coming**

A colon says: here comes the detail, the list, or the explanation. The clause before the colon must be a complete sentence. Watch me use it two ways.

> Example: List: She packed everything she needed: a torch, a sleeping bag, and three days' worth of food. | Elaboration: There was only one problem: nobody had thought to bring a map.

Before each colon I have a complete sentence. After the colon comes either a list (items separated by commas) or an elaboration that explains or expands the main clause idea. I don't put a capital after a colon unless it's a proper noun.

*Tip: Test: can the words before the colon stand alone as a complete sentence? If not, you need more before the colon — or replace it with a comma.*

**We Do — Place the Colon**

Add a colon to each sentence in the right place, then check the words before it form a complete sentence.
- There are three rules in this classroom respect others, work hard, and stay curious.
- The forecast was grim heavy rain, strong winds, and possible flooding.
- She had one ambition to represent New Zealand at the Olympics.

*Discussion prompt: Where does the list or elaboration begin? Is everything before that point a complete sentence?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add a colon to each sentence and check it is correctly placed.
- The museum contained many fascinating things ancient fossils, hand-painted maps, and a real Viking helmet.
- His decision surprised everyone he chose to walk away from the prize.
- The recipe requires four key ingredients flour, eggs, butter, and sugar.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two original sentences using colons — one introducing a list, one introducing an elaboration.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge)* — Colons for lists and elaboration: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Knowledge): A colon introduces what follows — a list, an explanation, or an elaboration of the main clause idea. The clause preceding the colon must be grammatically complete. The colon is a key Phase 2 punctuation mark expected in more sophisticated explanation and persuasive writing.
- *Writing — Punctuation (Practice)* — Applying colons accurately: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Punctuation (Practice): Students are expected to use a range of punctuation accurately and for effect, including the colon for introducing lists and elaboration in explanation and persuasive writing.


#### Wednesday: Using a colon to introduce and expand an idea

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): students build sentences that use a colon to set up a main idea and then expand or clarify it in the second part, creating a sophisticated two-part sentence structure common in formal writing.

**I Do — Building the Two-Part Sentence**

The most powerful use of a colon is in a two-part sentence where part one makes a claim and part two proves, explains, or dramatically reveals it. Watch me build one.

> Example: Claim then proof: The ancient kauri was extraordinary: it had stood for over 2,000 years, surviving storms, drought, and the arrival of humans. | Build-up then reveal: She opened the door and stopped: the room was completely empty.

In the first example, the colon promises an explanation of 'extraordinary' and delivers one. In the second, the colon creates a pause that makes the reveal more dramatic. Both structures set up reader expectation with part one, then satisfy it with part two.

*Tip: Read part one aloud: does it feel like it's promising something? If yes, the colon is probably right. If part one already feels complete and closed, a full stop may suit better.*

**We Do — Complete the Two-Part Sentence**

Complete each sentence starter with a strong part-two after the colon.
- The team had one weakness:
- The explorer made a startling discovery:
- There was only one thing left to do:

*Discussion prompt: What does part one promise? Does part two deliver a strong explanation, detail, or reveal?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write the full two-part sentence for each prompt, using a colon to link them.
- Start with: 'The storm had one final surprise'
- Start with: 'Every great piece of writing shares one quality'
- Start with: 'She had been preparing for this moment for ten years'
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph opening (3–4 sentences) where the first sentence uses a colon for dramatic elaboration.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Two-part colon sentence structure: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity, including the two-part colon sentence where the first clause makes a claim or builds anticipation and the second clause provides elaboration or revelation.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Building anticipation and revelation: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including structural choices that create anticipation. The two-part colon sentence is one of the most reliable tools for creating a moment of revelation or emphasis in both narrative and persuasive writing.


#### Thursday: Editing: upgrading weak verbs throughout a passage

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students systematically identify and replace weak, vague verbs throughout a whole passage, applying the precise verb skill from Monday in a sustained editing context.

**I Do — The Weak Verb Hunt**

Today I'll show you how to edit a whole passage for weak verbs — methodically, not randomly. I highlight every weak verb first, then replace each one before moving on.

> Example: Jake went to school. He looked at his friends and said hello. They all went into class and did their work quietly. The teacher looked up and said they could go.

Highlight: went (x2), looked (x2), said (x2), did. Replace: Jake trudged / He glanced at his friends and nodded. They filed into class and worked quietly. The teacher glanced up and murmured they were free to go. Now every verb earns its place.

*Tip: Highlight ALL weak verbs before changing any of them — this stops you from improving the first sentence and forgetting the rest.*

**We Do — Highlight Then Replace**

Highlight every weak verb in this passage first, then replace them one at a time.
- The soldiers went across the field. They moved carefully because they didn't want to be seen. The leader said they should stop. Everyone stopped and looked around nervously.

*Discussion prompt: How many weak verbs did we find? Which replacement verb best captures the mood — tense, cautious, military?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Edit this passage by replacing every weak verb with a more precise one. Aim to cut word count at the same time.
- The girl went into the kitchen and looked in the fridge. She got out some food and did some cooking. She said to herself that it smelled good. Her mum came in and said she was impressed.
- ✦ Bonus: Count the total words before and after your edit. How many words did you save while making the writing stronger?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Systematic weak-verb editing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to reread and revise their writing, making changes to vocabulary to improve clarity and impact. This lesson teaches a systematic, two-step editing strategy — highlight all targets first, then replace — applied specifically to weak verbs across a whole passage.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Verb precision across a sustained passage: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Sustaining precise verb choice across an entire passage — not just a single sentence — is the more demanding and authentic application of this skill at Phase 2.


### Week 2

#### Monday: Pathetic fallacy: weather and setting reflecting mood

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge) & Composing (Practice): pathetic fallacy is a literary technique in which the natural environment or weather reflects or reinforces the emotional mood of a scene or character.

**I Do — When the World Matches the Feeling**

Pathetic fallacy is when the weather or setting mirrors the mood of the scene — the storm that breaks during a crisis, the sunshine that appears at a moment of hope. Watch me use it deliberately.

> Example: Neutral: It was raining as she read the letter. → Pathetic fallacy: The rain hammered against the windows as she read the letter, each drop driving harder as if sharing her despair.

In the neutral version, rain is just a fact. In the pathetic fallacy version, the rain actively reflects and amplifies her emotional state. I chose 'hammered' and 'driving harder' to match 'despair' — the word choice in the weather description mirrors the emotional register of the scene.

*Tip: The weather shouldn't just be present — it should seem to participate in the mood. Match your weather verbs and adjectives to the emotion of the scene.*

**We Do — Match Weather to Mood**

Write a pathetic fallacy description of the weather that reinforces the mood given.
- Mood: dread and foreboding (a character approaches an abandoned house)
- Mood: joy and relief (a team wins an important match)
- Mood: loneliness and isolation (someone waits at an empty bus stop)

*Discussion prompt: What weather suits this mood? What verbs and adjectives make the weather seem to share the feeling?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write 1–2 sentences of pathetic fallacy for each scene.
- A character receives unexpected good news on a grey morning.
- A villain makes their entrance during a hot, still afternoon.
- Two old friends meet again after many years apart.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short paragraph (4–5 sentences) opening a story, where pathetic fallacy in the first two sentences sets the emotional tone for everything that follows.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Pathetic fallacy: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): Pathetic fallacy is a literary technique in which the natural environment or weather is described as reflecting or participating in the emotional atmosphere of a scene. Effective use requires matching weather vocabulary — verbs and adjectives — to the emotional register being conveyed. This is a key narrative craft technique at Year 5–6.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Creating mood through setting: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies, including figurative language and setting description, to create texts that engage the reader. Pathetic fallacy is the deliberate integration of setting and mood — a hallmark of more accomplished narrative writing at Phase 2.


#### Tuesday: Verb tense consistency in narrative

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): narrative writing must maintain a consistent past or present tense throughout; tense shifts within a passage are errors unless used deliberately for effect.

**I Do — Staying in One Tense**

Most narrative writing is written consistently in either the past tense or the present tense. Accidentally switching between them mid-passage is one of the most common errors at Years 5–6. Watch me find and fix tense shifts.

> Example: She walked into the room and sees a shadow by the window. She stops and her heart races. She had looked around but nothing moves.

'Walked' = past. 'Sees', 'stops', 'races' = present — tense shift. 'Had looked' = past perfect (unnecessary here). Fix: She walked into the room and saw a shadow by the window. She stopped and her heart raced. She looked around but nothing moved. All consistent past tense now.

*Tip: Before editing for tense, decide: is this piece past tense or present tense? Then read through looking ONLY for verbs, checking every one matches your decision.*

**We Do — Find and Fix the Tense Shifts**

Find every tense error in this passage and fix it to maintain consistent past tense.
- The door creaks open and Hemi steps inside. He looks around the dark room. A noise comes from upstairs. He grabbed his torch and moves to the stairs.

*Discussion prompt: Which verbs are in the wrong tense? What is the consistent tense this passage should use?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Edit this passage to make it consistently past tense throughout.
- The team arrives at the stadium early. They warm up on the field and the coach gives them a talk. Everyone had felt nervous, but when the whistle blew they play their best game of the season.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph of your own in present tense (as if the reader is watching a scene unfold right now) and check it is fully consistent.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Verb tense consistency: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): Narrative writing must maintain a consistent verb tense — past or present — throughout a passage. Unintentional tense shifts are grammatical errors that disrupt the reader's experience. Deliberate tense shifts such as past perfect for flashback must be clearly controlled and purposeful.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing for tense consistency: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking verb tense. This lesson makes tense consistency an explicit editing skill — deciding the tense first, then reading specifically for verb forms.


#### Wednesday: Past perfect for flashback

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): the past perfect tense (had + past participle) signals that an action was completed before the main narrative past tense; it is the grammatical tool for writing flashback sequences.

**I Do — Going Further Back in Time**

When we're writing in past tense and want to refer to something that happened even earlier — a flashback — we shift into the past perfect (had + past participle). Watch me build a flashback using it.

> Example: Main narrative (past): She stared at the old photograph. Flashback (past perfect): She had found it tucked inside her grandmother's journal, on the morning everything had changed. Return to narrative: Now, ten years later, she finally understood what it meant.

The flashback uses 'had found' and 'had changed' — past perfect — to signal these events happened BEFORE 'stared'. When returning to the main narrative, I switch back to simple past. The tense change IS the signal to the reader that we've moved in time.

*Tip: You only need a few past perfect verbs to establish a flashback. Once the reader is clearly in the past, you can drop back to simple past for the body of the flashback.*

**We Do — Build the Flashback**

Use past perfect to add a short flashback to each sentence.
- She looked at the trophy on the shelf. (Add a flashback to the day she won it.)
- He paused at the gate of the old house. (Add a flashback to his childhood there.)

*Discussion prompt: What past perfect verbs open the flashback? How do you signal the return to the main narrative?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a short paragraph (3–5 sentences) for each prompt, using past perfect to signal a flashback and simple past to return.
- A character finds a faded letter and remembers the day it was written.
- An athlete stands at the start line and recalls her first ever race.
- ✦ Bonus: Take a piece of narrative writing you've done this year and add a flashback sentence using past perfect to deepen the backstory.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Past perfect for flashback: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): The past perfect tense (had + past participle) signals that an action was completed before the point established by the main narrative past tense. It is the grammatical mechanism for writing flashback sequences, allowing writers to move back in time within a past-tense narrative.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Controlling narrative time: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to write grammatically correct sentences using a range of tenses and to use tense deliberately as a structural tool. The past perfect flashback is a significant expansion of students' narrative toolkit — allowing them to manage non-linear storytelling at the sentence-grammar level.


#### Thursday: Editing for tense consistency

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply a tense-consistency editing pass to a multi-paragraph narrative, identifying unintentional tense shifts, distinguishing them from deliberate past perfect flashbacks, and correcting errors systematically.

**I Do — The Tense Edit Pass**

Today I'll model the tense editing pass on a full paragraph — circling every verb, labelling it (simple past / past perfect / present), deciding if shifts are errors or intentional, and fixing the errors.

> Example: The explorers push through the undergrowth. It had been three days since they had lost the path. Now the forest grows darker around them. Last year, Mia had studied this very region — she was their only hope.

Verbs: 'push' (present — error), 'had been' (past perfect — intentional), 'had lost' (past perfect — intentional), 'grows' (present — error), 'had studied' (past perfect — intentional). Fix: 'pushed', 'grew'. The past perfect verbs stay because they correctly reach back in time.

*Tip: Circle verbs in pencil first, label each one, THEN erase and fix — never edit while circling or you'll lose track of the pattern.*

**We Do — Circle, Label, Fix**

Together, circle every verb in this passage, label it (simple past / past perfect / present), and fix any unintentional tense shifts.
- Aroha runs toward the finish line. She had trained for six months for this moment. Her legs ache but she keeps going. Back in January, she had nearly quit. Now she crossed the line first.

*Discussion prompt: Which verbs are errors? Which past perfect verbs are intentional? How do you tell the difference?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Edit this passage for tense consistency. Fix unintentional shifts — keep intentional past perfect verbs.
- The old lighthouse stood at the edge of the cliffs. For years, it had guided ships through the dangerous strait. Now, the light was broken and the keeper has long since gone. He had left one winter when a storm had damaged the lens beyond repair. Today the building sits empty, its windows dark.
- ✦ Bonus: Swap a piece of narrative writing with a partner. Each of you do a tense edit pass and report back what you found.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Tense consistency editing pass: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to reread and revise their writing to check accuracy, including verb tense. This lesson makes tense editing a deliberate, methodical pass — circle all verbs, label, then fix — distinguishing between unintentional errors and purposeful past perfect flashbacks.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Distinguishing error from intention in tense: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to use tense deliberately for effect, which requires the meta-skill of distinguishing an intentional past perfect construction from an accidental tense shift. This editing lesson develops exactly that judgement within the context of whole-passage revision.


### Week 3

#### Monday: Emotive language in persuasive writing

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge & Practice): emotive language deliberately targets the reader's feelings — using words with strong emotional connotations — to make a persuasive argument feel more urgent, personal, and compelling.

**I Do — Making the Reader Feel the Argument**

Emotive language doesn't just state facts — it makes the reader feel something about them. Watch me compare a neutral and an emotive version of the same argument.

> Example: Neutral: Some animals are kept in small spaces in zoos. This is not always good for them. → Emotive: Magnificent wild creatures are condemned to pace the same few square metres for their entire lives — robbed of everything nature intended for them.

'Animals' becomes 'magnificent wild creatures'. 'Small spaces' becomes 'the same few square metres'. 'Not always good' becomes 'condemned', 'robbed' — strong verbs with very negative connotations. The facts are the same — the emotional charge is completely different.

*Tip: Choose emotive words that match your audience's values — what do THEY care about? Tap into those feelings specifically.*

**We Do — Upgrade to Emotive**

Rewrite each neutral statement using emotive language that targets the reader's feelings.
- Some children don't have enough food.
- Old people sometimes feel alone.
- The native forest is being cut down.

*Discussion prompt: What emotions should the reader feel? Which words or phrases most powerfully trigger those feelings?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each statement using emotive language to make it more persuasive.
- Plastic bags are found in the ocean.
- Animals are tested on in laboratories.
- Children spend a lot of time on screens.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two versions of a persuasive opening sentence about a cause you care about — one neutral, one emotive. Explain what each version does differently to the reader.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Emotive language in persuasion: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): Emotive language deliberately selects words with strong emotional connotations to make persuasive writing feel urgent and personal, targeting the reader's feelings rather than only their reason. Emotive vocabulary choices are matched to the values and concerns of the intended audience.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Selecting words for emotional impact: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to select and use words and phrases that are fit for purpose and audience, specifically selecting vocabulary for deliberate emotional impact in persuasive writing. This extends Term 3's connotation work into the persuasive writing context.


#### Tuesday: Conditional sentences

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): conditional sentences express situations and their consequences using if/unless/provided that; students distinguish real (first) from unreal (second) conditionals and use them in persuasive and explanation writing.

**I Do — What Depends on What**

Conditional sentences link a condition to a consequence. First conditional (real/possible): If + present simple, will + infinitive. Second conditional (unreal/hypothetical): If + past simple, would + infinitive. Watch me use both.

> Example: First (real): If we reduce plastic use, we will see cleaner oceans within a decade. | Second (unreal): If every child had access to books, the world would be a very different place.

First conditional = a realistic possibility and its likely result. Second conditional = a hypothetical scenario and its imagined result. In persuasive writing, first conditionals make arguments feel achievable; second conditionals paint a vivid picture of what could be.

*Tip: Check the tense pair: If + present then will (first). If + past then would (second). Mixing them creates an error.*

**We Do — Build the Conditional**

Complete each conditional sentence with the correct form, then label it first or second.
- If schools banned single-use plastics, they ___ (reduce) their environmental footprint.
- If every student reads for 20 minutes a day, their vocabulary ___ (improve).
- If there were more cycle paths, more people ___ (choose) to cycle to work.

*Discussion prompt: Is this a real/likely scenario (first) or a hypothetical one (second)? What verb form does each need?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a conditional sentence for each persuasive topic, choosing first or second conditional and explaining your choice.
- Topic: reducing food waste.
- Topic: planting more native trees.
- Topic: giving students more choice in what they study.
- ✦ Bonus: Write two persuasive sentences about the same topic — one using a first conditional and one using a second conditional. Explain how each creates a different effect on the reader.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Conditional sentences: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): Conditional sentences link a condition to a consequence. First conditional (if + present simple, will + infinitive) expresses a real or likely possibility; second conditional (if + past simple, would + infinitive) expresses a hypothetical or unreal scenario. Both are important tools in persuasive writing at Phase 2.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Using conditionals in persuasive writing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to write grammatically correct sentences using a range of structures, including conditional sentences used deliberately for persuasive effect — first conditional for achievable arguments, second conditional for vivid hypothetical scenarios.


#### Wednesday: Counter-argument and rebuttal sentence structure

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Text Structure (Practice): a sophisticated persuasive text acknowledges the opposing view (counter-argument) and then dismisses or qualifies it (rebuttal); students practise the structural pattern for doing this within a single paragraph.

**I Do — Showing Both Sides, Winning One**

A strong persuasive writer doesn't ignore the other side — they raise it themselves and then knock it down. This shows intellectual honesty and makes the argument more credible. Watch me build a counter-argument and rebuttal.

> Example: Counter: Some people argue that banning plastic bags is inconvenient and costly for businesses. Rebuttal: However, the long-term environmental cost of plastic pollution — estimated at billions of dollars in ocean clean-up — far outweighs any short-term inconvenience.

I raised the opposing view fairly. Then I used 'however' to pivot and answered it with evidence that outweighs the objection. The rebuttal must be STRONGER than the counter-argument — if it isn't, I haven't won the point.

*Tip: Signal words for counter-argument: 'Some argue that', 'Critics claim'. Signal words for rebuttal: 'However', 'Nevertheless', 'Yet', 'Despite this'.*

**We Do — Build the Counter-Rebuttal Pair**

For each position, build a counter-argument sentence and then a rebuttal sentence.
- Position: School uniforms should be compulsory.
- Position: Students should have phones allowed in class.

*Discussion prompt: What is the strongest objection someone would make? Is the rebuttal stronger than the objection?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a full counter-argument and rebuttal paragraph (3–4 sentences) for the given position.
- Position: All schools should have a vegetarian-only canteen.
- Position: Homework should be banned for students under 12.
- ✦ Bonus: Take a PEEL paragraph you wrote earlier this term and add a counter-argument and rebuttal to it, making it into a fuller, more sophisticated persuasive paragraph.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Counter-argument and rebuttal: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to organise ideas into paragraphs with a clear main idea, supported by relevant detail and reasoning, including in persuasive writing the acknowledgement and dismissal of opposing views. Counter-argument and rebuttal is an advanced structural expectation for Year 5–6 persuasive writing.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Building credibility through balance: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including in persuasive writing the strategy of conceding then rebutting, which builds the writer's credibility and authority.


#### Thursday: Editing: strengthening a weak persuasive argument

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students diagnose the specific weaknesses in a persuasive paragraph (vague evidence, missing rebuttal, informal register, weak connectives) and make targeted revisions to strengthen it.

**I Do — Diagnosing What's Weak**

Not all persuasive problems are the same. Watch me read a weak persuasive paragraph and name exactly what's wrong before fixing anything.

> Example: I think everyone should recycle because it's really important. Lots of people already do it. Some people don't bother but they should. Recycling is just a good thing to do.

Diagnosis: (1) Informal register — 'I think', 'really', 'just'. (2) No specific evidence — 'lots of people' is vague. (3) Counter-rebuttal is present but undeveloped. (4) No persuasive connectives. (5) No PEEL structure. Now I know exactly what to fix, in order of importance.

*Tip: Always diagnose BEFORE revising — if you just start rewriting, you might fix one problem and miss four others.*

**We Do — Diagnose Together**

Read this paragraph and produce a diagnosis list before making any changes.
- Kids should be able to choose what they eat at school. The food at school isn't very nice. Some people say healthy food is important but lots of kids don't like it. So schools should let kids choose.

*Discussion prompt: How many problems can we name? Which is the most serious? What is the priority order for fixing them?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Diagnose then rewrite this paragraph to fix all its weaknesses.
- Zoos are bad for animals. Animals don't like being in cages. People argue that zoos help conservation but animals are still unhappy. We should close zoos.
- ✦ Bonus: Diagnose a piece of your own persuasive writing from earlier this term using the same checklist. Write a brief diagnosis, then make the most important fixes.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Diagnosing persuasive weaknesses: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to reread and revise their writing, making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact. This lesson teaches diagnosis as a deliberate pre-revision step — naming specific weaknesses before beginning revision.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Targeted persuasive revision: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to organise ideas into paragraphs with a clear main idea, supported by relevant detail and reasoning. This editing lesson consolidates all of Term 4's persuasive skills — register, PEEL, counter-rebuttal, connectives, and emotive language — into a single diagnosis-and-repair process.


### Week 4

#### Monday: Technical vocabulary in explanation writing

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge & Practice): explanation writing uses precise technical vocabulary specific to the topic being explained; students learn to introduce technical terms with definitions and use them consistently.

**I Do — Using the Right Words for the Job**

Explanation writing uses technical terms — the exact, agreed vocabulary of a subject. Watch me introduce a technical term correctly and then use it consistently.

> Example: Weak: Water turns into gas when it gets hot enough. → With technical vocab: When water reaches 100 degrees Celsius, it undergoes evaporation — the process by which liquid water converts into water vapour. This vapour rises and forms part of the water cycle.

I introduced the technical terms 'evaporation' and 'water cycle' with built-in definitions, then used them consistently instead of replacing them with vaguer words. Technical writing is precise because its audience expects accuracy.

*Tip: Introduce each technical term with a definition the first time, then use it consistently. Don't replace it with a vague synonym — that reduces precision.*

**We Do — Introduce and Use**

Upgrade each sentence by introducing a technical term with a definition, then use it in a follow-up sentence.
- The moon's pull makes the ocean water move up and down. (technical term: tidal force / tides)
- Plants use sunlight to make food. (technical terms: photosynthesis, chlorophyll)

*Discussion prompt: What is the correct technical term? How do we define it for the reader the first time?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence by introducing the appropriate technical term and defining it, then write a second sentence using the term correctly.
- Rocks are broken into smaller pieces by weather and water. (key term: erosion/weathering)
- The heart pumps blood around the body. (key terms: circulation, artery/vein)
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 4-sentence explanation paragraph about a process you know well, using at least two technical terms correctly introduced with definitions.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Technical vocabulary in explanation writing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): Explanation writing uses precise technical vocabulary specific to its subject. Technical terms are introduced with clear definitions when first used and then applied consistently — not replaced with vague synonyms — because precision is central to the explanation text type at Phase 2.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Consistent use of technical terms: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to select and use words and phrases that are fit for purpose and audience, including accurate technical vocabulary appropriate to explanation writing. This lesson makes technical term introduction and consistent usage an explicit writing strategy.


#### Tuesday: Present tense conventions in explanation writing

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): explanation writing typically uses the simple present tense because it describes how things generally work (timeless truth), not what happened on one specific occasion.

**I Do — Timeless Truth vs. One-Time Event**

Explanation writing describes how things GENERALLY work — so it uses the simple present tense (timeless truth), not the past tense (which suggests it only happened once). Watch me compare.

> Example: Past tense (narrative): The volcano erupted and lava flowed down the hillside. → Present tense (explanation): When pressure builds beneath the earth's surface, magma forces its way through cracks and erupts as lava.

The past tense version sounds like a specific historical event. The present tense version describes the general, repeatable process — which is what explanation writing does. 'Forces', 'erupts' = timeless present = this is how it always works.

*Tip: If your explanation says what happened, switch it to say what happens. That one word change shifts a narrative sentence into an explanation sentence.*

**We Do — Switch to Explanation Tense**

Rewrite each past-tense narrative sentence as a present-tense explanation sentence.
- The roots of the plant absorbed water from the soil and carried it to the leaves.
- The caterpillar made a cocoon and changed into a butterfly inside.
- The snow melted when the temperature rose above zero.

*Discussion prompt: Does the rewritten sentence describe a general, repeatable process? Does it still make sense in the present tense?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each sentence in the timeless present tense appropriate for explanation writing.
- The earthquake shook the buildings and caused cracks in the roads.
- The bee flew from flower to flower and collected pollen on its legs.
- The light passed through the prism and split into seven colours.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 3-sentence explanation of how something natural works, consistently in the timeless present tense.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Present tense in explanation writing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): Explanation writing typically uses the simple present tense to convey timeless truth — describing how processes generally work, not what happened on one specific occasion. This distinguishes explanation from narrative and recount text types, which use past tense for specific events.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge)* — Text type and tense convention: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge): Different text types use different tense conventions: narrative and recount use past tense for specific events; explanation uses simple present for general processes; procedure uses imperative or present tense for instructions. Understanding tense as a text-type signal is a key Phase 2 metalinguistic understanding.


#### Wednesday: Structuring a full explanation paragraph

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Text Structure (Practice): students build a complete, well-structured explanation paragraph with a topic sentence, logically sequenced cause-and-effect sentences using technical vocabulary, and a concluding link sentence.

**I Do — The Explanation Paragraph Blueprint**

A well-structured explanation paragraph follows a clear pattern: topic sentence (what you're explaining) → cause-and-effect body sentences (the how/why) → concluding link (the outcome or significance). Watch me build one.

> Example: Topic: How does rain form? Topic sentence: Rain forms through a continuous cycle of evaporation, condensation, and precipitation. Body: When the sun heats surface water, evaporation occurs — liquid water converts to water vapour and rises into the atmosphere. As the vapour cools at higher altitudes, it condenses around tiny dust particles, forming clouds. When cloud droplets combine and grow heavy enough, they fall as precipitation. Link: This cycle repeats continuously, driven by solar energy, and is essential to all life on Earth.

Every sentence does a specific job. Topic sentence names the process and its stages. Body sentences use cause-and-effect connectives and technical terms. Concluding link gives the broader significance.

*Tip: Read your topic sentence and your concluding link together. They should form a logical bracket around the paragraph.*

**We Do — Build Together**

Together, build a complete explanation paragraph for the topic given.
- Topic: How does a rainbow form? (Key terms: refraction, spectrum, wavelength)

*Discussion prompt: What goes in the topic sentence? What are the key cause-and-effect steps? What does the concluding link say?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a complete explanation paragraph for the topic given, using the full blueprint: topic sentence → cause-and-effect body → concluding link.
- Topic: How do volcanoes form? (Key terms: magma, tectonic plates, eruption, lava)
- ✦ Bonus: Write an explanation paragraph on a topic from another subject you're studying at the moment, applying the same blueprint and using correct technical vocabulary.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Structuring an explanation paragraph: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to organise ideas into paragraphs with a clear main idea, supported by relevant detail and reasoning specific to the explanation text type. This lesson gives students an explicit, repeatable blueprint — topic sentence, cause-and-effect body, concluding link — as a transferable cross-curricular writing skill.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge)* — Explanation paragraph structure: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Knowledge): A well-structured explanation paragraph opens with a topic sentence naming the process to be explained, develops through logically sequenced cause-and-effect sentences using appropriate technical vocabulary, and closes with a linking sentence that states the outcome or broader significance.


#### Thursday: Editing explanation writing for clarity and accuracy

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply an explanation-specific editing checklist — checking tense, technical vocabulary, cause-and-effect connectives, paragraph structure, and factual accuracy — to revise a weak explanation paragraph.

**I Do — The Explanation Edit Checklist**

Explanation writing has its own specific editing checklist. Watch me apply it to a weak paragraph.

> Example: The water cycle happens when water goes up into the sky. It then came back down as rain. The clouds made from water make rain fall. This is called the water cycle and it happened all the time.

Checklist: Present tense? — 'came' = past tense error. Technical vocabulary? — 'goes up' should be 'evaporates'. Cause-and-effect connectives? — none used. Topic sentence? — present but vague. Concluding link? — 'happened' = tense error; 'all the time' = informal. Fix each in order.

*Tip: Accuracy matters in explanation writing — if you're not sure a fact or term is correct, flag it for checking rather than leaving an error in.*

**We Do — Apply the Checklist Together**

Apply the explanation editing checklist to this paragraph together — check each item in order.
- Photosynthesis is when plants made food. They use the sunlight which hits the leaves. The green stuff in leaves (chlorophyll) catches the sun. Carbon dioxide went in and oxygen comes out. Plants need this for living.

*Discussion prompt: Which checklist item has the most errors? What is the priority order for fixing this paragraph?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Edit this explanation paragraph using the full checklist — tense, technical vocabulary, connectives, structure, and accuracy.
- Erosion happens when rocks and soil got moved by wind and water. Rivers carried rocks downstream and they break into smaller pieces over time. This is why valleys formed and the coastline changes. Erosion happened over millions of years.
- ✦ Bonus: Apply the explanation editing checklist to the paragraph you wrote on Wednesday. What did you find?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Explanation-specific editing checklist: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to reread and revise their writing, making changes to vocabulary, punctuation, and structure to improve clarity and impact, specifically applying text-type criteria to explanation writing. This lesson gives students a five-point explanation checklist as a transferable cross-curricular editing tool.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Revising explanation structure: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to organise explanation writing with clear cause-and-effect structure and appropriate language features. The editing checklist consolidates all four days of this week's explanation writing work into one applied revision skill.


### Week 5

#### Monday: Synonyms for said — precise dialogue tags

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): 'said' is a neutral dialogue tag; replacing it with a precise verb (muttered, snapped, insisted, whispered) tells the reader HOW something was said and adds characterisation without additional description.

**I Do — Said Tells Us Nothing**

'Said' is invisible — readers skip over it. A precise dialogue tag tells the reader the manner, tone, and emotion of speech in one word. Watch me upgrade dialogue by choosing precise tags.

> Example: 'I don't know where it is,' she said. → 'I don't know where it is,' she muttered, not meeting his eyes.

'Muttered' immediately tells us she's evasive, uncertain, perhaps embarrassed. I also added a small beat ('not meeting his eyes') to show rather than tell the emotion further. The combination of precise tag + action beat is the most powerful dialogue technique at this level.

*Tip: Match the tag to the emotion, not just the volume: 'shouted' only tells us it was loud. 'Snarled', 'barked', 'demanded' tell us it was loud AND angry/aggressive.*

**We Do — Choose the Precise Tag**

Replace 'said' with the most precise dialogue tag for the context described.
- 'Leave me alone,' she said. (emotion: angry, defensive)
- 'I think... maybe... we could try it,' he said. (emotion: uncertain, nervous)
- 'I know exactly where it is,' she said. (emotion: confident, perhaps smug)

*Discussion prompt: What emotion AND manner is the character expressing? Which verb captures both?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Replace 'said' with a precise dialogue tag and add a short action beat to show the emotion further.
- 'That's not fair,' she said.
- 'Come on, we'll be fine,' he said.
- 'I didn't do it,' the boy said.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 4-line dialogue between two characters in conflict, using a different precise dialogue tag for each line — no 'said' allowed.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Precise dialogue tags: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to select and use words and phrases that are fit for purpose and audience, including precise dialogue tags that convey manner and emotion rather than simply signalling speech. This is a specific application of the term's precise-verb-choice work to dialogue writing.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Characterisation through dialogue: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including characterisation through dialogue. Precise dialogue tags and action beats together create character voice and emotional subtext without relying on explicit description.


#### Tuesday: Concessive clauses

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): a concessive clause (introduced by although, even though, despite, while, whereas) acknowledges one idea while asserting a contrasting or more important one, adding sophistication to both persuasive and analytical writing.

**I Do — Acknowledging Before Asserting**

A concessive clause admits something is true, then asserts a more important contrasting truth. It's the grammar of nuance. Watch me build sentences using it.

> Example: Plain: Some students find homework hard. Many still benefit from it. → Concessive: Although some students find homework challenging, the majority benefit significantly from the practice it provides.

'Although' introduces the concession (the admitted truth), then the main clause asserts the stronger claim. Other concessive words: 'even though', 'despite', 'while', 'whereas'. Notice that 'despite' takes a noun phrase, not a clause: 'Despite its challenges, homework...' — not 'Despite it is challenging'.

*Tip: 'Despite' is followed by a noun or -ing form, not a full clause. 'Despite its being difficult' is correct. 'Despite it is difficult' is not.*

**We Do — Build the Concessive**

Combine each pair using a concessive clause opener.
- It was raining heavily. The match continued. (use: despite)
- The new policy has its critics. It has already reduced pollution by 20%. (use: although)
- The climb was gruelling. The view from the summit made it worthwhile. (use: even though)

*Discussion prompt: Which part is the concession and which is the main assertion? Does 'despite' need a noun or -ing form?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a concessive sentence for each pair using the conjunction given.
- Use 'although': Electric cars are currently expensive / they produce far lower emissions over their lifetime.
- Use 'despite': The project took three years / the results exceeded all expectations.
- Use 'whereas': Some students prefer group work / others find individual tasks more productive.
- ✦ Bonus: Write three sentences about the same controversial topic, each using a different concessive conjunction. Do they create subtly different effects?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Concessive clauses: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): A concessive clause, introduced by although, even though, despite, while, or whereas, acknowledges one idea while asserting a contrasting or more significant one. 'Despite' takes a noun phrase or gerund, not a full clause. Concessive clauses are a hallmark of sophisticated Phase 2 persuasive and analytical writing.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Nuanced sentence construction: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity, including concessive constructions that create nuance by balancing two contrasting ideas within a single sentence. This is closely related to the counter-argument work of Week 3.


#### Wednesday: Building tension with sentence length variation

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice) & Composing (Practice): writers deliberately manipulate sentence length to control pace and tension — short sentences slow the reader down and create impact, long sentences sustain flow or build complexity.

**I Do — Controlling the Reader's Heartbeat**

Short sentences make the reader stop, breathe, and feel the weight of a moment. Long sentences carry them forward in a rush of action or detail. The writer's job is to control this deliberately. Watch me use both.

> Example: Long then short: The crowd surged forward, voices rising to a single deafening roar as the scoreboard flickered, counted down, and finally froze on zero. Then: silence.

The long sentence mimics the building rush of noise and action. 'Then: silence.' — two words, the colon, the full stop — brings everything to a dead halt. The contrast makes both sentences more powerful than if the whole passage were the same length.

*Tip: For a sudden shock or revelation, make it the shortest sentence in the paragraph. The contrast with the sentences around it does the work.*

**We Do — Design the Tension**

Rewrite this passage by varying the sentence lengths to build tension toward the last sentence.
- She walked to the door. She opened it. There was a figure standing outside. She screamed.

*Discussion prompt: Which sentences should be long (building dread)? Which single sentence should be the shortest (impact point)?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a tense paragraph (5–7 sentences) for each scenario, deliberately varying sentence length to build toward a single short impact sentence at the end.
- Scenario: a player takes the last penalty in a shootout.
- Scenario: a character reaches the top of a mountain and sees what's on the other side.
- ✦ Bonus: Find a tense moment in a book you're reading. Count the sentence lengths. Does the author use this technique? Copy the pattern into your own writing about a different scenario.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Sentence length variation for tension: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity, specifically using deliberate sentence-length contrast to control pace and tension in narrative writing. This formalises the intuitive understanding of rhythm developed in Term 3 into a conscious narrative tool.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Controlling narrative pace and tension: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including deliberate sentence-length manipulation for pacing and tension. This is one of the highest-level narrative craft skills at Phase 2.


#### Thursday: Editing narrative pacing and tension

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply a pacing-and-tension editing lens to a narrative passage — identifying where pace is too flat, adding sentence-length variation, short impact sentences, and precise verbs to heighten tension.

**I Do — Reading for Pace**

When editing for pacing, I read the passage aloud at its current rhythm and ask: where does it drag? Where does it rush past something important? Watch me diagnose and fix a flat passage.

> Example: He walked to the edge. He looked down. It was very high. He felt scared. He stepped back.

Every sentence is the same length — the rhythm is completely flat. Fix: He crept to the edge, his breath shallow and quick. He looked down. Far below, the river glittered, cold and indifferent. He was so high. His foot found the edge — and he stepped back. Now there's a build, a reveal, a moment of dread, an impact, and a release.

*Tip: Read the passage aloud. If every sentence takes the same breath to say — vary at least one.*

**We Do — Diagnose and Fix**

Read this passage aloud. Identify where the pacing is flat, then rewrite it with deliberate sentence-length variation.
- The storm was getting worse. The boat rocked from side to side. A wave hit the deck. Everyone held on. Another wave came. The boat tilted dangerously.

*Discussion prompt: Where should pace slow down to build dread? Where should a single short sentence hit like a punch?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Edit this passage for pacing and tension using sentence-length variation, precise verbs, and one short impact sentence.
- The lion moved through the grass. It was looking at the zebra. The zebra didn't see it. The lion got closer. Then it ran at the zebra.
- ✦ Bonus: Take the most tense moment from a piece of your own narrative writing. Read it aloud, identify the flattest part, and revise specifically for sentence-length variation and precise verb choice.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Editing for pacing and tension: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to reread and revise their writing, making changes to vocabulary and structure to improve clarity and impact. This lesson teaches reading aloud as a pacing diagnostic tool and then applies sentence-length variation and precise verbs as the specific revision tools.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Pacing as a craft decision: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including managing narrative pacing as a deliberate craft choice. This editing lesson applies Wednesday's sentence-length technique to a real revision context.


### Week 6

#### Monday: Cohesive substitution — avoiding repetition

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): cohesive substitution replaces repeated nouns or noun phrases with synonyms, pronouns, or general-reference nouns to create flow without monotony, while maintaining clarity about who or what is being referred to.

**I Do — Saying the Same Thing a Different Way**

Repeating the same noun in every sentence is monotonous. But replacing it carelessly can confuse the reader. Watch me substitute carefully.

> Example: Repetitive: The kiwi is a flightless bird. The kiwi is nocturnal. The kiwi is endangered. The kiwi can only be found in New Zealand. → Substituted: The kiwi is a flightless, nocturnal bird. This remarkable creature is endangered and can only be found in New Zealand — our unique national symbol.

'The kiwi' becomes 'this remarkable creature' (adds description) and then 'our unique national symbol' (adds perspective). Both substitutions keep it clear we're still talking about the kiwi, while adding new information.

*Tip: Never substitute in a way that could confuse the reader about who or what you mean. If the substitution could refer to something else, keep the noun.*

**We Do — Substitute Without Confusing**

Rewrite this passage by substituting the repeated noun at least twice — without confusing the reader.
- The kauri tree is one of New Zealand's most ancient trees. The kauri tree can live for thousands of years. The kauri tree was heavily logged in the 19th century. The kauri tree is now a protected species.

*Discussion prompt: What synonyms or pronoun references could replace 'the kauri tree'? Does each substitution still clearly refer to the kauri?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each passage using cohesive substitution to reduce repetition without losing clarity.
- Elephants are the largest land animals on Earth. Elephants live in family groups. Elephants have excellent memories. Elephants are threatened by habitat loss and poaching.
- ✦ Bonus: Take a paragraph of your own writing and highlight every repeated noun. Try at least two substitutions — then check that a reader could still follow clearly who or what you're talking about.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Cohesive substitution: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to select and use words and phrases that are fit for purpose and audience, including using synonyms, pronouns, and reference nouns as cohesive substitution devices to reduce repetition while maintaining textual clarity. This is an advanced vocabulary and cohesion skill at Phase 2.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Cohesion through substitution: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to use cohesive devices — including pronouns and reference words — to link ideas within and across sentences. This lesson extends Term 3's cohesion work beyond connectives into the more subtle substitution devices that distinguish mature Phase 2 writing.


#### Tuesday: Relative clauses for embedded definitions

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): a relative clause (introduced by which, who, where, whose) can embed a definition or explanation directly inside a sentence, avoiding a clunky separate definition sentence — a key technique in explanation and formal writing.

**I Do — Defining Without Interrupting**

Instead of writing a separate definition sentence, I can embed the definition inside the main sentence using a relative clause. Watch me do it.

> Example: Separate: Photosynthesis is a process. It converts sunlight into energy. Plants use it. → Embedded: Photosynthesis, which converts sunlight into chemical energy, is the process that sustains almost all plant life on Earth.

The relative clause 'which converts sunlight into chemical energy' is embedded between commas, defining the term right where it appears. The reader gets the definition without the flow being interrupted by a separate sentence.

*Tip: If the relative clause is essential to identifying WHICH thing you mean, don't use commas. If it's adding extra information about something already identified, use commas around it.*

**We Do — Embed the Definition**

Combine each pair into one sentence using a relative clause to embed the definition.
- Erosion is a process. It gradually wears away rock and soil.
- Te Tiriti o Waitangi is a document. It was signed in 1840 between Maori chiefs and the Crown.
- The ozone layer is a region of Earth's atmosphere. It absorbs most of the sun's ultraviolet radiation.

*Discussion prompt: Does the relative clause add extra information (commas) or identify which one (no commas)?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Rewrite each pair as a single sentence, embedding the definition with a relative clause.
- Metamorphosis is a process. It transforms a caterpillar into a butterfly.
- New Zealand is a country. It was the first in the world to grant women the right to vote.
- A neuron is a cell. It transmits electrical signals through the nervous system.
- ✦ Bonus: Find a technical term in your reading or class notes. Write a sentence that introduces and defines it using an embedded relative clause.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Relative clauses for embedded definitions: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity, including using relative clauses to embed definitions or elaborations directly within a sentence — a key tool for explanation and formal writing. This consolidates relative clause knowledge from earlier terms into a specific, functional application.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Technical vocabulary integration: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to select and use words and phrases that are fit for purpose and audience, including integrating technical terms with their definitions smoothly and economically within explanation sentences. The embedded relative clause definition is the most sophisticated tool for this at Phase 2.


#### Wednesday: Topic sentences and paragraph-closing sentences

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Text Structure (Practice): a well-constructed paragraph opens with a topic sentence that states the main idea and closes with a sentence that clinches or links that idea; students distinguish strong from weak topic sentences and write effective closers.

**I Do — Opening and Closing a Paragraph Cleanly**

The topic sentence is a promise to the reader about what this paragraph will deliver. The closing sentence clinches that promise or links to the next paragraph. Watch me write both.

> Example: Weak topic sentence: 'This paragraph is about recycling.' → Strong: 'Recycling reduces the pressure on landfill sites while conserving the natural resources needed to produce new materials.' Weak closer: 'So that is why recycling is good.' → Strong: 'For these reasons, recycling is not merely a personal choice — it is a shared environmental responsibility.'

The strong topic sentence makes a specific claim that the paragraph can prove. The strong closer doesn't just repeat the topic — it elevates it, leaving the reader with a stronger impression.

*Tip: Test your topic sentence: could the reader predict what your paragraph will contain? Test your closer: does it add something, or just repeat the topic sentence?*

**We Do — Improve the Topic and Closer**

Improve the weak topic sentence and closer for this paragraph.
- Weak topic: 'New Zealand has a lot of native birds.' [body: kiwi, kakapo, takahe details] Weak closer: 'So New Zealand native birds are quite unique.'

*Discussion prompt: What specific claim could the topic sentence make? What does the closer need to add — elevation, significance, or a link?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a strong topic sentence and closing sentence for each paragraph body given.
- Body: sentences about how exercise releases endorphins, reduces stress hormones, and improves sleep quality.
- Body: sentences about how deforestation reduces biodiversity, increases carbon emissions, and leads to soil erosion.
- ✦ Bonus: Take any paragraph from a piece of your own writing. Rewrite its topic sentence to make a more specific claim, and rewrite its closing sentence to elevate or link rather than just repeat.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Topic and closing sentences: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to organise ideas into paragraphs with a clear main idea established by a strong topic sentence, and to close paragraphs with a sentence that clinches or links — not merely repeats — the main idea. Strong topic and closing sentences are hallmarks of well-controlled Phase 2 writing.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Paragraph-level composition: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including at the paragraph level — opening with a specific, testable claim and closing with an elevated or linking sentence.


#### Thursday: Adapting a piece for a different audience

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Composing (Practice) & Vocabulary (Practice): students revise a piece of writing for a different audience, changing vocabulary, register, examples, and level of detail to suit the new reader without changing the core information or argument.

**I Do — Same Message, Different Reader**

The same information can be written very differently depending on the audience. Watch me adapt a paragraph about recycling from a formal audience to a young audience.

> Example: Formal: Recycling reduces landfill pressure and conserves finite natural resources, contributing to a circular economy. | Young audience: When we recycle, we help keep rubbish out of enormous rubbish dumps called landfills, and we save natural materials so they can be used again.

Same facts, completely different vocabulary, sentence structure, and examples. Neither version is wrong — they're both right for their audience.

*Tip: Ask three questions before adapting: (1) How much does this audience already know? (2) What vocabulary will they understand? (3) What examples will feel relevant to their lives?*

**We Do — Adapt Together**

Adapt this paragraph — written for adults — for an audience of Year 4 students.
- New Zealand's native biodiversity is under significant threat from introduced predators, particularly rats, stoats, and possums, which compete with or prey directly upon endemic species.

*Discussion prompt: What vocabulary needs changing? What background knowledge can we assume? What everyday examples could replace the technical ones?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Adapt each passage for the new audience given.
- Original (for Year 6): 'Photosynthesis is the process by which plants convert solar energy into glucose using chlorophyll.' → Adapt for Year 2.
- Original (for Year 2): 'Leaves are green because of a special ingredient called chlorophyll.' → Adapt for an adult science magazine.
- ✦ Bonus: Take a paragraph from your own persuasive or explanation writing and adapt it for two different audiences — your school principal, and a Year 3 student. What changes between the two versions?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Audience adaptation: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies appropriate to audience and purpose, including adapting existing writing for a new reader by changing vocabulary, register, sentence structure, and examples while preserving the core content. Audience adaptation is a key Phase 2 composing skill with direct cross-curricular application.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Register and vocabulary for audience: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to select and use words and phrases that are fit for purpose and audience.
