This lesson applies the full year's vocabulary and register work to a genuine revision task — rewriting for a different audience — making register not just a label but an active writing decision.


### Week 7

#### Monday: Hyperbole for effect

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): hyperbole is deliberate and obvious exaggeration used for humorous, dramatic, or emphatic effect — not intended to be taken literally.

**I Do — The Art of Enormous Exaggeration**

Hyperbole is deliberate over-the-top exaggeration for effect. Unlike lying, the reader KNOWS it's exaggerated — that's the point. Watch me use it for comic and dramatic effect.

> Example: Comic: I've told you a million times to clean your room. | Dramatic: The silence in the hall was so complete you could have heard a heartbeat three streets away.

'A million times' is obviously untrue — its comic effect comes from the scale of the exaggeration. 'Heard a heartbeat three streets away' is physically impossible — its dramatic effect comes from the image of extreme, unnatural stillness.

*Tip: Hyperbole works best when it's CLEARLY impossible — the more obviously exaggerated, the better. Mild exaggeration just sounds like an inaccuracy.*

**We Do — Build the Hyperbole**

Write a hyperbolic sentence for each situation, making the exaggeration as vivid and obviously impossible as possible.
- The bag was very heavy.
- The test was very long.
- He was extremely hungry.

*Discussion prompt: Is the exaggeration obvious enough to be clearly hyperbole? Does it create a vivid image?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a hyperbolic sentence for each prompt.
- She ran very fast.
- The crowd was enormous.
- It was a very boring speech.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short comic paragraph (3–4 sentences) about a small everyday annoyance using at least two hyperboles. Aim to make the reader laugh.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Hyperbole: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): Hyperbole is deliberate and obvious exaggeration used for humorous, dramatic, or emphatic effect — not intended to be taken literally by the reader. Its effectiveness depends on the scale of the exaggeration being recognisably impossible. This completes the year's range of figurative language devices.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Comic and dramatic voice: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including humour as a deliberate compositional choice. Hyperbole is one of the primary tools for creating comic voice in writing.


#### Tuesday: Sentence fragments for deliberate effect

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): a sentence fragment lacks a subject, verb, or complete thought; while fragments are errors in formal writing, skilled authors use them deliberately in narrative writing for pace, emphasis, and dramatic effect.

**I Do — Breaking the Rules on Purpose**

A fragment is technically incomplete — but skilled writers use them deliberately in narrative for impact. The key is: it must be clearly intentional, not accidental. Watch me use a fragment deliberately.

> Example: Complete sentences: He reached the top of the hill and looked out at the view. He had never seen anything like it. → With deliberate fragment: He reached the top of the hill and looked out. Nothing could have prepared him. Endless. Wild. Breathtaking.

'Endless. Wild. Breathtaking.' — three fragments in a row. They create a staccato, breathless effect, mimicking the gasp of someone overwhelmed by a view. The incompleteness IS the effect.

*Tip: Only use deliberate fragments in creative/narrative writing — NEVER in formal, persuasive, or explanation writing. And always be able to explain why the fragment is there.*

**We Do — Add a Deliberate Fragment**

Add one or two deliberate fragments to heighten the impact of each passage.
- She opened the box. Inside was a letter she had been waiting for.
- The final whistle blew. The scoreboard showed the result.

*Discussion prompt: Where would a single-word or short fragment create the most impact? Is the fragment placement clearly intentional?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Add deliberate fragments to each narrative moment to heighten the effect. Mark each fragment with [F] so it's clear it's intentional.
- He stepped off the plane into the cold air of a new country.
- She looked at the test results for a long time.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph describing an overwhelming moment that uses at least three deliberate fragments for effect.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Deliberate sentence fragments: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): A sentence fragment is a grammatically incomplete sentence lacking a subject, verb, or complete thought. While fragments are errors in formal writing, skilled authors use them deliberately in narrative writing to create pace, emphasis, or a staccato dramatic effect. Deliberate fragments must be clearly intentional and contextually justified.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Style and craft in narrative voice: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader. Understanding when and why to break a grammar rule — and being able to justify the choice — is a hallmark of the most sophisticated writing at Phase 2 and into Year 7.


#### Wednesday: Opening hooks for different text types

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Composing (Practice): an effective opening hook immediately engages the reader; different text types use different hook strategies — narrative (in-scene, sensory), persuasive (bold claim, statistic, rhetorical question), explanation (surprising fact, question).

**I Do — The First Sentence Does the Heavy Lifting**

The opening sentence determines whether the reader continues. Watch me write strong hooks for three different text types.

> Example: Narrative hook (in-scene): The smell of smoke reached her before the alarm did. | Persuasive hook (bold claim): Every day, the equivalent of 2,000 trees' worth of paper is wasted in New Zealand's schools. | Explanation hook (surprising fact): Despite its name, a starfish is not actually a fish — and cannot survive outside saltwater for more than a few minutes.

Each hook uses a different strategy: narrative puts the reader immediately into a sensory moment. Persuasive opens with a specific, credible statistic. Explanation opens with a counterintuitive fact that creates curiosity.

*Tip: The hook's job is to make the reader ask a question they want answered. What question does each of your hooks make the reader want to answer?*

**We Do — Write the Hook**

Write a strong opening hook for each text type and topic.
- Narrative: a character who wakes up in an unfamiliar place.
- Persuasive: why zoos should be redesigned as conservation sanctuaries.
- Explanation: how the human eye works.

*Discussion prompt: Which strategy suits each text type? Does your hook make the reader want to read the next sentence?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write two different opening hooks for each topic — one using a narrative strategy, one using a persuasive or explanation strategy.
- Topic: the ocean.
- Topic: learning a new skill.
- ✦ Bonus: Go back to a piece of writing you completed earlier this term. Rewrite its opening sentence using a different hook strategy — does the new hook work better?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Opening hooks by text type: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including selecting an appropriate opening hook strategy matched to the text type and purpose. Narrative hooks use sensory/in-scene openings; persuasive hooks use bold claims, statistics, or rhetorical questions; explanation hooks use surprising or counterintuitive facts.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Text-type appropriate openings: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to organise ideas with openings appropriate to the text type, including hooks that immediately signal purpose and engage the reader.


#### Thursday: Structural edit — whole-text level

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): a structural edit evaluates the organisation of a complete piece of writing — checking that the opening hooks, paragraphs are in the right order, transitions are clear, and the ending resolves what the beginning promised.

**I Do — Editing the Shape, Not the Sentences**

A structural edit ignores spelling and grammar — it only asks: does the whole piece have the right shape? Watch me apply a structural checklist to a piece plan.

> Example: Structural checklist: Does the opening hook immediately engage? Are paragraphs in the most logical/effective order? Is there a clear climax or argument progression? Do transitions between paragraphs feel inevitable? Does the ending resolve or reflect back on what the opening promised?

I apply this top-down, not bottom-up. A structural problem (paragraph 3 should be paragraph 1) is more important than a word choice issue — fix structure first, always.

*Tip: Print or copy your writing and cut it into paragraphs. Can you rearrange them into a better order? If one paragraph could be removed without the reader noticing — it probably should be.*

**We Do — Structural Diagnosis**

Apply the structural checklist to this piece outline together. What needs moving or improving?
- 1. Conclusion: recycling is important. 2. Evidence: statistics about landfill. 3. Counter-argument. 4. Hook: what if every piece of rubbish you ever threw away was still here? 5. Main argument. 6. Rebuttal.

*Discussion prompt: Is this in the right order? Where does the hook belong? What should the conclusion say rather than just 'recycling is important'?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Apply the structural checklist to a full piece of your own writing. List at least two structural improvements, then make the most important one.
- Does the opening hook immediately engage?
- Are paragraphs in the most effective order?
- Is there a clear progression/climax?
- Do transitions feel inevitable?
- Does the ending deliver on the opening's promise?
- ✦ Bonus: Swap with a partner and do a structural edit of their piece. Tell them: what the shape is, what is working, one specific structural change you'd recommend.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Structural editing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to reread and revise their writing, making changes to structure to improve clarity and impact. This lesson distinguishes structural editing (whole-text organisation) from sentence-level editing — establishing that structure is always revised first, before sentence-level polish.
- *Writing — Text Structure (Practice)* — Whole-text organisation: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Text Structure (Practice): Students are expected to organise ideas into paragraphs with clear whole-text shape — appropriate opening, logical progression, and satisfying ending. The structural edit is the culminating text-structure skill of the year, applied independently to a complete piece.


### Week 8

#### Monday: Vocabulary of concession

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge & Practice): concessive adverbs and adverbial phrases (admittedly, granted, nevertheless, even so, that said) allow writers to acknowledge a valid opposing point while maintaining their own position, adding nuance and credibility to argument.

**I Do — Conceding Without Surrendering**

Concessive vocabulary lets you admit something is true (concede a point) while keeping your main argument firmly in place. Watch me use several different concessive words to do this.

> Example: Admittedly, electric cars are currently more expensive to purchase. Nevertheless, their lower running costs and environmental benefits make them the more sensible long-term investment.

'Admittedly' signals: I acknowledge this objection. 'Nevertheless' signals: but here's why my argument still holds. Other pairs: 'Granted... even so'; 'It is true that... yet'; 'That said...'. The concession makes the writer seem balanced and credible.

*Tip: The concessive word and the rebuttal word are a pair — don't use one without the other, or your argument sounds unfinished.*

**We Do — Choose the Right Concessive**

Add an appropriate concessive phrase and a rebuttal to each situation.
- Some people argue that homework causes too much stress. [Concede + rebut]
- Visiting zoos may be educational for some children. [Concede + rebut]
- Online learning is undeniably more flexible than traditional schooling. [Rebut this concession]

*Discussion prompt: Which concessive word fits best — admittedly, granted, that said, even so? Does the rebuttal actually outweigh the concession?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a concede-and-rebut sentence pair for each topic, using a different concessive word each time.
- Topic: whether competitive sport is good for children.
- Topic: whether social media should be available in schools.
- Topic: whether animals should be kept as pets.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full paragraph on a topic of your choice that uses two different concessive phrases — one near the beginning and one near the end — to build a nuanced, credible argument.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Vocabulary of concession: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): Concessive adverbs and adverbial phrases (admittedly, granted, nevertheless, even so, that said) allow writers to acknowledge valid opposing points while maintaining their own argument. They are always paired with a rebuttal signal word that reasserts the writer's position. This vocabulary extends Term 3's connective work and Term 4 Week 3's counter-argument structure into a more sophisticated one-sentence technique.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Nuance in persuasive vocabulary: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to select and use words and phrases that are fit for purpose and audience, including the vocabulary of concession in persuasive writing — words that signal intellectual balance and credibility rather than one-sided assertion.


#### Tuesday: Review: subordination vs coordination across a paragraph

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): students review and apply the distinction between coordinating and subordinating conjunctions across a full paragraph, ensuring controlled variety of simple, compound, and complex sentences.

**I Do — The Full Mix in One Paragraph**

Today we review the year's conjunction work and check we can mix simple, compound, and complex sentences fluently in one paragraph. Watch me read a paragraph and label each sentence type.

> Example: The hawk circled silently. [simple] It rode the thermal higher and higher, and the valley fell away beneath it. [compound] Although the air was still warm, the bird sensed the approaching evening. [complex] It folded its wings and dropped. [compound-simple]

Reading through: every sentence is grammatically correct. No two consecutive sentences are the same type. Short simple sentences contrast with longer compound and complex ones. The variety creates rhythm and sustains the reader's interest.

*Tip: Write a paragraph, then label each sentence S / Co / Cx. If you have three in a row of the same type — vary the next one.*

**We Do — Label and Improve**

Label each sentence in this paragraph S / Co / Cx and identify where the variety breaks down.
- The boat left the harbour. The sea was calm and the wind was light. They sailed north. The islands came into view. They anchored and they went ashore. They found the beach deserted.

*Discussion prompt: Where is the variety lacking? Which sentences could be combined or restructured to create better rhythm?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write a paragraph of 5–7 sentences on the topic given, then label each sentence S / Co / Cx. Ensure no three consecutive sentences share the same type.
- Topic: arriving at a new place for the first time.
- ✦ Bonus: Take a paragraph from your own writing. Label every sentence S / Co / Cx. If any type is missing — add or convert a sentence to include it.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Sentence variety: S/Co/Cx review: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): Consolidates the year's sentence-structure strand — simple, compound, and complex sentences created through coordinating and subordinating conjunctions — requiring students to apply all three types within a single paragraph with controlled variety. This is a comprehensive review of the year's grammar in context.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Controlled sentence variety in a paragraph: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to write a variety of sentence structures for effect and clarity. This review lesson requires students to demonstrate that sentence variety is not accidental but planned — labelling sentence types and deliberately ensuring variety across a paragraph, as they will be expected to do independently in Year 7.


#### Wednesday: Combining techniques — planning before drafting

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Composing (Practice) & Writing Process (Knowledge): students plan a short piece of writing by deliberately selecting which techniques to deploy (vocabulary devices, sentence structures, punctuation) BEFORE drafting, then write to their plan.

**I Do — The Technique Plan**

The most controlled, effective writing comes from planning which techniques to use BEFORE you start, not hoping they appear. Watch me make a technique plan for a short piece.

> Example: Topic: the moment before a performance. Plan: Hook — in-scene sensory opening. Vocabulary — precise verbs, pathetic fallacy. Sentences — -ing opener, long-then-short contrast for tension. Punctuation — colon for revelation, deliberate fragment for effect. Register — narrative, first person.

Having this plan means every choice I make while drafting is purposeful. I'm not searching for a technique — I'm executing a plan. This is how published writers work.

*Tip: Aim for 4–5 techniques in your plan. More than that and the piece feels forced — every sentence feels like it's showing off rather than telling a story.*

**We Do — Plan Together**

Together, build a technique plan for this topic before writing a single sentence.
- Topic: the last five minutes of a championship match.

*Discussion prompt: What hook strategy? Which vocabulary techniques? Sentence structures for tension? Punctuation for effect? What register and person?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Build a technique plan, then write a paragraph (5–7 sentences) to your plan. Label each technique you used.
- Topic: discovering something unexpected in an old building.
- ✦ Bonus: Write the same topic twice — once with a plan, once without. Which version is more controlled and effective? What does the planning add?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Planning technique use before drafting: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including planning specific techniques before drafting — selecting vocabulary devices, sentence structures, and punctuation choices in advance rather than improvising. Planning-before-drafting is a key indicator of writing maturity at Phase 2.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge)* — Pre-drafting planning strategies: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge): Effective writers plan their use of techniques — vocabulary devices, sentence structures, punctuation choices, and register — before beginning to draft, rather than hoping these features will appear incidentally. This lesson formalises the planning stage of the writing process as an explicit, teachable skill.


#### Thursday: Peer editing for voice and style

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply a voice-and-style checklist when peer editing, evaluating whether a partner's writing has a consistent, distinctive voice and whether stylistic choices are deliberate and effective.

**I Do — Reading for Voice**

Voice is the sense that a real person is behind the words — consistent in register, distinctive in word choice, deliberate in style. Watch me read two versions of the same passage and identify which has voice and why.

> Example: Version A: The old man sat in the chair by the window. He looked at the street. It was busy. Version B: Every morning, he claimed his chair by the window like a king ascending a throne — watching the street below with the patient authority of someone who had earned the right to simply observe.

Version A is accurate but voiceless. Version B has consistent register (slightly elevated, respectful), metaphor (king/throne), precise verb ('claimed'), and rhythm. The style feels like a choice, not a default.

*Tip: Ask: if I removed the name from this piece, would I know who wrote it? If yes, there's voice. If it could have been written by anyone — it probably needs more.*

**We Do — Voice Checklist**

Apply the voice checklist to this passage together.
- The forest was dark and quiet. We walked through it. There were lots of trees and bushes. We heard some birds. It was a nice experience.

*Discussion prompt: What voice checklist items are missing? Register? Distinctive word choice? Figurative language? Sentence variety?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Swap a piece of writing with a partner. Apply the voice-and-style checklist and write specific feedback — one strength and one specific suggestion.
- Is register consistent throughout?
- Are word choices distinctive — or could anyone have written this?
- Is figurative language present and purposeful — or absent/cliched?
- Is sentence variety clearly deliberate — or flat?
- Does the piece sound like a real, specific writer?
- ✦ Bonus: Using your partner's feedback, revise the weakest section of your own piece for voice — aim to make it clearly, identifiably yours.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Peer editing for voice and style: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to share writing with others and respond to feedback to improve their work, including receiving and giving feedback specifically on voice and style — the most subjective but also most important dimension of writing quality at Phase 2.
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Developing a distinctive writing voice: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, including developing a consistent, distinctive writing voice through deliberate choices of register, vocabulary, figurative language, and sentence structure. This is the culminating compositional skill of the year.


### Week 9

#### Monday: Year vocabulary review — choosing your best tool

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge & Practice): students review the full year's vocabulary toolkit and practise selecting the best tool for a given writing purpose rather than applying devices mechanically.

**I Do — The Full Toolkit**

This year we built a vocabulary toolkit. Today, the challenge is not 'can you use this device' but 'which device should you use here, and why?' Watch me make that choice explicitly.

> Example: Prompt: describe a wildfire approaching a town. Options: simile? metaphor? personification? precise verbs? pathetic fallacy?

Personification — the fire devoured the hillside — works because fire as a predator creates dread. Pathetic fallacy — if the sky turns blood-red — sets mood before the fire arrives. Precise verbs — the fire crept, then surged, then consumed — gives pace and progression. Best combination: start with pathetic fallacy for mood, then precise verbs for pace, then one strong personification at the peak.

*Tip: The question is never 'which device do I know?' It's always 'which device does this moment need?'*

**We Do — Toolkit Decision**

For each writing prompt, decide which vocabulary technique(s) would be most effective and explain why — before writing any sentences.
- Persuasive: argue for protecting New Zealand's native forests.
- Explanation: describe how a glacier forms.
- Narrative: the moment a character realises they are lost.

*Discussion prompt: Which vocabulary device(s) suit each text type and purpose? Why are some devices better fits than others here?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

For each prompt, choose your two best vocabulary tools, explain your choice, and write two sentences using them.
- A character watches their home disappear as a ship leaves port.
- An explanation of why coral reefs are important ecosystems.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph for one of the prompts above, using at least four different vocabulary techniques deliberately. Label each one in the margin.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Year vocabulary toolkit review: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): Consolidates the full year's vocabulary strand — precise verbs, figurative language (simile, metaphor, personification, pathetic fallacy, hyperbole, onomatopoeia, idiom), connotation, emotive language, technical vocabulary, and register — into a meta-level skill: choosing the best vocabulary tool for a specific writing purpose rather than applying devices mechanically.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Strategic vocabulary selection: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students are expected to select and use words and phrases, including figurative language, that are fit for purpose and audience. This review lesson assesses whether students have moved beyond technique knowledge to genuine technique selection — the highest-level vocabulary skill at Phase 2.


#### Tuesday: Year grammar review — mixed features from all four terms

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): students review the full year's grammar strand — sentence types, conjunctions, tenses, voice, modal verbs, reported speech, conditional sentences, and clauses — applying them in a mixed, integrated context.

**I Do — One Sentence, Many Grammar Features**

By now, a sophisticated sentence can contain multiple grammar features at once. Watch me build one deliberately, naming each feature as I add it.

> Example: Build-up: Base: The scientists made a discovery. Add passive: A discovery was made. Add modal: A significant discovery may have been made. Add relative clause: ... by the scientists, who had been working on the project for over a decade. Add concessive: Although the research had taken over a decade, a significant discovery may finally have been made — one that could change everything.

Each step adds a grammatical feature: passive voice, modal verb, relative clause, concessive conjunction. The sentence remains grammatically correct throughout. This is the level of control expected heading into Year 7.

*Tip: If you're unsure whether a multi-feature sentence is correct, simplify it. A clear, correct simpler sentence is better than a tangled complex one.*

**We Do — Identify All Features**

Identify every grammar feature from the year's learning in this sentence.
- Despite the fact that the match had been postponed three times, the players, who had trained for months, insisted that they would be ready — if the ground was deemed safe.

*Discussion prompt: Concessive clause? Passive voice? Past perfect? Relative clause? Conditional? Modal verb? How many features can we find?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Identify the grammar features in each sentence, then write one original sentence that combines at least three features from the year.
- 'I should have been told earlier,' she muttered, even though she suspected she wouldn't have listened.
- The ancient bridge, which had stood for 400 years, was finally demolished — a decision that the council had debated for over a decade.
- ✦ Bonus: Write three sentences — one from each main text type (narrative, persuasive, explanation) — each containing at least three grammar features from the year. Label each feature.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Year grammar toolkit review: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): Consolidates the full year's grammar strand — sentence types (S/Co/Cx), conjunction types, tense system (simple past, present, past perfect), active/passive voice, modal verbs, direct and reported speech, conditional sentences, and clause types (relative, subordinate, concessive, participial) — in an integrated, multi-feature context.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Integrated grammar application: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students are expected to write grammatically correct sentences using a range of tenses and structures. This review lesson assesses the highest-level Phase 2 grammatical skill: combining multiple grammatical features within a single, still-correct sentence — the standard expected at Year 7.


#### Wednesday: Planning and drafting a complete chosen-text-type piece

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Composing (Practice) & Writing Process (Knowledge): students independently plan and draft a complete piece in their chosen text type, applying the year's full toolkit — deliberately planned before drafting.

**I Do — The Full Pre-Draft Plan**

Today we write a complete piece. But first — the full plan. Watch me build one for a persuasive piece in 5 minutes, covering all the dimensions we've learned.

> Example: Topic: why every school should have a dedicated nature area. Text type: persuasive. Hook: rhetorical question and statistic. Structure: intro, 3 PEEL paragraphs, counter and rebuttal, conclusion. Vocabulary: emotive language, concessive phrases, precise verbs. Grammar: conditional sentences, relative clauses for definitions. Sentence variety: -ing opener, colon for revelation, deliberate fragment for closing. Register: formal, third person.

Everything is decided before I write word one. The drafting is now execution, not exploration. This means the draft will be more controlled and better structured from the start.

*Tip: Your plan should be specific enough that someone else could write the piece from it. If it's vague, it won't help you when you're actually drafting.*

**We Do — Plan Together**

As a class, build the full plan for a narrative piece about an unexpected discovery.
- Topic: a character finds something buried in their garden that changes everything.

*Discussion prompt: What goes in the plan? Is it specific enough that the draft will write itself from it?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Choose your text type. Build your full plan. Then draft your complete piece (minimum 3–4 paragraphs). Label at least 5 techniques in the draft.
- Choose one: Narrative (a moment of unexpected change) / Persuasive (a cause you believe in) / Explanation (a natural process you understand well).
- ✦ Bonus: After drafting, circle every deliberate technique you used. Were there any in your draft that weren't in your plan? Were there any in your plan that you forgot to include?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Independent complete-piece composition: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students are expected to use composing strategies to create texts that engage the reader, demonstrating the full integration of the year's vocabulary, grammar, sentence structure, and text organisation strategies in an independently composed, complete piece of writing.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge)* — Planning as part of the writing process: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge): Effective writers plan systematically before drafting, making deliberate decisions about text type, structure, vocabulary, grammar, and sentence variety that guide and control the drafting process. This lesson demonstrates that planning-before-drafting is the foundation of quality independent writing at Phase 2 and beyond.


#### Thursday: Full publication-ready edit of own writing

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students apply a comprehensive, sequential editing process (structural, paragraph-level, sentence-level, word-level, final proofread) to the piece they drafted on Wednesday, producing a publication-ready version.

**I Do — The Five-Pass Edit**

Professional writers edit in passes — top down, not randomly. Watch me apply all five passes to a paragraph.

> Example: Five passes: 1. STRUCTURAL — is the whole piece in the right shape? 2. PARAGRAPH — do topic sentences and closers work? 3. SENTENCE — variety, sentence types, clause work. 4. WORD — precise verbs, figurative language, connotation, register. 5. PROOFREAD — punctuation, spelling, grammar errors.

Pass 1: structure check — nothing to fix. Pass 2: first topic sentence is weak — fix it. Pass 3: two consecutive simple sentences — combine one. Pass 4: 'went' appears twice — replace both. Pass 5: missing comma after fronted adverbial. Five focused passes catch what one frantic reading misses.

*Tip: Do NOT proofread first. Structural problems waste your proofreading effort on sentences you might delete.*

**We Do — Pass by Pass**

Together, take a sample paragraph through all five passes in order, making one fix per pass.
- The forest was big and old. Many animals lived there. People were cutting it down and this was bad for the animals. Something should be done about this. It needs to stop.

*Discussion prompt: Pass 1: Does this have the right structure for its type? Pass 2: Topic sentence? Pass 3: Sentence variety? Pass 4: Precise verbs and word choice? Pass 5: Punctuation errors?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Apply all five passes — in order — to the piece you drafted on Wednesday. Mark every change and note which pass caught it.
- Pass 1: Structure check
- Pass 2: Paragraph-level check
- Pass 3: Sentence-level check
- Pass 4: Word-level check
- Pass 5: Final proofread
- ✦ Bonus: Count how many changes each pass found. Which pass was most productive for your piece? What does that tell you about where you most need to improve as a writer?

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Five-pass sequential editing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students are expected to reread and revise their writing to check that it makes sense and is accurate, including checking vocabulary, punctuation, and structure. The five-pass sequential edit — structural, paragraph, sentence, word, proofread — is the publication-standard editing process that consolidates every editing skill developed across the year.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge)* — Sequential editing as professional practice: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge): Effective editing is sequential — structural problems are addressed before paragraph-level issues, which are addressed before sentence-level issues, which are addressed before word choice, before proofreading. This is the capstone editing lesson of the year.


### Week 10

#### Monday: Year celebration — vocabulary Hall of Fame

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge & Practice): students reflect on and celebrate the most powerful vocabulary tools they have learned across the year, selecting their personal Hall of Fame and articulating why each device is effective.

**I Do — What Made the Biggest Difference?**

We've built a huge vocabulary toolkit this year. Today, I'll share my Hall of Fame — the vocabulary techniques that make the biggest difference to writing quality — and explain exactly why.

> Example: My Hall of Fame nominations: 1. Precise verbs — because one strong verb does what ten weak words can't. 2. Pathetic fallacy — because it makes the whole world part of the story. 3. Connotation — because the difference between 'determined' and 'stubborn' is the difference between a hero and a villain.

For each, I give a reason that goes beyond 'it sounds better'. The reflection IS the learning.

*Tip: Your Hall of Fame should be personal — the techniques that changed YOUR writing, not just the ones that sound impressive to mention.*

**We Do — Class Hall of Fame**

As a class, nominate vocabulary techniques for the class Hall of Fame and make the case for each one.
- Nominations open: which vocabulary technique made the biggest difference to your writing this year? Make the case in one sentence.

*Discussion prompt: Why THIS technique specifically? What does it let a writer do that they couldn't do without it?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Write your personal vocabulary Hall of Fame — three techniques, with one paragraph of justification for each.
- Choice 1 and why it matters.
- Choice 2 and why it matters.
- Choice 3 and why it matters.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short paragraph that deliberately uses all three of your Hall of Fame techniques. Annotate it so a reader could see exactly where each one appears and what it does.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge)* — Metacognitive vocabulary reflection: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Knowledge): Synthesises the year's vocabulary strand through metacognitive reflection — students evaluate which vocabulary tools have been most effective for their own writing development and articulate the reason, demonstrating the highest-level understanding: not just knowing a technique, but understanding why it works.
- *Writing — Vocabulary (Practice)* — Personal vocabulary Hall of Fame: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Vocabulary (Practice): Students demonstrate ownership of vocabulary skills by selecting, justifying, and applying their most personally significant vocabulary techniques. This culminating activity confirms that vocabulary knowledge has become internalised and transferable — the standard expected at the transition to Year 7.


#### Tuesday: Year celebration — grammar mastery challenge

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge & Practice): students complete a mixed grammar mastery challenge drawing on the full year's grammar strand, demonstrating secure knowledge of all major Phase 2 sentence structure concepts.

**I Do — The Grammar Full Circuit**

Today we go back to the beginning and run the full grammar circuit — every major concept from all four terms. Watch me work through a mixed challenge, naming each feature as I go.

> Example: Challenge: rewrite this passage to include a complex sentence, past perfect flashback, reported speech, passive voice, modal verb, and concessive clause.

Original: The team won. The captain was happy. She said she was proud. → Rewrite: Although the season had been difficult — they had nearly given up after a losing streak early on — the team finally won the championship. The captain was reported to have said that she was overwhelmingly proud. It was a result that should have come sooner.

*Tip: Work feature by feature through the challenge list, not all at once. Tick each one off before moving to the next.*

**We Do — Mixed Challenge**

Complete this grammar challenge together — one feature at a time.
- Base sentence: The school held a special event. It was a surprise. Everyone enjoyed it. Challenge: add a relative clause, a past perfect, a modal verb, and a concessive clause.

*Discussion prompt: Which feature do we add first? Does the sentence remain grammatically correct after each addition?*

**You Do — Your Turn**

Complete the grammar mastery challenge. Rewrite the passage using ALL the grammar features listed.
- Base: Scientists found a new species. They had worked for years. The discovery was important. Required features: passive voice, past perfect, modal verb, relative clause, concessive clause, reported speech.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a fully original paragraph — topic of your choice — that contains every grammar feature from the list above, correctly used. Annotate each one.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge)* — Year grammar mastery challenge: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Knowledge): The year's grammar mastery challenge synthesises all major Phase 2 sentence structure concepts — sentence types, conjunctions, tense system, voice, modal verbs, direct and reported speech, conditional sentences, concessive clauses, and clause types — in a single integrated task that confirms secure grammatical understanding heading into Year 7.
- *Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice)* — Grammar integration at Year 7 readiness level: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Sentence Structure (Practice): Students demonstrate that they can write grammatically correct sentences using the full range of Phase 2 structures simultaneously — the standard expected for Year 7 transition.


#### Wednesday: Year celebration — flexible writing showcase

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Composing (Practice): students write a short, polished piece in their chosen text type on a topic of their choice, showcasing the full range of the year's techniques as a celebration of writing growth.

**I Do — Writing with Everything You Know**

Today is a free write — but 'free' doesn't mean unplanned. It means you choose the topic, the text type, and the techniques. You decide what this piece is and what it does. Watch me make those decisions confidently and fast.

> Example: My choice: narrative. Topic: the last day of summer. Techniques: pathetic fallacy for the opening, precise verbs throughout, one deliberate fragment, and a colon for the emotional revelation in the final paragraph. Register: reflective, first person. Length: 4 paragraphs.

I commit to a plan in 2 minutes, then draft with confidence. The decisions are fast because I trust my toolkit. That's what a year of writing practice is supposed to produce.

*Tip: Don't spend more than 3 minutes planning. Make confident choices and write. The editing pass comes afterwards.*

**We Do — Share Plans**

Each student shares their plan (text type, topic, 3 technique choices) with a partner before writing. Partners give a 30-second response: does the plan sound like it will produce a strong piece?
- Share: text type, topic, three technique choices, and your intended opening hook.

*Discussion prompt: Does the plan hang together? Does the hook suit the text type and topic?*

**You Do — Write Your Showcase Piece**

Plan (3 minutes), draft (15 minutes), quick self-edit (5 minutes). Aim for 3–5 paragraphs.
- Choose your text type and topic freely — any text type, any subject that genuinely interests you.
- Aim to use at least 5 techniques from across the year's learning.
- After drafting, do a quick 5-minute self-edit — one pass for the most important fix.
- ✦ Bonus: Annotate your showcase piece — circle and label every technique you used deliberately. This is your record of the year's learning in action.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Composing (Practice)* — Independent flexible writing showcase: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Composing (Practice): Students independently compose a complete, polished piece in their chosen text type, demonstrating the full range of Phase 2 composing strategies — including planning, technique selection, deliberate vocabulary and grammar choices, and self-editing — without teacher scaffolding. This is the summative composing task for the year.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Planning to publication independently: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students independently manage the full writing process — planning, drafting, and self-editing — with confident, fast decision-making that reflects internalised Phase 2 writing knowledge. This independent control of the writing process is the central goal of the year's programme.


#### Thursday: Portfolio piece — the whole-year best edit

**NZ Curriculum Link:** Writing — Writing Process (Practice): students select their best piece of writing from across the year, apply the five-pass edit to bring it to portfolio quality, and write a brief reflection on their growth as a writer.

**I Do — What Makes a Portfolio Piece?**

A portfolio piece is the best version of your best work. Today I'll show you how to choose it and how to bring it to the highest standard it can reach.

> Example: What to look for when choosing: the piece that has the most interesting ideas. The piece where you took the most risks. The piece that is closest to being great — not already perfect, but closest to having genuine power.

The best portfolio piece is often not the longest or the most technically polished — it's the one with the strongest ideas and the most personal voice. Apply the five-pass edit to bring the execution up to the level of the ideas.

*Tip: Edit the ideas first (structural pass), then everything else. A beautifully proofread piece with weak ideas is still a weak piece.*

**We Do — Select and Plan the Edit**

As a class, share: which piece are you choosing for your portfolio and why? What does the structural pass show needs fixing first?
- Share: title of your chosen piece, reason for choosing it, and one structural improvement you know it needs.

*Discussion prompt: Is the reason for choosing it about ideas and voice, not just length or neatness? What is the most important fix?*

**You Do — Final Five-Pass Edit and Reflection**

Apply the five-pass edit to your chosen portfolio piece. Then write a 100-150 word reflection.
- Five-pass edit: structural, paragraph, sentence, word, proofread.
- Reflection: What was your writing like at the beginning of the year? What can you do now that you couldn't do then? What technique made the biggest difference? What do you want to get better at in Year 7?
- ✦ This is your final writing task for the year. Make it the best version of your best work.

**Learning Objectives (AOs):**
- *Writing — Writing Process (Practice)* — Portfolio-quality editing: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Practice): Students select, revise, and edit a piece to portfolio standard, applying the year's full editing sequence and reflecting metacognitively on their growth. This is the capstone task of the Year 5–6 writing programme.
- *Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge)* — Metacognitive reflection on writing growth: NZC Phase 2 Writing — Writing Process (Knowledge): Students reflect on their development as writers, identifying which strategies have most improved their writing and setting goals for future development. This final lesson closes the year's writing programme by consolidating not just skills but self-awareness — the foundation for continued growth as a writer into Year 7 and beyond.
